L243: ## 5.2.7. Blinded AEs for AGC vs. Lilly
L244: This reviewer evaluated blinded AE reports by product (AGC vs. Lilly). In total, the adverse event analysis includes data from 223 patients who either received Lilly teplizumab or placebo and 104 patients who either received AGC teplizumab or placebo. No meaningful differences were apparent.
L245: ## 5.2.8. Cytokine Release Syndrome
L246: CRS is considered an important safety signal in the teplizumab development program. Among the original BLA safety population, CRS events were identified in up to 5% of teplizumab-treated patients using the Applicant's analysis based on Common Terminology Criteria for
L247: Adverse Events (CTCAE) version 3.0 criteria, and there were 7 patients (0.9% of the teplizumab treatment group) who required hospitalization for CRS.
L248: For the safety update population, 3/327 (0.9%) subjects in the PROTECT study had SAEs of CRS which all occurred within the first three days of study drug administration requiring hospitalization. If it is assumed that all cases occurred in teplizumab-exposed patients, the incidence rate is 1.5%. Importantly, the reviewed CRS cases resemble the previously reported CRS cases in the original BLA with respect to severity, onset, and symptoms. In all three cases, the study treatment was discontinued, and each subject was discharged from the hospital within 48 hours of admission. One of the subjects, a 9 year-old male, required intravenous fluid and a single dose of epinephrine for hypotension.
L249: The sponsor evaluated the data for possible differences in adverse event frequency between the AGC and Lilly products. 16/223 (7.2%) of the subjects treated with either Lilly or placebo reported AE of CRS, compared to 1/104 (1%) of the AGC or placebo-treated subjects.
L250: None of subjects (N=4) in the TN-10 Extension study experienced SAEs of CRS, with one reported TEAE of CRS. The non-interventional Protégé Extension was not considered relevant for the analysis of CRS.
L251: *Reviewer comment: The safety update information does not change the original safety conclusions with respect to CRS.*
L252: ## 5.2.9. Hypersensitivity/ Rash
L253: In the safety update, AEs with the PT of 'hypersensitivity' were reported in 1/327 (0.3%) of PROTECT subjects and no patients in the TN-10 Extension study. The sponsor performed an additional analysis at the request of the Agency (received 6/14/2022, SDN 63) which revealed 2/327 (0.6%) of PROTECT subjects reporting the PT of 'infusion related reaction'. These 3 AEs were mild to moderate in severity, considered by the investigators to be related to study drug and recovered despite continued treatment with study drug. There were no reported adverse events with the following PTs: 'anaphylaxis', 'drug hypersensitivity', 'immune reaction' or 'serum sickness' reported in either the PROTECT or TN-10 Extension studies. The noninterventional Protégé Extension study was not considered relevant for this analysis due to the inclusion of only safety data at least 2 years after teplizumab dosing.
L254: There were no SAEs of rash in the safety update. Rash was noted as one of the common AEs for the PROTECT study 81/327 (24.8%), consistent with previous observations in the clinical program. One rash was reported in the TN-10 extension study, 1/4 (25%). The
L255: noninterventional Protégé Extension study was not considered relevant for this analysis due to the inclusion of only safety data at least 2 years after teplizumab dosing.
L256: *Reviewer comment: The safety update information does not change the original safety conclusions with respect to hypersensitivity/rash.*
L257: ## 5.2.10. Lymphopenia
L258: Lymphocyte abnormalities and white blood cell (WBC) abnormalities occurred in up to 22% of teplizumab-treated subjects in the PROTECT study, consistent with the original BLA safety data where a consistent association between lymphopenia was observed though placebo-controlled phase 3 data.
L259: ## 5.2.11. Evaluation of Potential Drug Induced Liver Injury
L260: No laboratory analyses were included for the studies included in the safety update; however, no cases of potential drug induced liver injury were reported.
L261: ## 5.2.12. Infections
L262: ## 5.2.12.1 General Infections
L263: In the safety update population, there were 2 infection SAEs, both in the PROTECT study. The first SAE was reported to be "IV-related bacteremia" and "cellulitis" starting after the 3rd infusion of study drug treatment. During the hospitalization, this subject was found to have staphylococcal bacteremia and started on oral antibiotics with resolution of symptoms and the AE following treatment. A temporal relationship with lymphopenia was observed in this subject, and although the lymphopenia in this case was mild, it is plausible that temporary immunosuppression from teplizumab may have contributed to the development of cellulitis and bacteremia despite the baseline risk of this AE from an indwelling catheter. It should be noted that this subject has not yet been unblinded so the treatment is currently unknown, and no safety conclusions can be made based on this case.
L264: Another subject (blinded to treatment assignment) had experienced a viral infection requiring hospitalization during the PROTECT study. This subject, a 15 year-old female, initially presented with a SAE of palpitation to the emergency room and was found to have abnormally elevated troponin 125 ng/L (normal range &lt;16.47 ng/L) and was diagnosed with myocarditis and mild pericardial effusion on thoracic echocardiography, requiring hospitalization. This SAE occurred from study days 76-82, approximately 2 months after completion of the first course of either teplizumab or placebo. Following outpatient cardiology consultation after hospital discharge it
L265: was determined that the myocarditis and pericarditis were due to Coxsackie B4 virus infection based upon the viral serology of 1:640. This subject also had experienced recent infectious mononucleosis reactivation from study day 29-43 with complete resolution of EBV viral titers by study day 137 when EBV viral titers were &lt;200 copies/mL. It is possible that temporary immunosuppression contributed to this patient's development of viral myocarditis, although the case report did not contain enough information to conclude that the subject was experiencing immunosuppression (lymphopenia or neutropenia) at the time of this infection event. On evaluation of these two events in a currently blinded study there are barriers and limitations in the assessment of causality. However, even in the setting of a blinded study, the possibility for infection with teplizumab use remains biologically plausible and continues to be recommended as a Warning &amp; Precaution in labeling.
L266: *Reviewer comment: As noted in the original BLA review, infection secondary to immunosuppression is an important safety issues of interest for teplizumab. While the safety update does not contribute additional information beyond two cases of infection in PROTECT study patients currently blinded to treatment arm, the individual case narratives continue to reflect that infection remains a potential issue with teplizumab use. Therefore, it remains prudent to continue to recommend a Warning and Precaution for a potential risk of serious infection as a risk mitigation measure.*
L267: ## 5.2.12.2 Epstein-Barr Virus (EBV) Infection
L268: In the safety update, the Sponsor repeated an analysis for “acute mononucleosis-like illness” using the PROTECT study and the most common events reported by this analysis were “lymphadenopathy” reported in 8/327 (2.4%) of the subjects, who remain blinded to treatment assignment. According to the sponsor's analysis, none of the cases were associated with clinical symptoms or a diagnosis of mononucleosis or mononucleosis-like syndrome, or positive EBV or cytomegalovirus (CMV) viral titer elevation on review of the case reports. There was 1 subject (0.3%) each reporting Epstein-barr viraemia and Epstein-barr virus infection and 5 subjects with “EBV test positive” listed as an adverse event in PROTECT (1.5%, 5/327). There was little additional information from the safety update regarding this signal, but the sponsor submitted detailed reports representing the current (blinded) safety data they have collected on each subject (including EBV and CMV quantitative PCR (copies/mL) collected at baseline, day 28, 84, 210, 273, 364 and 546 and serologies at baseline). Therefore, the safety update included reassuring safety data that the sponsor will continue to collect adequate safety data related to EBV/CMV infections for the PROTECT study to further understand this risk upon future analysis of the unblinded data.
L269: ## 5.2.13. Diabetic Ketoacidosis
L270: For the safety update, there were no DKA events reported in the PROTECT study (which remains blinded) or the TN-10 extension study.
L271: For the Protégé Extension study, 1.7% (3/181) teplizumab-treated subjects experienced DKA SAEs. By comparison, DKA SAEs occurred in 1.9%, of the teplizumab-treated original BLA safety population. All DKA events were reported as SAEs, and no patients treated with placebo reported DKA events. One subject reporting two events of DKA in the Protégé Extension population also experienced two DKA events during the original Protégé study (reported as a part of the original BLA safety analyses). There are some limitations to interpreting adverse events reported during the Protégé Extension study as there was a difference in safety exposure information between the study arms in the Protégé Extension study, which may lead to a 5-fold higher rate of adverse events among the teplizumab arm, which had higher enrollment and follow-up time than the placebo arm. Overall, similar rates of DKA were observed among the Protégé Extension study as the original BLA safety population with stage 3 T1D.
L272: *Reviewer comment: While a higher proportion of teplizumab-treated patients (1.7%) versus control patients (0%) experienced DKA events in the long-term follow up period of the Protégé Extension study, the rate of DKA is similar to the rates observed in the teplizumab-treated patients in the original BLA safety analyses. Additionally, there are limitations to interpreting this data given the follow up period was greater for teplizumab-treated patients.*
L273: ## 5.3. Safety Analyses by Demographic Subgroups
L274: In the original application, our analyses demonstrated that there were no important safety differences significant treatment-emergent adverse events (cytokine release syndrome, infections, cytopenias, hepatic enzyme elevations) among subgroups by age, sex, and race. The safety update did not contain enough unblinded demographic and safety information to perform these analyses in the update population which contained data from two blinded, ongoing studies and one unblinded long-term follow-up study.
L275: ## 5.4. Additional Safety Explorations
L276: ## 5.4.1. Human Carcinogenicity or Tumor Development
L277: The number and types of cancers reported from the updated data were reviewed. There were no events from TN-10 Extension or Protégé Extension studies which met criteria for lymphoma
L278: or malignancy.
L279: For the PROTECT study, there were two reported events for the SOC "lymphoma or other malignancies", one for AE term "papilloma" with verbatim term "verrucae planae juveniles" for an 8 year old female starting 52 days after the first dose of study treatment (teplizumab or placebo), and the second "papilloma" AE term with verbatim term "warts right foot" for an 11 year old male which was noted 35 days after the first dose of study treatment (teplizumab or placebo).
L280: As verrucae or warts are common in children, and the treatment group remains blinded, it is possible that these events are unrelated to study treatment dosing in both cases. Similar to the original BLA, we did not detect a safety signal related to cancer for the safety update.
L281: For details regarding the original BLA human carcinogenicity assessment please refer to the original review (July 2, 2021). It is important to note that the clinical safety data submitted in the original BLA was not sufficient to evaluate the potential risk of malignancy or lymphoproliferative disease because of the short duration of follow-up as well as the relatively young age of the safety population. The longest trial duration available in publication is the 7-year follow-up of the AbATE trial (n=43), and this trial reported no malignancy among the 43 patients treated with two courses of teplizumab (given 1 year apart). Following this safety update, there were no notable signals for malignancy, but uncertainty remains regarding rare or long-latency risks which could be addressed through the creation of a postmarketing patient registry for approximately 10 years in order to assess further the risk of malignancy.
L282: ## 5.4.2. Human Reproduction and Pregnancy
L283: Since the original submission, one pregnancy was reported during PROTECT. This participant discontinued course 1 treatment and the treatment group remains blinded at the time of this safety update. Per the Data Monitoring Committee (DMC) open minutes, the pregnancy resulted in a healthy baby.
L284: ## 5.5. Safety in the Postmarket Setting
L285: ## 5.5.1. Expectations on Safety in the Postmarket Setting
L286: The teplizumab clinical trials in the original BLA submission had broadly similar discontinuation criteria that provided a standardized approach to discontinuing drug treatment, with provisions to stop treatment for laboratory abnormalities including bilirubin and aminotransferase
L287: elevations, or abnormal platelet, neutrophil, hemoglobin, or coagulation parameters, and the occurrence of certain adverse events. If this approach to treatment discontinuation is applied in the postmarket setting, it is estimated that approximately 10% of patients will not be able to complete the full course of teplizumab therapy. This rate of treatment discontinuation is expected in the post-market setting if similar drug discontinuation criteria are put in place in labeling.
L288: Less stringent laboratory and adverse event-based discontinuation criteria were used for the PROTECT study, with an observed lower rate of discontinuations related to adverse events, at 3.7%. While the PROTECT study remains blinded, if these discontinuations are attributed to patients only treated with teplizumab in worst-case scenario analyses, the discontinuation rate would be 5.5%, approximately half the discontinuations observed in the original safety database.
L289: On review of the individual narratives for each laboratory adverse-event related discontinuation, including discontinuations related to aminotransferase elevations, neutropenia and lymphopenia, each adverse event resolved following either the discontinuation of product, withholding dosing for 1-2 days, and in the majority of cases of neutropenia and lymphopenia, despite continued treatment with study drug. Notably, there were no serious adverse events related to continued treatment among patients who would have met premature treatment discontinuation criteria. This reviewer therefore concludes that more serious or permanent harms are not expected if less restrictive discontinuation criteria (from the PROTECT study) are reflected in teplizumab's approved labeling. Additionally, the PROTECT study provided reassuring support that laboratory related adverse events were reversible, even after application of less stringent discontinuation criteria, and therefore the PROTECT study discontinuation criteria adequately mitigate risk of severe adverse events and can be safely adopted in the postmarketing period. A discussion on the discontinuation criteria and findings from this analysis are discussed extensively in section 5.2.5, Laboratory Findings.
L290: Otherwise, there are no other modifications to the expectations of safety in the postmarket setting based on the safety data included in this resubmission. Please refer to the original review, dated 7/2/2022 for details regarding safety expectations and section 6 for proposed labeling and section 8 for Postmarketing Requirements and Commitments.
L291: ## 5.6. Integrated Assessment of Safety
L292: The integrated assessment of safety is not meaningfully changed with the safety update.
L293: ## 6. Labeling Recommendations
L294: ## 6.1. Prescription Drug Labeling
L295: Labeling recommendations are contained within this review as appropriate.
L296: The labeling issues discussed during this submission included the following:
L297: - Update to proposed language for the Established Pharmacologic Class
- Update to proposed language for the indication statement
- Updated with revised dosing regimen based on recommendations of the clinical pharmacology review team
- Inclusion of laboratory safety data for the PROTECT study in section 6.1, Clinical Trial Experience, Laboratory Abnormalities
- Additional information regarding serious infection signal observed in the clinical trial populations in warnings and precautions
- Inclusion of additional information related to immunogenicity in Section 12.6, and
- Revision of information in Clinical Trials limiting Table 1 to adverse event data from the only study in the indicated population (TN-10) with descriptive trial data for studies conducted in the unapproved population (stage 3 type 1 diabetes) where appropriate.
L298: ## 7. Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS)
L299: No REMS was recommended for the original BLA and this recommendation continues upon this resubmission based on no new significant safety signals noted in this resubmission.
L300: During the original BLA primary review, the Division of Risk Management (DRM) and DDLO discussed potential FDA-driven communications close to the time of approval to highlight the novel treatment for patients with stage 2 T1D and to convey safety information regarding cytokine release syndrome (CRS). Given the extension to the PDUFA timeline for this application, this FDA-driven communication will be composed after the completion of this primary review.
L301: ## 8. Postmarketing Requirements and Commitments
L302: The Pediatric Research Equity Act (PREA) requirement is discussed in the original BLA review, dated July 2, 2021. This section discusses potential (Food and Drug Administration Amendments Act of 2007) FDAAA-related postmarketing requirements (PMRs) and postmarketing commitments (PMCs). The final decision about PMRs/PMCs is deferred to the CDTL memo.
L303: During the original review we identified the following safety issues, for which we considered a PMR safety study: CRS, infection, and longer latency events like LPD or other malignancy. The Applicant was notified of these potential PMR issues during the mid-cycle communication meeting for the original submission.
L304: At the time of the initial review, we determined that malignancy is a safety issue that cannot reasonably be resolved in a premarket development program for teplizumab, and therefore considered a 10-year registry study with long-term follow-up for documentation and evaluation of any events of malignancy as a conservative approach to address any uncertainty about the potential safety issue.
L305: Regarding the safety risks of cytokine release syndrome (CRS), and lymphoproliferative disease, we are not currently recommending any postmarketing requirements (PMRs) or postmarketing commitments (PMCs) for further characterization of these events; these events should be reported in routine Periodic Safety Update Reports.
L306: Following advice from DPMH, we initially recommended a pregnancy study for women and their offspring who are exposed to teplizumab during pregnancy in order to assess risk of pregnancy and maternal complications, adverse effects on the developing fetus and neonate, and adverse effects on the infant. However, it is anticipated that pregnancy with use of teplizumab (a 14-day course) would be exceedingly rare as the treatment is transient. Given the recommended screening for pregnancy prior to treatment and that the population of intended use consisting primarily of youth and adolescents being treated for a very limited time period (14 days) we anticipate that there will be minimal numbers of pregnancies and that any study performed in this way would be of limited value.

L338: ## 1.1. Product Introduction
L339: Teplizumab-mzwv (called teplizumab in this review; proposed proprietary name TzielD) is a first-in-class humanized anti-CD3 monoclonal antibody proposed to delay the onset of type 1 diabetes mellitus (T1D) in high-risk individuals.
L340: The Applicant has proposed a 14-day intravenous (IV) dosing regimen, involving a 4-day ramp up of the following doses (51 µg/m2 on Day 1, 103 µg/m2 on Day 2, 207 µg/m2 on Day 3, and 413 µg/m2 on Day 4) followed by repeated doses of 826 µg/m2 on Days 5 to 14. The proposed dose form for teplizumab is a 2 mg/ 2 mL solution in a vial. Teplizumab is a new biologic product and has not been previously marketed.
L341: If approved, teplizumab would be the first treatment for patients at risk of developing T1D.(b)(4)
L342: ## 1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L343: The Applicant has provided substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE) for the delay of T1D in at-risk1 individuals to support approval. The SEE consisted of one adequate and well-controlled (AWC) trial plus confirmatory evidence (CE).
L344: The single AWC trial, TN-10, was a randomized placebo-controlled trial that investigated the effect of teplizumab to prevent the onset of T1D (also called 'stage 3' T1D). TN-10 successfully demonstrated the treatment effect of teplizumab in delaying T1D diagnosis in at-risk patients. The results were robust to sensitivity analyses under a range of conservative assumptions for missing data and baseline imbalances in patient demographics and disease characteristics. The estimated magnitude of the treatment effect was clinically meaningful.
L345: The Food and Drug Administration (FDA) has issued guidance documents on demonstrating SEE: the 1998 Guidance "Providing Clinical Evidence of Effectiveness for Human Drug and Biological Products" and the 2019 Draft Guidance for Industry "Demonstrating Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness for Human Drug and Biologic Products." One of the scenarios described for meeting the substantial evidence standard is one adequate and well-controlled (AWC) trial plus CE, as noted in section 115(a) of the Food and Drug Administration Modernization Act (FDAMA), which amended the 21 U.S.C. § 355(d), to state: "If [FDA] determines, based on relevant science, that data from one adequate and well-controlled clinical investigation and confirmatory evidence (obtained prior to or after such investigation) are sufficient to establish effectiveness, [FDA] may consider such data and evidence to constitute substantial evidence." As CE of effectiveness, the Applicant provided results of a meta-analysis of C-peptide changes from baseline from five previously conducted studies of teplizumab in patients with new- or recent-onset T1D. FDA's independent analyses confirmed that teplizumab demonstrated superiority to control at 1 year of follow-up with respect to C-peptide preservation. In the meta-analysis, teplizumab's statistically significant impact on the rate of decline of C-peptide at a later stage of T1D is considered evidence of a similar treatment effect on C-peptide decline and delay in T1D diagnosis at an earlier stage of disease, in at-risk patients.
L346: C-peptide is a 31-amino-acid polypeptide that connects insulin's A-chain to its B-chain in the proinsulin molecule, and after processing, equimolar amounts of C-peptide and insulin are released into circulation. Therefore, measurement of C-peptide is a method to establish the endogenous insulin secretion of a patient; in a patient with T1D, a lower C-peptide measurement denotes greater dependence on exogenous insulin. C-peptide is typically measured through the oral glucose tolerance test (OGTT) prior to a diagnosis of T1D, and through a mixed-meal tolerance test (MMTT) after clinical T1D diagnosis. Teplizumab is considered a 'disease modifying' therapy. In contrast to insulin, teplizumab targets the underlying autoimmune disease process. Therefore, teplizumab is expected to either prevent
L347: 1 Discussion of the term 'at-risk' is included in Section 7 of this review.
L348: further beta-cell loss or restore beta-cell function which would be manifest by either stabilization or improvement in C-peptide concentrations.
L349: The relevance of the change in C-peptide in new-onset T1D as CE with respect to the proposed indication for teplizumab (delay of T1D) is supported by the continuous nature of the T1D disease process, i.e., stage 2 T1D is a phase in the continuum of this irreversible and progressive autoimmune disease. Natural history studies following children and adults from stage 2 T1D to stage 3 T1D have shown that an impaired C-peptide response to an OGTT may arise more than 5 years before the diagnosis of T1D (Evans-Molina et al. 2018). Once a critical mass of insulin-producing beta cells is destroyed, T1D (i.e., stage 3) begins with subsequent need for exogenous insulin replacement. The progressive nature of the disease is well-accepted with a predictable rate of beta-cell decline and ultimate diagnosis of T1D.
L350: This biologics license application (BLA) was brought to the Endocrine and Metabolic Advisory Committee (EMDAC) on May 27, 2021 to discuss the question of whether SEE had been met for the proposed indication. We carefully considered the committee's discussion, and we do not agree with the committee's view that the C-peptide meta-analysis does not constitute CE for this BLA. The lack of support for the use of C-peptide as CE appeared to be largely based on a statement in the FDA background document that C-peptide is not a validated surrogate endpoint for traditional regulatory approval. Although FDA's evidentiary standard for effectiveness has not changed since 1998, FDA's December 2019 draft guidance provides clarity that compelling mechanistic evidence, in the setting of well-understood disease pathophysiology, may be sufficient to provide SEE of a new drug or a new indication. In this case, the mechanistic evidence would "be obtained from clinical testing using a relevant and well understood pharmacodynamic endpoint not accepted by itself as an endpoint to establish evidence of effectiveness." In our view, the use of the C-peptide meta-analysis to support a conclusion of SEE conforms well to this standard.
L351: Importantly, the Division considers C-peptide to be a reasonably likely surrogate endpoint (RLSE) for clinical trials in new-onset T1D to demonstrate preservation of beta-cell function and to support a regulatory submission through the Accelerated Approval pathway, although it has not yet been used as the basis of approval for any application. C-peptide is not a validated surrogate endpoint because the relationship between C-peptide and clinical outcomes is understood through correlational studies (e.g., diabetes control and complications trial [DCCT]) as no intervention trials establish that therapeutics that increase C-peptide result in predictable and quantifiable clinical outcomes regardless of mechanism of action. We also note that C-peptide is consistently recommended as a primary outcome measure for new- or recent-onset T1D trials (Palmer et al. 2004). At a June 2021 workshop sponsored by the Critical Path Institute, "Design of Clinical Trials in New-Onset Type 1 Diabetes: Regulatory Considerations for Drug Development," stakeholders including FDA, the European Medicines Agency (EMA), the Juvenile Diabetes Research Foundation (JDRF), and several industry representatives, discussed
L352: the important role of C-peptide endpoints in clinical trials intended to support regulatory decision-making in the T1D therapeutic area.
L353: The remaining question is to consider whether this regulatory pathway, a single AWC trial with CE, is appropriate for establishment of SEE. When considering the feasibility of establishing SEE based on a single AWC trial plus CE, the 2019 draft guidance cites the persuasiveness of the trial, the robustness of the CE, the seriousness of the disease, the size of the patient population, and the presence of unmet need as factors for consideration. As will be discussed in this review, TN-10 is a persuasive trial, and the CE is robust. T1D is a serious disease, with no spontaneous remission. Although T1D is not a rare disease, the population of at-risk individuals who are available for enrollment in trials meant to delay T1D is very small. The low prevalence of stage 2 T1D makes it challenging to identify 'at-risk' patients for clinical studies, and studies of relatively long duration are needed to observe outcomes and quantify treatment benefits. Furthermore, there is an unmet need in this population, as there are no approved therapies to delay the onset of T1D. And given the lack of available therapies, an additional trial, if conducted, would necessarily compare teplizumab to a placebo. The community may lack the equipoise necessary to support another placebo-controlled trial; we have questions about the ethics of dosing children at risk of T1D with placebo. In summary, in light of the severity and rarity of the disease, the lack of treatments to delay its onset, and the impracticality of conducting a second long-term clinical trial, it is appropriate to consider the single AWC trial (TN-10) plus CE as meeting the standard for SEE.
L354: ## 1.3. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L355: ## Benefit-Risk Integrated Assessment
L356: Teplizumab is a first-in-class humanized anti-CD3 monoclonal antibody intended to delay the onset of type 1 diabetes mellitus (T1D) in high-risk individuals, who have two or more T1D-related autoantibodies and dysglycemia and consequently have a 75% risk of disease progression within approximately 4 to 5 years, and a lifetime risk of nearly 100%. Teplizumab's proposed indication is novel, and teplizumab would be the first disease-modifying therapy for T1D.
L357: T1D is a serious, life-long autoimmune disease that presents most commonly in childhood. The autoimmune process destroys insulin-producing beta cells, and over time, declining production of insulin results in dysregulation of glucose homeostasis and hyperglycemia with a requirement for exogenous insulin for survival. Despite exogenous insulin use, many patients are not able to fully control their hyperglycemia, especially without adverse sequelae such as life-threatening severe hypoglycemia. Additionally, constant vigilance is required with respect to glucose management, causing significant stress on patients and their families. Even with treatment, life-threatening complications can occur. Chronic hyperglycemia can lead to macrovascular (heart disease and stroke) and microvascular disease (kidney, eye, and dental disease; nerve damage). Kidney failure can occur, necessitating dialysis or a kidney-pancreas transplant, and diabetes is a leading cause of blindness and lower extremity amputation in the US. Evidence suggests the risk of these complications is highest in those who develop T1D in early childhood. Thus, there is a significant unmet need for therapies that delay or modify the disease pathogenesis of T1D.
L358: The benefit of teplizumab in delaying the onset of T1D was demonstrated in the TN-10 trial, a randomized, placebo-controlled, event driven trial in 76 patients at risk of T1D, with 44 given teplizumab and 32 given placebo intravenously in a single 14-day course. After approximately five years of follow-up, 18 patients in the teplizumab group (41%) and 22 in the placebo group (69%) had been diagnosed with T1D, meeting the primary endpoint. Teplizumab significantly delayed the onset of T1D by a median of 2 years. The observed delay is clinically meaningful (see Benefit Risk Framework), as it is expected to result in improved quality of life, and potentially lower cumulative risk of complications of chronic hyperglycemia, as well as acute risks of insulin therapy, e.g., hypoglycemia.
L359: Cytokine release syndrome (CRS) and serious infections were the most important identified risks and were not unexpected given teplizumab's action to engage T-cells and cause lymphopenia. Importantly, the majority of CRS cases identified in the teplizumab program were mild to moderate without requirement for intervention other than antipyretics. Serious infections were more frequent within 1 to 3 months of teplizumab dosing, but there were no permanent sequelae related to infection in the teplizumab-treated patients. A single case of serum sickness and several hypersensitivity reactions occurred; the majority of patients developed anti-drug antibodies (ADA) which may be, in part, contributory. As association of ADAs and rash was also noted; rash occurred at a 13-fold higher rate in the teplizumab group compared with the placebo group, again likely related to CRS or drug hypersensitivity. With the exception of two serious adverse events (SAEs) related to rash, however, the rashes were generally non-serious and did not require additional intervention.
L360: Laboratory abnormalities including cytopenias and transaminase elevations were predictable and self-limited. Approximately 10% of patients were unable to complete their treatment course with the majority of events related to protocol-driven laboratory drug discontinuation criteria. Laboratory abnormalities leading to drug discontinuation included transaminase elevations, hematologic parameters (lymphopenia, neutropenia, anemia, and thrombocytopenia) and cytokine release syndrome. It is important to note, however, that the safety profile must be interpreted in light of the conservative drug discontinuation criteria employed in the clinical protocols. If similar discontinuation criteria are used in the real-world setting, over 10% of patients will not be able to receive the full course of teplizumab, but a similar safety profile to that observed in the clinical trials can reasonably be assured.
L361: Overall, we did not identify irreparable harms, and conclude that the nature and level of severity of these risks are acceptable in light of the expected benefit, with the understanding that heretofore unrecognized risks may be observed post-marketing. Our belief is that the risks can be adequately managed with standard product labeling without need for a REMS.
L362: Uncertainties that remain include potential longer-term risks including lymphoproliferative disorders caused by Epstein-Barr virus reactivation or other malignancy due to immunosuppression. Although, these safety issues were taken under consideration within the context of the benefit risk assessment, these risks are only theoretical at this time as they are more likely to be associated with chronic immunosuppression. An exposure registry may help elucidate if there are any longer-term risks that may have an impact on the benefit risk assessment. Also, the safety and effectiveness in children under 8 years of age have not been
L363: established, and a PREA PMR will be issued to study teplizumab in this age group.
L364: Based on literature, the Advisory Committee meeting, and other expert opinion and patient testimonial, there is no question that a 2-year delay is highly meaningful, and it is likely that patients would accept the risks of teplizumab for even less of a delay than 2 years. For this reason, the uncertainty around the estimated treatment effect is not a concern with respect to determination of a favorable benefit-risk assessment. As noted above, the development of T1D is virtually inevitable once two autoantibodies and dysglycemia are present.
L365: Although the patient population selected for TN-10 was limited to relatives of patients with T1D, a large majority of individuals with T1D (85%) have no family history of disease. Furthermore, the risk of progression to T1D for stage 2 T1D individuals who are not relatives appears to be similar. As was generally supported at the advisory committee meeting, expansion of the treatment indication to include non-relatives is recommended, while keeping the intended population otherwise narrow, to the population that was studied in TN-10 (age \ge 8 years with presence of stage 2 T1D).
L366: In summary, the lack of available disease-modifying therapies, significant unmet medical need, severity of T1D, and the clinical meaningfulness of a median 2-year delay of onset of T1D on a patient's quality of life outweigh the observed and potential risks of teplizumab which were self-limited and expected based on the drug's mechanism of action.
L367: ## Benefit-Risk Dimensions
L368: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of Condition	Type 1 diabetes mellitus (T1D) is a serious, life-long autoimmune disease that presents most commonly in childhood. It has been estimated that T1D affects 4.5% of the world's population under the age of 14 years (Karvonen et al. 2000).
Over time, declining production of insulin from autoimmune beta cell injury results in dysregulation of glucose homeostasis and hyperglycemia. The long-term effect of prolonged hyperglycemia is vascular damage, resulting in a high rate of cardiovascular and renal morbidity and mortality, highest in those with longer duration of disease.
Type 1 diabetes mellitus (T1D) is a serious chronic autoimmune disease that results in progressive beta-cell loss and life-long insulin dependence. Inadequate glycemic control is associated with increased risks of micro- and macrovascular disease.
The burden of insulin therapy, as well as its acute risks (DKA, severe hypoglycemia)
L369: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
There is evidence of increased mortality and higher cardiovascular morbidity in patients with younger age of T1D diagnosis, before 10 years of age (Rawshani et al. 2017).
The natural history of T1D development has been defined by longitudinal studies in at-risk populations. Relatives of patients with T1D who have two or more islet autoantibodies have close to a 100% lifetime risk of developing type 1 diabetes, termed "stage 1 T1D". Once a patient reaches stage 1 T1D, 40% will progress to dysglycemia within 2 years, and once patients develop dysglycemia on oral glucose tolerance testing, e.g., "stage 2 T1D", the 2-year risk of disease is 60% and 4- to 5-year risk of T1D is 75%.
Longitudinal natural history studies have demonstrated that impaired beta cell function (as measured by C-peptide, a biomarker of endogenous insulin production) may arise in patients at-risk of T1D more than 5 years before diagnosis. Once a critical mass of insulin-producing beta cells is destroyed, symptomatic T1D (i.e., stage 3) begins with subsequent need for exogenous insulin replacement.
Once T1D is diagnosed, maintenance of C-peptide levels has been correlated with important clinical benefits, and treatments that arrest this decline in insulin production, as measured by C-peptide, even after the new-onset period, may have clinical benefit.
Although it is relatively well understood that the progression to T1D in at-risk individuals is predictable, a large majority of individuals with T1D (85%) have no family history and are not identified at earlier stages of T1D prior to need for insulin dependence. Therefore, the pool of patients who are currently identified as at-risk for T1D is extremely small.
contributes to poorer quality of life. Based on natural history studies, the progression to T1D is predictable, and steady loss of beta cell function (as measured by C-peptide) is observed prior to the onset of disease.
It is possible to identify patients at high risk of developing T1D based on the presence of diabetes-related autoantibodies and dysglycemia. In these patients there is a high unmet need to modify the disease process and delay the onset of T1D requiring insulin therapy.
L370: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Current Treatment Options	There are no current treatment options for the delay of T1D in at-risk individuals.
Although there are therapies to improve glycemic control in patients with T1D, there are no currently approved disease-modifying therapies.
There are no treatment options to delay T1D available at this time.
Benefit	In TN-10, a randomized placebo-control trial designed to evaluate teplizumab's efficacy in the delay of T1D onset in at-risk children and adults aged \ge 8 years, teplizumab successfully delayed T1D diagnosis in at-risk relatives of T1D patients for a median time of approximately 2 years. Among 76 patients enrolled, 44 were given teplizumab and 32, placebo. After approximately five years of follow-up, 18 patients in the teplizumab group (41%) and 22 in the placebo group (69%) had been diagnosed with T1D (met the primary endpoint).
The median time to T1D diagnosis was 49.5 months in the teplizumab group vs. 24.9 months in the placebo group. In the primary analysis of time to T1D diagnosis, the hazard ratio was 0.41 (95% confidence interval (CI): 0.22 to 0.78, p=0.0066). The treatment effect was statistically significant and robust to sensitivity analyses under a range of conservative assumptions for missing data.
The magnitude of benefit patients could expect from treatment is best approximated by the median time to diagnosis of 49.5 months in the teplizumab-treated group, where the lower limit of the 95 % CI was 32.1 months, whereas median time to diagnosis in the placebo patients was 24.9 months (95% CI: 9.5, 48.6 months).
In addition, a delay in the onset of T1D can avoid the significant disease burden associated with T1D on both the patient and caregivers, and result in an improved quality of life as later age of onset of diabetes has been associated with improved health-related quality of life, glycemic control, given that HbA1c values are higher in patients with younger age
Teplizumab significantly delayed the onset of T1D by a median of 2 years. The observed delay is clinically meaningful, as it is expected to result in improved quality of life, and potentially lower cumulative risk of complications of chronic hyperglycemia, as well as acute risks of insulin therapy, e.g., hypoglycemia.
The magnitude of delay of T1D that is considered clinically meaningful may vary among at-risk individuals, e.g., delay of T1D in a young child may be more meaningful than in an adult.
Based on literature, the Open Public Hearing portion of the Endocrine and Metabolic Advisory Committee meeting, and expert opinion there is no question that a 2-year delay is highly meaningful.
Should general population screening become more widespread, the availability of a therapeutic meant to delay the onset of this chronic disease would potentially benefit

Younger age of onset of T1D (<7 years) is also associated with worse cognitive function as compared to older age of onset of T1D (>7 to 17 years) (Ferguson et al. 2005). Finally, a diagnosis of T1D earlier in life is associated with a loss of life expectancy, with a loss of 17.7 years for women and 14.2 years for men (Kalyva et al. 2011). In addition, a delay in the onset of T1D avoids the significant disease burden associated with T1D on both the patient and caregivers, and the strain of having a chronic illness, resulting in an improved quality of life (Ferguson et al. 2005; Kalyva et al. 2011; Rawshani et al. 2018).
A delay in the onset of T1D, with the maintenance of endogenous insulin production, allows patients to avoid diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA), which is associated with worse cognitive function in newly diagnosed pediatric T1D patients, as compared to age-matched T1D without DKA (Jessup et al. 2015).
A delay in the onset of T1D allows the avoidance of insulin therapy and the accompanying risks of insulin therapy, such as iatrogenic hypoglycemia.

Risk and Risk Management	The safety of teplizumab was evaluated in 791 pediatric and adult patients, 44 at-risk patients (study TN-10) and 747 patients with new- or recent-onset T1D.
The most important safety concerns were related to immunosuppression and cytokine release. Lymphopenia was common, reported in ≥75% of teplizumab-treated patients during the 14-day dosing period, but resolved within 2-4 weeks of dosing, and in the majority of patients, while teplizumab dosing continued. Cytokine release syndrome was also an important safety concern and was identified in up to 5% of teplizumab-treated patients. This event occurred primarily in older
Overall, the safety profile has been reasonably well characterized in the relevant population of at-risk patients and a similar population with new- or recent-onset T1D.
The safety issues were not unexpected given the drug's mechanism of action.
The safety profile for teplizumab appears qualitatively similar to, but more benign than, that of other T-cell stimulating monoclonal

This safety concern was expected given teplizumab's mechanism. CRS rates differed by grading method, and FDA pursued another grading system, which detected a high rate of CRS among placebo/control subjects, without any change in risk difference between the groups.
Rash was a common adverse event (AE) among teplizumab-treated patients (45%) but was self-limited and all cases resolved without sequelae. Drug hypersensitivity was expected given the behavior of other monoclonal antibodies; however, a strong signal was not detected. Other than a single patient with serum sickness, other possible cases of drug hypersensitivity were few. Continued monitoring for events in the post-market setting would help to improve our understanding of the true risk.
Transaminase elevations were also frequently reported AEs and were observed in ~25% of teplizumab-treated patients. Similar to lymphopenia, transaminase elevations are also attributed to cytokine release and were transient with documented recovery, the majority recovering within 2 weeks after treatment. Transaminase elevations leading to drug discontinuation occurred in 5.6% of teplizumab patients and 0.9% of control patients. Half of drug discontinuations in the clinical studies were related to transaminase elevations. Analyses of transaminase elevations, including outlier analyses, did not reveal a signal for drug-induced liver injury.

antibodies. Safety issues include cytokine release, infection, immunosuppression, lymphopenia, and rash, although many of these adverse events were self-limited, mild, or transient (limited to the 14-day dosing period).
There are some uncertainties related to infection, and drug hypersensitivity risks, although the observed risks of these events appear to be low. Assessments of such events in the postmarket setting would aid in further characterization of teplizumab's safety. In addition, there are some theoretical concerns related to the higher frequency of Epstein-Barr virus (EBV) reactivations observed during the teplizumab clinical studies, and the associated risk of lymphoproliferative disease (LPD) when EBV reactivations are observed with more intensive immunosuppression.
Because of endocrinologists' lack of experience with CRS, a communication plan REMS was considered, but not recommended by DRM; the risks of CRS were determined to not meet the severity typically needed for initiation of REMS. In the Oncology space, tools like REMS have been used to inform providers of the risks of severe cytokine release syndrome (e.g., with blinatumomab,

thrombocytopenia) and cytokine release syndrome. It is unknown if adhering to prespecified drug discontinuation criteria prevented more serious adverse events during the clinical trials; therefore, a conservative approach to mitigate risk will be to incorporate a modified version of the discontinuation criteria included in the integrated summary of safety (ISS) studies into teplizumab's labeling.
Other safety findings that were expected for an immunomodulatory biologic agent included increased risk of serious infections (including reactivation of EBV) and cytokine release syndrome. As EBV reactivation is a risk of teplizumab, there is a theoretical risk for later malignancy from lymphoproliferative disease (LPD). Nonclinical findings in chimpanzees also support a potential risk for LPD with teplizumab, as non-neoplastic B-cell lymphoproliferative disease associated with an EBV-like virus and accompanied T-cell immunosuppression was observed at high doses. In the clinical development program, no lymphoproliferative disorders were observed among the pooled safety population with T1D, and an increased risk of general malignancy was not identified, although the safety database was not sufficient in duration to observe long-latency events such as malignancy. Given the increased risk of EBV reactivation, uncertainty remains regarding the potential for lymphoproliferative disease.
In the postmarket setting, providers who are intended to use teplizumab may not have experience with identification and treatment of cytokine release syndrome.
There was a high rate of ADA development in TN-10 patients, observed in 59% of patients at 3-6 months post-randomization; however, there was little impact of ADA status on efficacy or safety. ADA formation was higher after a second treatment course which did affect pharmacokinetics. Very limited data indicate a potential for higher ADA

Further assessment of ADAs is only needed if the Applicant eventually pursues a change to the approved dosing regimen (to add a second treatment cycle) and/or in the context of establishing biocomparability of the commercial and clinical trial product.
Off-label use is unlikely because the product will need to be administered by a health care professional in an infusion center and face reimbursement issues. Nevertheless, off-label use is not considered high risk from a safety perspective. No mitigation measures to prevent off-label use are warranted.
Uncertainty regarding rare or long-latency risks could be addressed through the creation of a patient registry for approximately 10 years in order to assess further the risk of malignancy. In addition, the long-term safety is uncertain.

L378: ## 2. Therapeutic Context
L379: ## 2.1. Analysis of Condition
L380: Type 1 diabetes mellitus (T1D) is caused by autoimmune destruction of the pancreatic islet beta cells. Over time, declining production of insulin from the damaged beta cells results in dysregulation of glucose homeostasis and hyperglycemia. Although approximately 25% of patients are diagnosed with T1D as adults (Haller et al. 2005), children are disproportionately affected, and the peak ages of diagnosis are between 5 to 7 years of age and at or near puberty (Maahs et al. 2010).
L381: Autoimmune diseases like T1D are initiated by the loss of immune tolerance to self-antigens, usually maintained through immune system regulatory mechanisms, and it is well-established that T-cells are the primary effector of damage to the pancreatic beta cell in T1D. Although many aspects of the etiology of T1D are still unclear, both genetic and environmental influences appear to play a role. T1D is a polygenic disorder, but class 2 Human Leukocyte Antigen (HLA) genes, responsible for antigen presentation, account for approximately half of the overall genetic risk of T1D. The HLA haplotypes that confer high susceptibility to T1D are DR4—DQ8 (often abbreviated DR4) and DR3—DQ2 (abbreviated DR3). It is estimated that up to 90% of patients with T1D have either DR4 or DR3 haplotypes, and about 30% of patients have a combination of the two (Redondo et al. 2018). These haplotypes are prevalent in White populations (DiMeglio et al. 2018).
L382: The incidence of T1D varies widely depending on the population studied, with age-adjusted incidences ranging from as low as 0.1/100,000 per year in China and India to as high as &gt;60/100,000 per year in Finland (Atkinson et al. 2014). In the US, incidence has been estimated to be from 22 to 24/100,000 per year for youth. From 2001 to 2009, the prevalence of T1D in US youth increased by 21%, and the number of youths with T1D in the US is estimated to triple from 2010 to 2050 (Imperatore et al. 2012; Dabelea et al. 2014). It has been estimated that T1D affects 4.5% of the world's population under 14 years of age (Karvonen et al. 2000). The increase in T1D incidence noted worldwide has largely been attributed to environmental factors, as the trends in new diagnoses are not explained by genetic changes, but no specific environmental factor has been determined to have unequivocal influence on T1D (Atkinson et al. 2014).
L383: Although T1D is diagnosed in females and males in youth at similar rates, T1D is slightly more common in males when diagnosed in adulthood (Maahs et al. 2010). The group with the highest incidence of T1D in the US are non-Hispanic White youths, but with increases in incidence over the last two decades observed in non-Hispanic Black youths, with the largest incidence increases observed among Hispanic youths (Mayer-Davis et al. 2017). Additionally, the growing
L384: prevalence of T1D is thought to be driven by a striking increasing incidence of T1D in young children (age &lt;5 years), a trend that has been observed since the 1990s, and has been confirmed in several populations (Dabelea 2007; Vehik et al. 2007; Patterson et al. 2009).
L385: Insulin therapy, administered via multiple daily injections or insulin pump, is required for survival, but patients are at risk of acute complications related to insulin under- or over-dosing, including diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) and hypoglycemia. DKA is a substantial risk in children and adolescents and can be fatal. One in 14 US pediatric patients with T1D duration \ge 1 year are diagnosed with DKA requiring treatment in a health care facility, with the majority of US studies showing a rate of DKA in pediatric patients of 1 to 15% per established patient per year (Maahs et al. 2015). Severe hypoglycemic events occur at rates of 5 to 10% per year for pediatric patients with T1D, with highest rates in the youngest age group, &lt;6 years old (Cengiz et al. 2013). Adult patients with T1D are also affected by severe hypoglycemia at rates as high as ~19% per patient, per year for patients age 40 and older, with higher risk with longer duration of T1D (Weinstock et al. 2013).
L386: Children and adults with T1D have a complex and demanding daily treatment regimen, with the need to administer the correct dose of insulin at the right time. Continual attention to diabetes management pervades nearly all of the patient's daily activities causing strain on patients and families.
L387: Microvascular disease is one of the chronic complications of T1D. It is characterized by nephropathy, retinopathy, and neuropathy. Diabetes is a leading cause of kidney failure and blindness. Nephropathy affects patients with T1D more severely and disproportionately than type 2 diabetes (Zhao et al. 2009) and is implicated in approximately 60% of nontraumatic amputations in the US (Melendez-Ramirez et al. 2010). The presence of early microvascular complications is common, with one in three individuals with T1D showing early signs of complications or comorbidities after just 8 years of average disease duration, at an estimated age of 21 years (Dabelea et al. 2017).
L388: In addition to microvascular disease, patients with T1D have increased morbidity and mortality related to macrovascular disease with higher rates of coronary heart disease, cerebrovascular disease, and peripheral artery disease, with cardiovascular events occurring earlier and more frequently in patients with T1D than age-matched nondiabetic populations. There is also evidence that a younger age of diagnosis is associated with worsened cardiovascular outcomes, with a recent study demonstrating a 30 times higher rate of cardiovascular morbidity in patients diagnosed with T1D before the age of 10 years, compared to patients diagnosed between 26 to 30 years of age. The Pittsburgh Epidemiology of Diabetes Complications study reported that T1D patients younger than 40 years of age with disease onset before age 17, have cardiovascular events at a rate of 1% per year, and an annual mortality rate of 1.5% (Pambianco et al. 2006). Although improvements in insulin management and administration have led to
L389: significant reductions in long-term macro- and micro-vascular complications of the disease, cardiovascular morbidity and mortality remain a significant concern.
L390: Despite advances in diabetes management tools, e.g., continuous glucose monitoring, the mean hemoglobin A1c (HbA1c), a clinically relevant measure of glycemic control, has increased in children and adolescents with T1D (Foster et al. 2019). As a consequence of worsening glycemic control and increasing disease incidence among the youngest age groups, as well as the aging and growth of the population with the longest duration of disease, the overall adverse effect of diabetes on public health is increasing (Ingelfinger and Jarcho 2017).
L391: Based on screening programs, which typically focus on family members of patients with T1D, a greater understanding of the natural history of T1D has been achieved, and a model that classifies T1D into 3 stages has been proposed Figure 1. These programs screen for circulating autoantibodies to pancreatic islet cells; the most commonly tested autoantibodies are insulin antibody (IAA), glutamic acid decarboxylase antibody (GADA), protein tyrosine phosphatase-like antigen antibody (IA-2A or ICA-512), islet cell antibody (ICA), and zinc transporter 8 antibody (ZnT8A). In family members of patients with T1D followed longitudinally, a greater number of positive autoantibodies is associated with a more rapid progression from stage 1 (\ge 2 T1D autoantibodies) to stage 2 T1D (\ge 2 T1D autoantibodies + dysglycemia) (Verge et al. 1996). Data suggest that only a small proportion of children and adults who develop a single T1D-specific autoantibody progress to T1D disease. An individual with two or more autoantibodies is classified as having stage 1 disease. As beta cell mass progressively declines, abnormal glucose metabolism, or dysglycemia, develops. The period between development of dysglycemia, but not yet meeting diagnostic criteria for T1D, is classified as stage 2. The 6-month, 2-year, and 4-year risk of progression to T1D at this stage is approximately 25%, 60%, and 75%, and the lifetime risk approaches 100% (Krischer 2013). Once T1D is diagnosed, the patient is considered to have entered stage 3 T1D (Insel et al. 2015).
L392: Figure 1. Type 1 Diabetes Disease Model
L393: Source: Reviewer generated, adapted from article by (Insel et al. 2015).
L394: Worldwide screening initiatives in multiple populations have repeatedly demonstrated that Although relatives of patients with T1D have a higher risk (15X) compared to the general population, 85% of those newly diagnosed with T1D have no family history of disease (Mahon et al. 2009; Insel et al. 2015).
L395: Longitudinal natural history studies have demonstrated that impaired beta cell function (as measured by C-peptide) may arise more than 5 years before diagnosis (Evans-Molina et al. 2018). Figure 2 shows natural history data in children and adults from the Trial Net TN-01 cohort. Following an initial period of relatively stable gradual stimulated C-peptide decline, there is a rapid decline, beginning 6 months before diagnosis that continues at the same rapid rate for 12 months after diagnosis (Bogun et al. 2020). Once a critical mass of insulin-producing beta cells is destroyed, symptomatic T1D (i.e., stage 3) begins with subsequent need for exogenous insulin replacement.
L396: Figure 2. Rate of C-peptide Decline Before and After T1D in At-Risk TrialNet TN-01 Cohort Followed Longitudinally
L397: Source: Adapted from article by (Bogun et al. 2020).
Abbreviations: Dx, diagnosis; OGTT, oral glucose tolerance test; T1D, type 1 diabetes
L398: Immediately following diagnosis, the majority of patients with T1D retain the ability to produce endogenous insulin in amounts that reduce their daily exogenous insulin requirement to maintain glycemic control, a time termed the “honeymoon.” Experience with large cohorts of individuals with new-onset T1D suggest that for at least 2 to 4 years after the clinical onset of T1D, many patients still retain detectable levels of endogenous insulin production, as measured by C-peptide (Greenbaum et al. 2012; Hao et al. 2016). Therefore, a disease modifying therapy may be expected to show a favorable effect on C-peptide even after T1D is diagnosed (see also Section 1.2).
L399: Although T1D as a whole is not considered a rare disease, the population of individuals at-risk of disease who can be identified for enrollment in trials meant to delay or prevent T1D is very small; therefore, conducting trials in this population is challenging. Only a fraction of those followed in family screening programs will develop T1D, with the risk of first-degree relatives developing T1D estimated to be 4 to 7% by 30 years of age. The prevalence of stage 1 and 2 T1D was determined to be 0.48% in a US primary care pediatric cohort (McQueen et al. 2020), and 0.31%, in a similar population in Bavaria, Germany. Remarkably, the German study screened 90,632 children over a period of 4 years and identified 26 children, aged 2 to 5 years with stage 2 T1D, for a prevalence of 0.03%, and a combined prevalence of 0.31% for stage 1 and 2 T1D (Ziegler et al. 2020).
L400: ## 2.2. Analysis of Current Treatment Options
L401: Currently, there are no approved agents that are considered disease-modifying, or that affect the underlying pathophysiology of T1D; thus, there are no current treatment options for patients with 'stage 2' disease (two positive autoantibodies and dysglycemia). Treatment of hyperglycemia begins once T1D is diagnosed, and treatment consists of insulin therapy, typically in a basal bolus regimen or delivery of insulin via an insulin pump. An additional drug approved to improve glycemic control in patients with T1D is the amylin analog Symlin, although this product has not gained widespread use. Insulin is a necessary (life-saving) treatment for patients with T1D as severe insulin insufficiency results in ketoacidosis and death. Although insulin therapy is required, it carries significant risks, most importantly, severe hypoglycemia associated with overdose and use errors. In addition, insulin administration is highly burdensome, requiring constant attention to caloric intake, activity level, and symptoms of hypoglycemia, despite recent advances in insulin delivery systems (e.g., artificial pancreas devices), and many patients still do not achieve glycemic goals.
L402: Finally, pancreas transplant has been used to restore beta-cell function, and although islet-cell transplant is being investigated as a possible treatment for T1D, it has not gained widespread use.
L403: ## 3. Regulatory Background
L404: ## 3.1. U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
L405: Teplizumab is a new molecular entity (NME) not approved for marketing in the US or elsewhere. Teplizumab is also being developed under IND 100262 for the treatment of recent-onset T1D.
L406: ## 3.2. Summary of Presubmission/Submission Regulatory Activity
L407: The TN-10 study, the single trial in the intended population, was conducted under IND 102629 (delay or prevention of T1D in at-risk relatives of patients with T1D). As noted above, additional clinical studies evaluating teplizumab in the new or recent-onset T1D population were conducted under IND 100262 (treatment of recent-onset T1D).

L421: ## 4.1. Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI)
L422: *For details refer to the OSI review by Dr. Cynthia Kleppinger dated November 10, 2020.*
L423: A routine (i.e., not “for-cause”) OSI audit was requested for the TN-10 study because: (1) it provided the sole adequate and well-controlled evidence of efficacy, (2) the study was small, and (3) the study participants were concentrated in a few potentially influential study sites.
L424: Of the 30 total sites participating in TN-10, the Division of Diabetes, Lipid Disorders and Obesity (DDLO) requested inspections for the three domestic sites with the highest enrollment. The inspection also included remote inspections of the contract research organization (CRO) and the Applicant.
L425: OSI identified regulatory deficiencies in one clinical site (Barbara Davis Center, site 7) with 10 enrolled patients in TN-10. At the conclusion of the inspection, a Form FDA-483, Inspectional Observations, was issued for a failure to adhere to protocol whereby a peripherally inserted central catheter (PICC) line was placed prior to gaining proper approval. Per the OSI reviewer, the observed violations are not expected to have an impact on safety or efficacy analyses. There were no objectionable conditions noted in the other clinical sites.
L426: The COVID-19 global pandemic limited the Office of Regulatory Affairs' ability to conduct a foreign onsite good clinical practice (GCP) inspection for the Applicant sites located outside of the United States (Canada and Germany).
L427: Overall, based on the inspections of the three domestic clinical sites and the remote regulatory assessment of the contract research organization (CRO) and Applicant, the inspectional findings support validity of data as reported by the Applicant under this BLA.
L428: ## 4.2. Product Quality
L429: The Office of Pharmaceutical Quality (OPQ) recommended a Complete Response to this BLA. Dr. Jennifer Swisher was the lead OPQ reviewer; for details refer to her Office of Product Quality (OPQ) summary review in Document Archiving, Reporting, and Regulatory Tracking System (DARRTS) dated May 14, 2021. The pertinent review findings are summarized below.
L430: ## Overall Conclusion
L431: The data submitted in this application are not sufficient to support a conclusion that the manufacture of Tzield is well-controlled and will lead to a product that is pure and potent for the duration of the shelf-life. The Cation-Exchange High Performance Liquid Chromatography (CEX-HPLC) acceptance criteria are unacceptably wide, and variability of the CEX-HPLC data under long-term storage conditions for the DP preclude a determination of shelf life and introduce uncertainty regarding interpretation of the CEX-HPLC data to support comparability between proposed commercial and clinical materials (manufactured at AGC Biologics and Eli Lilly, respectively). Although it cannot be confirmed whether this variability arises from product instability or method variability, either cause would preclude a recommendation that teplizumab is approvable at this time. Therefore, from a chemistry, manufacturing, and controls (CMC) standpoint, OBP is recommending a Complete Response.
L432: Clinical Review
Lauren K. Wood Heickman, MD
BLA 761183
Tzield/Teplizumab (PRV-031)
L433: The list of deficiencies is included on page 2 of the OPQ summary memo and also can be found in the Complete Response Letter. There are four CMC deficiencies in total. In brief, these are related to:
L434: - Unacceptable charge variation measured in teplizumab drug substance (DS) and drug product (DP) under recommended storage conditions which precluded the ability to assign a shelf-life for either
- Lack of information regarding plans to monitor Master Cell Bank and Working Cell Bank stability
- Deficient protocol for requalification of the primary reference standard
- Insufficient information regarding the levels and types of leachates in teplizumab derived from its container closure and the risk to patients from any leachates that are potentially present in the drug product by the end of the expiry period
L435: Additional chemistry, manufacturing, and controls (CMC) issues that are not approvability issues are also listed in the OPQ summary review and CRL.
L436: Additionally, this application cannot be approved due to facility deficiencies. As of 5/14/2021, there is a potential official action indicated (pOAI) alert for [REDACTED] (b) (4) the DP manufacturer. During an ongoing inspection of the manufacturing facility, deficiencies were conveyed to the representatives of the facility by investigators from FDA. Satisfactory resolution of the observations is required before this BLA may be approved. OPQ is recommending a withhold status, which will be communicated in the Complete Response letter.
L437: ## Review Summary
L438: ## Environmental assessment
L439: The Applicant claimed a categorical exclusion from the requirement to prepare an environmental assessment, as this biologic product is comprised of substances that occur naturally in the environment. The OPQ review agreed this is acceptable.
L440: ## Immunogenicity
L441: Pharmacokinetics, anti-drug antibody (ADA) and neutralizing antibody (Nab) data are available only for pivotal trials: Protégé, Encore, and TN-10. Method 1A, Method 1B and Method 3 were developed to assess ADA for each individual clinical trial, respectively. No deficiencies related to the immunogenicity assays were reported in the OBP review.
L442: ## Drug product
L443: The proposed drug product is a 2 mL sterile solution pre-filled in a single-dose vial. The teplizumab concentration is 1 mg/mL, in phosphate [REDACTED] (b) (4) (pH 6.1, [REDACTED] (b) (4) NaPO4,
L444: (b) (4) NaCl, 0.05 mg/mL polysorbate 80). All excipients are compendial per USP. The teplizumab drug product formulation and filling process has been carried out at (b) (4) and very few changes have been made to the DP manufacturing process throughout development.
L445: ## Drug product compatibility
L446: Both (b) (4) are compatible with teplizumab. In addition, the material made from (b) (4) should not be used for drug administration.
L447: ## Drug product facilities
L448: The DP is manufactured at (b) (4). During an ongoing inspection of this manufacturing facility, deficiencies were conveyed to the representatives of the facility by investigators from FDA. Satisfactory resolution of the observations is required before this BLA may be approved.
L449: ## Drug substance
L450: Teplizumab-mzww is a humanized IgG1kappa monoclonal antibody produced in CHO cells and consists of two heavy chains that are each composed of 449 amino acids and two light chains that are each composed of 213 amino acids. Each heavy chain contains an N-linked glycan site at asparagine 299 (Asn299). It is a humanized version of the mouse monoclonal antibody OKT3 with additional leucine to alanine mutations at positions HC 234 and 235. The Ala-Ala ("LALA") mutations reduce binding to complement and Fc\gammaR that can result in T cell activation and cytokine release syndrome. The molecular weight of (b) (4). Teplizumab is an agonist of cluster of differentiation 3 (CD3) on T lymphocytes and engages signaling through CD3\epsilon and (nuclear factor of activated T cells) NFAT-dependent signaling cascades downstream.
L451: The teplizumab drug substance was originally developed by MacroGenics. Eli Lilly partnered with MacroGenics in December 2007 with the intent to manufacture teplizumab DS at the Lilly (b) (4) site. The teplizumab DS used in the clinical studies to support this BLA was manufactured either at the MacroGenics facility in (b) (4) or, later, the Eli Lilly facility in (b) (4). Prevention Bio acquired teplizumab from MacroGenics in 2018 and transferred the DS manufacturing technology to AGC Biologics (AGC) where additional improvements were made, and the process was scaled up to a (b) (4). The AGC product is the intended commercial product.
L452: During review of stability testing data, the OPQ team identified unacceptable variability in a control parameter, charge heterogeneity, via CEX-HPLC for the to-be-marketed formulation. CEX-HPLC is used to detect the relative presence of positively and negatively charged amino acids and negatively charged glycans in teplizumab. Changes in the CEX-HPLC signature can
L453: (b) (4)
L454: indicate
L455: The variability between batches, and over time, was higher when compared to the reference (Lilly) product.
L456: OPQ concludes the variability of the CEX-HPLC data under long-term storage conditions for DP preclude a determination of shelf-life and introduce uncertainty regarding interpretation of the CEX-HPLC data to support comparability between proposed commercial and clinical materials (manufactured at AGC Biologics and Eli Lilly, respectively). Importantly, the results of the CEX-HPLC call into question the validity of the analytical similarity assessment. Therefore, OPQ concluded the data submitted in this application are not sufficient to support a conclusion that the manufacture of teplizumab (Tzield) is well-controlled and will lead to a product that is pure and potent for the duration of the shelf-life.
L457: ## Drug substance facilities
L458: (b) (4)
L459: Teplizumab DS is manufactured at the AGC
L460: ## Dating period and storage conditions
L461: The unacceptable charge variation under real-time storage conditions at (b) (4) months precludes setting an expiry for the DS at this time.
