L463: Analytical comparability testing was carried out in order to demonstrate that the proposed commercial material being manufactured at AGC Biologics is comparable to the material that demonstrated safety and efficacy in the clinical studies. The main differences found between AGC and Lilly material were
L464: (b) (4)
L465: The Applicant was advised in a teleconference held April 23, 2021 regarding this issue that they may wish to investigate whether these differences could have caused the increased clearance
L466: of the teplizumab manufactured at AGC as compared to the clinical lot manufactured by Eli Lilly.
L467: ## 4.3. Clinical Microbiology
L468: The microbiological controls used in the drug product manufacturing process, including, drug product specifications for sterility, container closure integrity, endotoxin, validation, depyrogenation and component sterilization, media fill studies, hold times, and postapproval stability commitment, were reviewed and determined to be acceptable by OPQ. For details refer to the *Office of Product Quality (OPQ) review by Dr. Jennifer Swisher dated May 14, 2021*.
L469: ## 4.4. Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L470: The Division of Pharmacology/Toxicology for Cardiology, Hematology, Endocrinology and Nephrology (DPTCHEN) concluded the nonclinical data package supports approval and does not have any recommendations to convey to the Applicant. Dr. Dan Minck was the primary nonclinical reviewer, for details refer to the *nonclinical pharmacology/toxicology review dated May 5, 2021*. Findings from Dr. Minck's review are summarized here.
L471: Teplizumab cross-reacts only with human, gorilla, chimpanzee, and gibbon CD3+ T cells. As a result, a minimal number of nonclinical studies were conducted with teplizumab. Instead, a (b)(4) surrogate monoclonal antibody (mAb) that binds to CD3 in was developed and used for multiple dose toxicology and reproduction and developmental toxicology studies in mice. Treatment of mice with this surrogate induced analogous effects on T-cell populations, B-cells, and cytokines (where evaluated), which supports the use of the surrogate as a model for teplizumab. The surrogate antibody has an apparent affinity for the mouse CD3\epsilon similar to that shown by teplizumab for human CD3.
L472: ## 4.4.1. General Toxicology
L473: The most relevant in vivo study with the original version of teplizumab was conducted in the chimpanzee in which a single subcutaneous dose of 0, 0.1, 1.0, or 10 mg/kg was administered. At the highest dose, all animals died or were euthanized. The cause of death was attributed to a suspected EBV (Epstein-Barr virus)-like lympho-cryptovirus infection. The T-cell suppression occurring as a result of the pharmacologic activity of the drug was a contributing factor. The no-observed-adverse-effect level (NOAEL) for the study was considered the middle dose of 1 mg/kg, or 34 mg/m2 based on body surface area (BSA). This is 3.8-fold higher than the cumulative dose studied in humans (9 mg/m2) in a full 14-day cycle of treatment. Animals treated with a single higher dose (10 mg/kg) died or were euthanized due to infection. Other effects were noted, such as reductions in T-cell populations and increases in cytokines such as TNF\alpha, IL-6, and IL-10. These effects were consistent with the magnitude and time course observed in the clinic.
L474: Two repeat-dose toxicity studies were conducted in mice using the surrogate mAb. This (b) (4) surrogate was used in repeat-dose studies of subcutaneous and intravenous administration in mice. In the murine studies, lymphoid tissue was identified as the target organ, with dose-related reduction in T-cells with a trend toward partial recovery following a 2-week rest period. For a 6-day repeat-dose study, there were no observed off-target effects, the NOAEL for the murine studies was considered to be 20 mg/kg, equivalent to a human dose of 60 mg/m2, approximately 7x the total human dose administered in a cycle of treatment based on body surface area (BSA). In a second repeat-dose murine study, where the teplizumab surrogate was administered every 3 days for 1 month, deaths began to occur following the fourth dose administration at all dosages preceded by symptoms of severe hypersensitivity reactions, the NOAEL was determined to be &lt;0.3 mg/kg. These findings were not reproduced in the reproductive and developmental toxicity studies, with similar dosing regimens.
L475: No stand-alone safety pharmacology studies were performed. Cardiovascular and respiratory functioning was evaluated as part of the single dose study in chimpanzees. Central nervous function (CNS) was evaluated as a component in non-dedicated toxicology studies; no observations suggestive of an effect on the CNS were noted.
L476: ## 4.4.2. Genotoxicity and Carcinogenicity
L477: No genetic toxicology or carcinogenicity studies were submitted for review.
L478: ## 4.4.3. Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
L479: One embryo-fetal development study was performed in mice using the surrogate antibody. Drug or control was administered to pregnant dams on gestational days 6, 10, and 14. There was a numerically small, but statistically persuasive difference in the mean number of viable fetuses per dam in the high dose group (20 mg/kg) compared to lower doses and controls (0, 0.03, 0.3 mg/kg): 11.9-12.1 viable fetuses versus 9.1. Mice that received the high dose exhibited reduced food intake; however, it is not clear if the litter loss was a result of reduced food intake and maternal body weights. The effects at the high dose were considered adverse, leading to identification of 0.3 mg/kg (0.9 mg/m2) as the NOAEL for the study. The NOAEL for the dose level is 1/10th the cumulative clinical dose based on BSA scaling. The NOAEL for the cumulative dose is 3/10th the cumulative clinical dose based on BSA scaling. Importantly, treatment with the surrogate did not affect fetal gross, visceral, or skeletal development. Of note, the next highest dose studied is 60-fold higher, which limits the precision of the NOAEL.
L480: One prenatal and post-natal development study was conducted in mice using the surrogate antibody. Three dose levels of surrogate (b) (4) anti-CD3 or control were administered once every three days starting on gestational day 6 and ending on lactation day 19. Assuming a gestational period of 21 days, up to 12 doses were administered. There were no effects on the
L481: ability of maternal animals to maintain a pregnancy, deliver a litter, and rear the litter. As a result, the F0 maternal NOAEL was considered the highest dose (HD) of 20 mg/kg. At the low dose 0.3 mg/kg (0.9 mg/m2), pup levels ranged from 16% to 37% of maternal levels, but pup levels were not obtained at birth, so it was not possible to determine if the levels in pups reflected placental transfer, transfer from milk, or both. The offspring of dams exposed to the “high dose” had reductions in sperm count and motility and a reduction in the number of resulting pregnancies, but no difference in the ability to maintain a pregnancy and deliver a viable litter. The mid dose (MD) of 3 mg/kg was considered the NOAEL for effects on the offspring. The 3 mg/kg group received cumulatively up to 12 times higher dose than the cumulative clinical dose. The Division of Pharmacology/Toxicology for Cardiology, Hematology, Endocrinology and Nephrology (DPTCHEN) concludes a potential risk exists for effects on the offspring of maternal animals administered high doses of the mAb as an increased incidence of embryofetal loss was observed in an embryofetal development study. Furthermore, treatment of maternal animals through pregnancy and lactation was associated with pharmacologic effects on T and B cell populations, primary and secondary immune responses, and fertility of the F1 animals. DPTCHEN plans to summarize these effects in the proposed label.
L482: ## 4.5. Clinical Pharmacology
L483: The Office of Clinical Pharmacology (OCP), Division of Cardiorenal and Endocrine Pharmacology and Pharmacometrics reviewed the information contained in BLA 761183. The OCP review team recommends a complete response (CR) until the sponsor can adequately address the issue related to lack of comparability between the clinical trial and to-be-marketed drug products. Dr. Harisudhan Thanukrishnan and Dr. Elyes Dahmane were the primary clinical pharmacology and pharmacometrics reviewers. *For details of the clinical pharmacology assessment, refer to the review dated May 5, 2021.* Findings from OCP's review are summarized here.
L484: Teplizumab targets the cluster of differentiation 3 (CD3) antigen, which is co-expressed with the T-cell receptor (TCR) on the surface of T lymphocytes. Though the mechanism of action of teplizumab for the proposed indication has not been confirmed, it appears to involve weak agonistic activity on signaling via the TCR-CD3 complex, which is thought to expand regulatory T-cells and re-establish immune tolerance.
L485: Pharmacokinetic parameters were estimated using population pharmacokinetics. The central and peripheral volume of distribution is 3.4 and 6.9 L, respectively. The mean clearance of teplizumab is 2.3 L/day, and its terminal half-life is ~4 days. Under the proposed clinical dosing regimen, the average accumulation ratio for area under the curve (AUC) between Day 5 and Day 14 (the first and the last day with the full dose administration) is predicted to be 3.4. Steady state is not expected to be achieved at the end of dosing on Day 14. Teplizumab is expected to be catabolized into smaller peptides and is not expected to inhibit or induce major
L486: CYPs or transporters. Exposure to teplizumab after BSA-based regimen was found to be independent of age and body weight. Intrinsic/extrinsic factors are not expected to impact the exposure to this monoclonal antibody.
L487: The majority (~60 to 70%) of study subjects developed anti-drug antibodies (ADA) to teplizumab in response to the single 14-day course. Teplizumab PK concentrations after course 1 were similar when compared between the ADA status. Supporting studies revealed that there was a greater impact of ADA on teplizumab exposure (clearance increased by up to 438%) during course 2, when the 14-day regimen was repeated after 6 months, however, this is not directly relevant to the proposed indication, as a single course will be administered, but may be important to understand from a mechanistic perspective because the Applicant may be planning to study a second treatment course for at-risk patients.
L488: The bioanalytical methods for teplizumab PK assessments partially met the criteria for 'method validation' and 'application to routine analysis' set by the 'Guidance for Industry: Bioanalytical Method Development'. The rationale provided for not meeting the specific criterion on sample storage and method performance during study sample analysis were accepted and their implications are documented in the OCP review. The assays for C-peptide were found adequate to support use as an efficacy biomarker.
L489: In addition, the Applicant had initiated manufacturing of commercial product from a new batch of drug substance at a new facility (AGC Biologics) and submitted results from a bio-comparability study to establish equivalence between the clinical (Eli Lilly and MacroGenics) and the to-be-marketed product (manufactured by AGC Biologics). The results of the pivotal bio-comparability study failed to establish an appropriate bridge between the clinical trial and the to-be-marketed teplizumab products. Although the Cmax was comparable between products, indicating similar drug quantity was delivered, AUC was substantially lower (approximately (B)(4)%) indicating faster clearance of the to-be-marketed formulation. This finding suggests there is a difference in the to-be-marketed antibody, rather than a difference in potency, leading to faster clearance.
L490: Figure 3. Mean (+/- SD) Teplizumab Concentration-Time Profiles Following Single-Dose Administration of Teplizumab Manufactures Using DS from AGC Biologics (Test Product) and Eli Lilly (Reference Product)
L491: Source: Sponsor, PRV-031-004 CSR
Abbreviations: DS, drug substance; SD, standard deviation
L492: The Applicant provided a comparison of reduction in circulating lymphocyte counts as support for biocomparability. However, the validity of lymphocyte count as a single surrogate marker for the proposed indication is not yet established. Hence, PK parameters, during this review, were considered as the sole criterion for the bio comparability assessment from a regulatory perspective (for comparison of product's quality attributes). As PK comparability was not established, a CR is recommended.
L493: ## 4.6. Devices and Companion Diagnostic Issues
L494: A companion diagnostic was not included in the BLA. Commercially available autoantibody assays were used in the teplizumab clinical trials, and the Applicant did not propose specific FDA cleared assays for the product labeling. Center for Devices and Radiological Health (CDRH) identified a potential requirement for the development of autoantibody assays as a companion diagnostic because T1D autoantibody testing is requisite for the identification of the patient
L495: population for teplizumab's intended use. A discussion of the autoantibody assays and their performance characteristics is included in Section 6.1.1.3.
L496: At this time the Division has not concluded whether a companion diagnostic will be needed for this BLA. The discussion of this issue is being deferred to the next cycle for this application, and the Applicant is aware that should a companion diagnostic be required, they will need to bridge the performance of autoantibody assays used in clinical trials to the companion diagnostic devices in the post-marketing period.
L497: ## 4.7. Consumer Study Reviews
L498: Teplizumab is intended to be administered by health care professionals. No label comprehension or human factors studies were necessary for this application.

L509: ## 5.1. Review Strategy
L510: For the efficacy review, the clinical reviewer and the FDA statistician reviewed TN-10 as the single adequate and well-controlled trial submitted to provide substantial evidence of effectiveness for the intended population of patients at risk for T1D. The clinical reviewer and statistician confirmed the Applicant's analyses and conducted additional sensitivity and subgroup analyses that are presented in this review.
L511: The second line of efficacy evidence (confirmatory evidence) was a meta-analysis of five other clinical trials conducted in patients with new- or recent-onset T1D. This group of studies is referred to herein as the 'Integrated Summary of Efficacy,' or 'ISE.' For the efficacy review, the clinical reviewer and FDA statistician did not review the ISE studies in full, but rather focused on the C-peptide change from baseline endpoint, regardless of whether the primary endpoint was C-peptide change from baseline. To explain further, the studies in the ISE were originally designed to evaluate teplizumab's efficacy in preventing further beta-cell decline in patients already diagnosed with T1D, but with residual beta-cell function. The primary endpoint in Protégé was the proportion of subjects who had both a total daily insulin dose of &lt;0.5 units/kg/day and an HbA1c level &lt;6.5\%, and in Encore was the proportion of subjects who had both a total daily insulin dose &lt;0.25 U/Kg/day and an HbA1c level &lt;7.0\%, whereas the three other studies evaluated the change from baseline in the mean C-peptide 4-hour AUC in response to an MMTT. The FDA statistician used different methodology to perform the meta-analysis (the rationale for which is acceptable and explained in detail in the FDA statistical review). In this review, we present the results of the C-peptide meta-analysis conducted by the FDA statistician. Please note that data from TN-10 were not included in the efficacy meta-analysis. Whereas TN-10 collected stimulated C-peptide data, different endpoints and testing methodologies were used, with 2-hour OGTTs used in the TN-10 study, compared to 4-hour MMTTs in the other supporting studies, making direct comparison difficult. In addition, TN-10 did not collect C-peptide data after T1D diagnosis.
L512: For the safety review, the Applicant's integrated summary of safety (ISS) database included a pool of five studies (AbATE, Delay, Protégé, Encore, and TN-10) and served as the basis for the Applicant's safety analysis. The FDA clinical reviewer used the ISS for independently conducted safety analyses.
L513: There are several important distinctions between patients with new- or recent-onset overt T1D and at-risk patients studied in TN-10 that influence the interpretation of adverse events (AEs). For example, rates of DKA and hypoglycemia would not be comparable between patients with and without overt T1D. Therefore, a secondary pool including the four studies in new or recent-onset patients with T1D was analyzed separately from TN-10, using a dataset referred to as the
L514: modified ISS (mISS). Analyses from the mISS were supplementary to the Applicant's safety analyses.
L515: In the Delay study, 18 patients received placebo in the randomized portion of the study but received teplizumab during the open-label extension. In the Applicant's ISS, safety data from these 18 patients were included in both placebo and teplizumab groups. The FDA reviewers analyzed these 18 patients per their originally assigned treatment group and limited analyses to the course 1 treatment period; this approach resulted in a different total number of patients in the full ISS pool and the modified ISS pool compared to the Applicant's analyses. The FDA analyses are presented in this review.
L516: Study 1 was not included in the ISS because it used different dosing regimens than the other studies. Study 1 and other completed controlled trials evaluating teplizumab for the treatment of conditions (e.g., transplant) other than T1D were surveyed for any AESI (adverse event of special interest) or serious adverse event (SAE) that may be informative to the T1D population.
L517: Three supportive studies (AbATE, Encore, and Protégé) used a second treatment cycle of teplizumab. Therefore, AE incidence rates under these conditions may not be comparable to the proposed indication. Additionally, a higher proportion of patients treated with teplizumab in these three studies (vs. comparator) in the first cycle did not receive treatment in the second cycle. Dropout rates may be influenced by study treatment, and thus the AE data in the second cycle may be biased. For this reason, FDA analyses estimating incidence rates and risk differences in the ISS dataset generally present results limited to the first treatment cycle.
L518: ## 6. Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
L519: ## 6.1. TN-10
L520: ## 6.1.1. Study Design
L521: ## 6.1.1.1 Overview and Objective
L522: TN-10 was an efficacy and safety study designed to evaluate the treatment effect of teplizumab compared with placebo in the delay of T1D in individuals at high risk for developing T1D.
L523: ## 6.1.1.2 Trial Design
L524: TN-10 was a randomized, placebo-controlled, double-blind, parallel-arm study. Patients were randomized 1:1 to receive teplizumab or placebo by daily intravenous infusion (over at least 30 minutes) for a 14-day treatment period.
L525: TN-10 was blinded (blinded to patient and clinical investigator) to reduce potential bias during treatment evaluation. The trial medications were identical in appearance, handling, preparation, and administration.
L526: The TN-10 trial was conducted at 30 sites in the US, Canada, United Kingdom, Australia, and Germany. Randomization was stratified by study site and age (&lt;18 years or ≥18 years) by a central TrialNet coordinating center. TN-10 trial design is summarized in Figure 4.
L527: **Figure 4. Overview of TN-10 Trial Design**
L528: **Inclusion criteria:** Age 8-45, Relative with T1D, ≥2 autoantibodies + dysglycemia
L529: **Exclusion criteria:** Diagnosis of T1D
L530: Source: Reviewer-generated, from TN-10 Study Report
Abbreviations: IV, intravenous; T1D, type 1 diabetes mellitus
L531: *Reviewer comment: The TN-10 study design is adequate for the stated objective(s).*
L532: ## 6.1.1.3 Subjects
L533: Study subjects were identified through their participation in the TrialNet TN-01 Natural History Study, a longitudinal monitoring program for relatives of patients with T1D sponsored by the National Institute of Diabetes and Digestive and Kidney Diseases (NIDDK), which was established to improve understanding of the demographic, immunologic, and metabolic characteristics of individuals at risk for developing T1D and to provide a source of subjects for enrollment into prevention trials. Individuals aged 8 years or older with two or more islet autoantibodies and dysglycemia on oral glucose tolerance testing, but not meeting criteria for a diagnosis of T1D, were eligible for enrollment in TN-10.
L534: ## Inclusion Criteria (summarized):
L535: - Participated in the TrialNet Natural History Study (TN-01) and thus, a relative of a patient with T1D
- Positive for two or more diabetes-related autoantibodies (anti-GAD65, anti-ICA512, micro-insulin autoantibody (mIAA), ZnT8 antibody, and/or ICA on 2 occasions, with the more recent within the 6 months prior to study drug administration. Note, the confirmation test did not need to involve the same two autoantibodies as were found on the first occasion. T1D Autoantibodies were counted positive by the following cutoff values: anti-GAD65 &gt;20 units/mL, anti-IA-2 &gt;5 units/mL, mIAA &gt;0.010 JDF (Juvenile Diabetes Foundation) units, ICA &gt;10 JDF units, anti-ZnT8 &gt;0.020 JDF units.
- Between the ages of 1 and 45 years at the time of enrollment in TN-01 and ≥8 years at the time of randomization
- Met criteria for abnormal glucose tolerance on TrialNet-conducted OGTT:Abnormal glucose tolerance defined as a fasting plasma glucose ≥110 mg/dL and &lt;126 mg/dL, or a 2-hour plasma glucose ≥140 mg/dL and &lt;200 mg/dL, or a 30, 60, or 90-minute value on OGTT ≥200 mg/dL.
- If &lt;18 years of age at time of randomization, a single OGTT demonstrating abnormal glucose tolerance within 7 weeks of the baseline visit.
- If ≥18 years of age at the time of randomization, two consecutive OGTTs demonstrating abnormal glucose tolerance, the most recent of which had been within 7 weeks of the baseline visit.
- Body weight ≥26 kg
- Female and male subjects on appropriate contraception
- Willing and able to postpone live vaccine immunizations for 1 year after treatment
L536: ## Exclusion Criteria (summarized):
L537: - A diagnosis of T1D, orIf ≥18 years old, having an OGTT in which fasting plasma glucose ≥126 mg/dL, or 2-hour plasma glucose ≥200 mg/dL
- &lt;18 years old, having a screening random glucose ≥200 mg/dL
- Lymphopenia (&lt;1000 lymphocytes/µL), neutropenia (&lt;1500 polymorphonuclear neutrophils/µL), thrombocytopenia (&lt;150,000 platelets/µL), abnormal coagulation (international normalized ratio [INR] &gt;0.1 above upper limit of normal [ULN]), or anemia (hemoglobin &lt;10 grams/deciliter) at baseline
L538: - AST (aspartate aminotransferase) or ALT (alanine transaminase) &gt;1.5 \times \text{ULN}, and bilirubin &gt;1.5 \times \text{ULN} (with the exception of subjects with the diagnosis of Gilbert's syndrome)
- Infection: Chronic active infection other than localized skin infections, including a positive tuberculin purified protein derivative (PPD) test, or serological evidence of current or past HIV, hepatitis B or hepatitis C infection.
- Acute infection with Epstein-Barr virus (EBV) or cytomegalovirus (CMV), or a history of infectious mononucleosis within the 3 months prior to enrollment.
- Vaccination with a live virus within 8 weeks or with a killed virus within 4 weeks of randomization
- Pregnant, actively attempting to get pregnant, or lactating
- Current use of medications that affect glycemic control
- Chronic use of steroids or other immunosuppressive agents, and/or a history of asthma or atopic disease requiring chronic treatment
- Untreated hypothyroidism or active Graves' disease at randomization
- Prior OKT3 or other anti-CD3 treatment, or administration of a monoclonal antibody within the year before randomization
- Participation in any type of therapeutic drug or vaccine clinical trial within the 12 weeks before randomization
- Any condition that, in the opinion of the investigator, would interfere with the study conduct or the safety of the subject
L539: ## **Reviewer comments:**
L540: ## ***Inclusion criteria***
L541: *A key inclusion criterion was that the subject was to be "Positive for two or more diabetes-related autoantibodies (anti-GAD65, anti-ICA512, micro-insulin autoantibody (mIAA), ZnT8 antibody, and/or ICA) on two occasions." The other key inclusion criterion was meeting criteria for abnormal glucose tolerance on TrialNet-conducted OGTT. As discussed previously in Section 2.1, the risk of developing T1D in patients with two autoantibodies (AA) with dysglycemia has been estimated in natural history studies to be approximately 40% at 6 months, and 74% at 4 years. Together, these criteria enrich the study population to speed the accrual of events and also represent the appropriate target patient population for treatment with teplizumab. Prior studies in at-risk populations have indicated that among at-risk stage 1 patients who ultimately develop T1D, there are fluctuations between the normal and dysglycemic states before the onset of T1D (Sosenko et al. 2009). In TN-10, adult subjects required a*
L542: confirmatory test to diagnose diabetes, although allowed the inclusion of pediatric patients (&lt;18 years old) without a second result confirming dysglycemia on OGTT. Natural history studies in at-risk populations have demonstrated that the estimated hazard rate of developing T1D for ages 8 to 17 years without a confirmed OGTT is 0.194, greater than the hazard rate of developing T1D in age 18 or older with a confirmed OGTT (0.164) (Figure 5). It is also important to note that there was no age by treatment interaction detected in our analysis of TN-10 (see Section 7).
L543: **Figure 5. Model for Time to T1D Adjusting for Baseline Age and OGTT Status**
L544: Source: TN-10 Protocol, generated by TrialNet investigators using data from the Natural History Study (TN-01) to estimate hazard rates for the control group assuming a constant risk over time. Sample size estimates were determined from this model by applying the eligibility criteria for TN-10 study.
L545: Abbreviations: OGTT, oral glucose tolerance test; T1D, type 1 diabetes
L546: *If approved, product labeling should emphasize the importance of a confirmatory test among the age group &gt;18 years old.*
L547: *With respect to the following inclusion criterion: Participated in the TrialNet Natural History Study (TN-01), it is important to note that all participants in TN-01 were relatives of a patient with T1D, and TN-01 was used to recruit patients for TN-10. Therefore, this was a convenience criterion. The applicability of the TN-10 data to non-relatives is discussed elsewhere in this review.*
L548: *Patients at least 8 years of age were enrolled in TN-10 because children are disproportionately affected with T1D. In addition, 501 children ages 8 to 17 years old had received teplizumab treatment in other clinical trials lending reassurance with respect to safety in this age group.*
L549: *Only patients with body weight \ge 26 kg were eligible for enrollment in this trial, which is approximately 50th percentile for pediatrics 8 years of age. As teplizumab dosing is based on body surface area, overdose is not a concern for real world use, and I do not recommend a labeling restriction based on body weight. See Section 7.2.1 for additional discussion on efficacy and safety considerations regarding this inclusion criterion.*
L550: *Subjects also needed to be willing and able to postpone live vaccine immunizations for 1 year after treatment in order to enroll in TN-10. Therefore, the safety of live vaccine immunizations after a course of teplizumab was not investigated in the clinical study. Following consultation with the Division of Hematologic Malignancies 1 (DHM1) regarding the risk of immunosuppression, the withholding of live vaccination for 1-year post-treatment per the protocol does not appear to be guided by the true risks of immunosuppression with teplizumab. Instead, the DHM1 team recommended consideration of a Warning and Precaution against live viral vaccination within 4 weeks prior to planned use and within at least 3 months after treatment with teplizumab, which would sufficiently mitigate the risk of serious infections.*
L551: ## Exclusion criteria
L552: *The TN-10 glycemic-related exclusion criteria prevented patients who were likely to have already progressed to T1D from enrolling so were fully justified.*
L553: *Excluding patients for lymphopenia or transaminase abnormalities is related to the potential for teplizumab to cause cytokine release syndrome (discussed elsewhere in this review). Similarly, the protocol excluded patients with chronic active infection and active infection with EBV or CMV for subject protection in light of the immunosuppressive effect of teplizumab. Therefore, these criteria are reasonable and may need to be considered for labeling.*
L554: *TN-10 exclusion criterion 10 prevented patients with untreated hypothyroidism or Graves' hyperthyroidism from enrolling. This was likely an efficacy consideration as untreated thyroid disorders have been shown to worsen the metabolic control of patients with T1D, with the assumption that this too may impact progression to T1D in at-risk patients (Biondi et al. 2019). Patients with genetic risk of T1D are also susceptible to other autoimmune disorders such as autoimmune*
L555: *hypothyroidism, which is relatively common; therefore, inclusion of patients with treated hypothyroidism, like the TN-10 protocol, would be recommended if approved. Similar to the TN-10 protocol, treatment should be delayed in patients with active Graves' disease or untreated hypothyroidism in order to avoid the risk of adverse events associated with both conditions, e.g., hemodynamic events.*
L556: *The protocol also excluded patients with asthma or atopic disease requiring chronic treatment for the purpose of improving interpretation of the trial's efficacy, as glucocorticoid use in these conditions may contribute to dysglycemia, and there was concern regarding added immunosuppression with corticosteroid use. I do not recommend disallowing patients with asthma or atopic disease requiring chronic treatment from receiving teplizumab therapy post-approval. Many children use inhaled daily corticosteroids or topical steroids for atopic disease, and systemic side effects (such as adrenal suppression and hyperglycemia) risks are minimal for patients receiving low doses but can be substantial for high doses (National Asthma Education and Prevention Program 2007). Therefore, in real world use, implementation of these broad exclusion criteria may overly restrict the population that could benefit from teplizumab. Instead, I recommend consideration of a Warning and Precaution for immunosuppression with teplizumab if the patient is taking corticosteroid doses with significant risk of immunosuppression.*
L557: *Although exclusion criteria for TN-10 mentioned any condition that, in the opinion of the investigator, would interfere with the study conduct or the safety of the subject, there were no patients reported as screen failures meeting this criterion, and therefore, this criterion did not affect the safety or efficacy conclusions for TN-10.*
L558: ## Screening T1D Autoantibody Testing
L559: A focused discussion on T1D autoantibody testing is warranted because these tests are not routinely recommended in clinical practice, and there are assay considerations that must be addressed.
L560: [REDACTED] (b) (4) developed autoantibody assays. Screening for T1D autoantibodies to identify at-risk patients for TN-10 was performed entirely through the TrialNet Pathway to Prevention screening study (TN-01) through [REDACTED] (b) (4). These autoantibody assays are not FDA cleared, but are widely used in clinical practice and in the research setting.
L561: In general, surveillance of the quality of T1D autoantibody assays has largely been provided by the Islet Autoantibody Standardization Program (IASP) workshop, organized by the NIH NIDDK
L562: and the Immunology of Diabetes Society. IASP organizes international interlaboratory comparisons and provides participating laboratories with blinded T1D, multiple islet autoantibody-positive at-risk first-degree relatives of T1D patients, and control serum samples to assess and compare interlaboratory sensitivity, specificity, and accuracy. Centralized collection and analysis of results by the IASP provide participants with an unbiased comparison of assay performance. The Applicant provided IASP validation certificates from the [REDACTED] (b) (4) demonstrating the consistency of the specificity of these tests at 97-100% during this time period (Table 4). [REDACTED] (b) (4) assay is also CLIA2-certified.

L575: Reviewer comment: The Applicant provided evidence that screening T1D autoantibodies used in the TN-10 study were highly specific for the identification of the at-risk population (stage 2 T1D) and comparable to the FDA-cleared (b) (4) assay. In TN-10, the presence of \ge 2 T1D autoantibodies was verified with a second confirmatory antibody panel, which improved the specificity of this testing. Although the antibody type did not need to be consistent between the initial and confirmatory test, the presence of 2 or more T1D autoantibodies, regardless of type, is highly predictive of risk of T1D (Ziegler et al. 2013). Additionally, the requirement for the presence of dysglycemia ensures that all patients enrolled in the trial have stage 2 disease. For these patients, false positives for antibody assessments are unlikely because dysglycemia distinguishes advancement from stage 1 to stage 2 T1D.
L576: ## 6.1.1.4 Study Treatments and Dose Selection
L577: The treatment course consisted of daily doses of 51 \mu\text{g}/\text{m}^2, 103 \mu\text{g}/\text{m}^2, 207 \mu\text{g}/\text{m}^2, 413 \mu\text{g}/\text{m}^2 during the first 4 days, respectively, and 10 subsequent daily doses of 826 \mu\text{g}/\text{m}^2, with a total cumulative dose of 9034 \mu\text{g}/\text{m}^2 (\sim 9 \text{mg}/\text{m}^2). Less than 10% of the total dose is given on the first 4 days of ramp-up as a precaution to avoid adverse reactions mediated by immune cell activation, especially cytokine release syndrome. In addition to avoidance of adverse events, dose selection was made considering evidence from studies conducted in patients with new- or recent-onset T1D. Analysis of secondary and exploratory endpoints of the Protégé study
L578: demonstrated a significant improvement in AUC mean C-peptide during a 4-hour MMTT in patients with new-onset T1D treated with the same full-dose 14-day course as used in TN-10 (\sim 9 \mu\text{g}/\text{m}^2) compared with placebo (p=0.046), but not with a 1/3 dose 14-day course (\sim 3 \mu\text{g}/\text{m}^2) or a 6-day curtailed regimen (\sim 2.4 \mu\text{g}/\text{m}^2) (Sherry et al. 2011).
L579: Teplizumab or saline was to be given as an IV infusion over a minimum of 30 minutes in an outpatient research or hospital setting. The infusion was scheduled at the same time each day \pm 4 hours.
L580: Study drug infusion was to be withheld in the case of laboratory abnormalities or other adverse events (discussed in subsequent sections), but there was no provision for dose reduction in the case of adverse events. In addition to withdrawal of consent and pregnancy for female patients, study drug infusion was withheld for the following laboratory abnormalities:
L581: - Hepatic abnormalities (total bilirubin &gt;1.3 \text{ mg}/\text{dL} on day 1, \ge 2.0 \text{ mg}/\text{dL} on other days. AST level &gt;2\times \text{ULN} on Day 1. AST, ALT or (lactate dehydrogenase) LDH \ge 3\times \text{ULN} on other days)
- Thrombocytopenia (platelets &lt;140,000/\mu\text{L} on day 1, &lt;100,000/\mu\text{L} on other days)
- Neutropenia (&lt;1000 \text{ cells}/\text{m}^3)
- Anemia (hemoglobin \le 8.5 \text{ g}/\text{dL} or a drop in \ge 2\text{g}/\text{dL} compared with prior to infusion to a value &lt;10.0 \text{ g}/\text{dL})
- Coagulopathy (INR &gt;0.1 above ULN)
L582: Study drug infusions were also held for anaphylaxis, fever of grade 3 or higher on Day 0 or Day 1, or any Grade 3 or higher adverse event (except for lymphopenia, hypoglycemia, hyperglycemia, fatigue/malaise, insomnia, cheilitis, dry skin, nail changes, hot flushes/flushes, headache, myalgia, flu-like symptoms) or any medically important event that in the opinion of the investigator, contraindicated continued dosing. Several of these criteria were developed by the data safety monitoring board (DSMB) following a safety review performed in 2006. In patients with new-onset T1D, the DSMB identified a symptom complex consistent with cytokine release syndrome (CRS) during the first 6 days of teplizumab treatment, requiring drug discontinuation in about 10% (6/60) of patients with T1D. The DSMB identified a grade 3 fever (defined as temperature &gt;40^\circ\text{C} [104^\circ\text{F}] for 24 hours or more), increase in prothrombin time (PT) and/or partial thromboplastin time (PTT) and an increased D-dimer, and increase in total bilirubin, hypotension and increase in liver enzymes in these cases. Importantly, all cases identified were self-limited, did not progress, and completely resolved without sequelae.
L583: *Reviewer comment: The protocol-specified dose withholding instructions for TN-10 seem reasonable. In addition, the Applicant explained in detail how specific drug withdrawal criteria relate to the DSMB's identification of features of CRS; therefore, stringent criteria for withholding drug were developed to improve the safety of teplizumab administration. As we do not know if the protocol-specified*
L584: *withdrawal criteria may have prevented more serious or permanent harms from continued dosing, the specific drug discontinuation criteria used in TN-10 should also be provided in labeling.*
L585: *Unblinding*—Importantly, given that safety lab assessments were performed on the same day prior to each infusion, there is potential for unintentional unblinding in TN-10, as the investigators were required to perform laboratory assessments including assessment for lymphopenia prior to drug infusions. As constitutional symptoms and lymphopenia were frequently observed in teplizumab-treated patients, it is plausible that investigators were able to distinguish teplizumab from placebo.
L586: ## 6.1.1.5 Study Visits and Procedures
L587: Patients in TN-10 underwent a screening visit, 14 dosing visits, and follow-up safety visits at Day 20, 6 weeks, 3 months, 6 months and then every 6 months thereafter. A table of study visits and procedures is presented in Table 6.
L588: A screening visit with OGTT occurred within 7 weeks of the first dosing visit. The screening visit and follow-up visits could be completed at any TrialNet site, whereas the baseline and infusion visits (Day 1 to 14) were completed at a limited number of designated TrialNet infusion sites.
L589: Safety assessments (physical examination, vitals, chemistry and hematology labs, HbA1c, EBV and CMV viral loads, serologies, etc.) and efficacy assessments (OGTT and assessments for symptoms of T1D) were performed according to the schedule of activities as summarized in Table 6.
L590: - **Screening Visit (Day -1):** Because patients were identified through their participation in the Natural History/Pathway to Prevention screening study (TN-01), the 'screening visit' was the visit in which the presence of stage 2 disease was either identified (in patients &lt;18 years) or confirmed (in patients 18 years and older). Following this visit, patients were assessed for inclusion/exclusion criteria and completed informed consent. At this visit, patients received HbA1c and random glucose testing (if &lt;18 years of age and not undergoing an OGTT on the same day). Also, HIV and Hepatitis B and C serologies and PPD testing were performed.
- **Dosing Visits (Day 0-13):** Vital signs were monitored for 2 hours after each infusion. Laboratory studies were obtained prior to each dose and the results of chemistries including liver function tests, complete blood count (CBC), and coagulation parameters were reviewed each day prior to commencement of the drug infusion. An EKG was performed and reviewed prior to initiation of study drug. Ibuprofen and antihistamine were administered prophylactically prior to teplizumab and placebo infusion on the first 5 days of treatment. Further dosing of ibuprofen, antihistamines, and/or acetaminophen was used as
L591: needed for fever, malaise, headache, arthralgia, or rash. Mechanistic assessments including samples for RNA, plasma, serum, DNA, and measures of B and T cell number and function were collected at baseline, day 20, months 3, 6, 18, 30, 42, 54, 66, and every 6 months thereafter, to understand the effect of teplizumab on the immune system and risk for infectious disease. The schedule for these assessments could vary as appropriate. At no time would the blood draw volume exceed what was allowable according to the patient's age and body weight.
L592: *Reviewer comment: The protocol-guided premedication with prophylactic ibuprofen and antihistamine in TN-10 may have had a role in mitigating infusion-related reactions or masking some symptoms of CRS (like fever). We reviewed the premedication guidance for TN-10 with the FDA Division of Hematologic Malignancies 1 (DHM1) in consultation, who agreed that the premedication in the protocol was appropriate. We recommend that the same premedications should be included in section 2 of the label.*
L593: 3. **Follow-Up Safety &amp; Efficacy Visits:** In addition to routine safety monitoring, other important monitoring included EBV and CMV serologies and viral loads. Additional blood samples were drawn in the case of symptoms of EBV/CMV or for drug withholding. Blood was collected for the assessment of anti-drug antibodies (ADAs) at 3 months. An EKG was performed at month 12. A limited physical exam was performed at months 3, 6, 18, 30, 42, 54, 66, and every 6 months thereafter. All patients had interim contact with study personnel for formal inquiry about AEs, presence or absence of blurred vision, polyuria, polydipsia, or unintended weight loss. In addition, random glucose samples were obtained at 3-month intervals, alternating with scheduled OGTTs, and those with symptoms or random glucose &gt;200 mg/dL underwent fasting glucose or OGTT evaluation. Patients who developed diabetes during the follow-up period (and thus reached the study endpoint), were offered annual follow-up, but this was optional.
L594: *Reviewer comment: Even though the TN-10 protocol specified continued follow-up for safety assessments after patients met the primary endpoint (diagnosis of T1D), these safety analyses post-diagnosis were not pre-specified and were not included in the BLA as the majority of patients did not attend post-diagnosis follow-up safety visits.*

L605: ## Administrative Structure
L606: For the purposes of adverse event reporting, an independent physician masked to treatment assignment was designated to serve as the medical monitor for TN-10, and reviewed all adverse event reports, and had the duty to file event reports.
L607: The data safety monitoring board (DSMB) met approximately every 3 months to review indicators of safety. In addition, they met every 6 months to review the interim effectiveness and potential toxicity of the study treatments based on interim analyses of indicators of effectiveness and safety. The DSMB was firewalled from the investigators and sponsor.
L608: A [REDACTED] (b) (4) which was masked to treatment assignment, was to review all relevant information for each patient in TN-10 who was diagnosed with T1D by way of symptoms and casual glucose &gt;200 mg/dL. The Committee was to determine whether the diagnosis of diabetes in each of these patients sufficiently sound so as to include that patient among the cases who have reached the primary outcome in the statistical analysis.
L609: *Reviewer comment: The majority of patients meeting the primary outcome, 34/43 the T1D diagnoses were derived based on two consecutive OGTTs. Only two patients were brought to the committee via this method for adjudication and these records were reviewed by the FDA and determined to be adequate in demonstrating that these patients met the primary endpoint at the date selected.*
L610: ## 6.1.1.6 Study Endpoints
L611: ## Primary Endpoints
L612: The primary efficacy endpoint for TN-10 was the elapsed time from randomization to diagnosis of T1D or time of last contact. The diagnosis of T1D is a clinical endpoint as it directly assesses how a patient feels or functions. This endpoint was not discussed with FDA prior to conduct of the study but was agreed upon at the pre-BLA meeting between the FDA and Applicant that it was an acceptable endpoint to support the proposed indication. See Section 3, Regulatory Background. There is no regulatory precedent for this endpoint.
L613: T1D was diagnosed by the following criteria: a 2-hour plasma glucose of \ge 200 mg/dL on oral glucose tolerance testing, a casual plasma glucose &gt;200 mg/dL plus symptoms of diabetes (defined as polyuria, polydipsia, or weight loss), or a fasting plasma glucose \ge 126 mg/dL. These criteria had to be confirmed on two occasions at least 1 day apart. Diagnosis occurred on the date of the confirmatory test. In addition, the presence of unequivocal hyperglycemia with acute metabolic decompensation, or diabetic ketoacidosis (or DKA) was also considered diagnostic of T1D. Cases diagnosed with these latter criteria were to be adjudicated by the
L614: (b) (4) If diagnosis was confirmed through adjudication, diagnosis occurred on the date the patient was confirmed to have met criteria.
L615: Oral glucose tolerance tests (OGTTs) for ascertainment of the primary endpoint were performed at baseline, month 3, month 6, and every 6 months thereafter until diagnosis of T1D or study termination. In addition, at 3 month intervals in which there was no OGTT scheduled, random post-prandial blood glucose levels were measured, and via intermittent phone contact, patients were asked directed questions about the presence or absence of symptoms associated with diabetes (blurry vision, unintended weight loss &gt;3 kg, polyuria, and polydipsia). If indicated by symptoms or random screening glucose levels, a fasting blood glucose or OGTT screening was performed.
L616: Reviewer comment: The method of event ascertainment is acceptable and is consistent with clinical practice (the method of diagnosis of T1D in TN-10 is the same that is used in clinical practice, with the exception of not including the HbA1c \ge 6.5\% criterion). The frequency of testing (every 3 months) is also acceptable. As discussed earlier, there is a reasonable possibility that unintentional unblinding occurred in the TN-10 study. However, the primary endpoint is based on an objective laboratory-based measure.
L617: ## Secondary Endpoints
L618: In addition to the analyses supporting the primary endpoint, time to diagnosis of T1D, the Applicant provided assessments of C-peptide, a biomarker that reflects beta cell function.
L619: C-peptide is typically measured through the oral glucose tolerance test (OGTT) prior to a diagnosis of T1D, and through a MMTT after clinical T1D diagnosis. These methods result in a 'stimulated' C-peptide result (i.e., stimulated by oral intake of carbohydrate).
L620: Stimulated C-peptide from a 2-hour OGTT was a secondary endpoint in TN-10. C-peptide data in response to OGTT were collected pre-baseline (up to 1 to 2 years before randomization as part of the TN-01 study), at baseline (randomization), then month 3, month 6 and every 6 months thereafter until the primary endpoint of T1D diagnosis was met. C-peptide was not collected after T1D diagnosis. The C-peptide parameters included area under the curve (AUC), mean AUC, and peak C-peptide. Glucose and insulin measurements were also collected.
L621: In this review, we focused on mean C-peptide AUC as this parameter was also used in the meta-analysis included as confirmatory evidence to support efficacy. In addition, our analyses focused on C-peptide data for the first 12 months of observation only. It is important to note that because patients did not continue to have 2-hour OGTT monitoring after T1D diagnosis, upon diagnosis, there was informative censoring, rendering an analysis of available C-peptide
L622: data uninterpretable after the point when censoring became an issue (approximately 6 to 12 months post-randomization).
L623: ## Statistical Analysis Plan
L624: The statistical analysis plan (SAP) is reviewed in detail in Dr. Wang's statistical review. Provided here is a brief overview and commentary on aspects or issues of the SAP that have clinical relevance or affect interpretation of the efficacy results.
L625: The SAP was reviewed by the FDA prior to submission, and SAP revisions were made following recommendations from the FDA with regard to handling of missing data and revisions to sample size. The original study protocol planned to enroll 144 subjects over 2 to 3 years. Because of slower than expected accrual rates, the protocol was revised to reduce the sample size to 71 and revised to follow all enrolled subjects until 40 subjects were diagnosed with T1D (event-driven trial). The study was targeted to detect an effect size measured by a hazard ratio of 0.4 for teplizumab vs. placebo, with 80% power at a one-sided alpha level of 0.025. The Agency agreed that 40 events would be sufficient, and revisions were made to the protocol and the SAP. In addition, the FDA reviewed the SAP (March 27, 2018 version) prior to interim efficacy monitoring or unblinding of TN-10 study results.
L626: Defined analysis populations included the intention-to-treat (ITT) population, all patients who were randomized; the full analysis set (FAS), which included patients who received any study treatment; and the safety population, which analyzed patients based on treatment received. Analyses on ITT and FAS sets were conducted by assigned treatment, regardless of treatment adherence.
L627: Per the SAP, at the time of the study cutoff date, patients who had experienced a T1D diagnosis were considered as having "completed study." Study completion was defined as the date the patient was confirmed as meeting criteria for T1D. Study completion status for patients who were still T1D-free and continued to be followed at the study cutoff date was categorized as "ongoing", and patients were to be censored at the last OGTT measurement or physical examination, whichever was later. If patients discontinued the study before the clinical cutoff, they were censored at their last contact date.
L628: The Applicant summarized the primary endpoint using Kaplan-Meier (KM) estimates to demonstrate the proportion of patients free of T1D by time since randomization. A Cox proportional hazards (PH) model was used to analyze the time to T1D diagnosis, stratified by age and OGTT status at randomization. The Cox model was adjusted for age, with the exception of the interaction test for age (&lt;18 years vs. ≥18 years) but was not adjusted for multiple testing.
L629: The Applicant prospectively planned three sensitivity analyses to handle missing data:
L630: - Sensitivity analysis #1 (SA1) set time-to-diagnosis of T1D to 1 year for placebo patients whose time to event was within 1 year. By delaying event time in a portion of patients in the placebo arm, this approach favors placebo to an extent depending on the actual data.
- Sensitivity analysis #2 (SA2) assumed that subjects who were censored before study cutoff had been diagnosed with T1D at their last evaluation. SA2 penalizes missing data without favoring either treatment group at the analysis planning stage. The actual data determine which arm will benefit from this approach.
- Sensitivity analysis #3 (SA3) was a combination of SA1 and SA2.
L631: The three pre-planned sensitivity analyses (SAs) were all considered reasonable by the FDA statistical reviewer in handling missing data.
L632: *Reviewer comment: In addition, the FDA statistical reviewer also performed a fourth sensitivity analysis, which was similar to SA2, but only applied the imputation (T1D diagnosis) to teplizumab patients who were censored before study cutoff.*
L633: The Applicant also prespecified specific subgroup analyses based on baseline age, sex, body mass index (BMI) at baseline (&lt;median, \gemedian), autoantibody type (anti-GAD65, micro insulin, anti-IA-2, ICA, anti-ZnT8), HLA type (HLADR3, HLA-DR4), pretreatment C-peptide (&lt;median, \gemedian) and glucose levels (&lt;median, \gemedian) during the oral glucose-tolerance tests. These subgroup analyses were planned to be presented as a forest plot, with hazard ratios and 95% confidence intervals to compare teplizumab's effects between each subgroup.
L634: *Reviewer comment: In addition to corroborating the Applicant's subgroup analyses per the SAP, FDA performed additional subgroup analyses evaluating important baseline characteristics from the literature with the potential to modulate teplizumab's treatment effect, including patient age (by quartile), BMI, BMI-z, genetic characteristics (HLA type), and autoantibody number.*
L635: The initial TN-10 study design (the original protocol, dated June 25, 2014; Statistical Analysis Plan (SAP), dated October 26, 2017) included plans on assessing treatment effect on secondary efficacy endpoints such as C-peptide AUC from a 2-hour OGTT. The original objective was to determine whether treatment with teplizumab was superior to placebo with respect to C-peptide response to oral glucose, as obtained from repeated collections during longitudinal tests.
L636: *Reviewer comment: Because of both the varying lengths of patients' participation in this time-to-event study and the discontinuation of C-peptide AUC collection upon T1D diagnosis, the C-peptide variables were determined by FDA to be*
L637: *insufficient to support an appropriate intent-to-treat analysis, but instead were considered exploratory analyses for descriptive purposes.*
L638: There was one planned interim analysis conducted when 18 subjects were diagnosed with T1D. Please refer to the statistical review for further information. We confirmed that results of the interim analysis were firewalled from investigators and from the sponsor of teplizumab at the time (MacroGenics); only the closed-group DSMB had access.
L639: ## 6.1.1.7 Protocol Amendments
L640: The original study protocol for TN-10 was amended four times during the study. Key changes to the protocol are listed below and were incorporated into the final protocol, which was dated June 25, 2014.
L641: The most substantive protocol changes were related to modifications to the target sample size because of slower than expected enrollment. This prompted changes in the study accrual goals (71 patients vs. the original 140), study cutoff point (at 40 T1D diagnoses vs. the original 69) and study duration (6 to 10 years vs. the original 2 to 3 years). These changes to accrual goals and changes in study design power calculations were acknowledged by FDA, in addition to revisions in the SAP made to address these changes. In addition to changes in study accrual goals, inclusion criteria were modified to bolster enrollment through “expanded eligibility criteria.” These expanded criteria changed the requirement for a second confirmatory dysglycemic OGTT (within 7 weeks of the baseline visit), to allow individuals &lt;18 years to qualify for enrollment based on a single qualifying OGTT meeting criteria for dysglycemia; the confirmatory OGTT remained a requirement for patients ≥18 years. As noted previously, this determination was guided by evidence that the risk of progression to T1D is similar among at-risk patients &lt;18 years of age with a single dysglycemic OGTT compared to at-risk patients ≥18 years of age with two consecutive OGTTs consistent with dysglycemia. Analyses outlined for the primary efficacy endpoint were also adjusted to be stratified by age and OGTT status (confirmed abnormal or not) prior to randomization. In addition, exclusion criteria were modified to exclude at-risk patients &lt;18 years of age with screening random glucose ≥200 mg/dL in order to prevent the enrollment of patients who had already progressed to T1D.
L642: There were minor changes to the laboratory-based exclusion criteria to allow the enrollment of patients with Gilbert's syndrome, who could enroll despite meeting laboratory based exclusion criteria, e.g., they were allowed to have total bilirubin above 1.5x ULN, provided they had no other causes of hyperbilirubinemia. This amendment was considered in the Agency's review of safety data for this subgroup of patients with baseline elevations in total bilirubin.
L643: Other than enrollment criteria modifications, there were changes made to the method and timing of monitoring for the primary endpoint, which was changed from monitoring specifying “3-month intervals by phone contact” outside of scheduled visits to monitoring via “routine
L644: contact between visits.” As contact was specified in the schedule of assessments at every 3-month intervals, this amendment allowed for more frequent contact by methods other than phone.
L645: ## 6.1.2. Study Results
L646: ## Compliance with Good Clinical Practice
L647: The Applicant provided attestation that the TN-10 study was performed in compliance with International Council for Harmonization (ICH) guideline on Good Clinical Practice.
L648: ## Financial Disclosure
L649: The Applicant has adequately disclosed financial arrangements with clinical investigators, and no investigators had disclosable financial interests. See Section 13.2 for details.
L650: ## 6.1.2.1 Patient Disposition
L651: In total, 146 patients were screened, of whom 76 were randomized and included in the full analysis set (Table 7). Of the 76 patients randomly assigned to the two treatment groups: 44 (58%) were assigned to the teplizumab group and 32 (42%) to the placebo group. All patients received at least one dose of the randomized treatment.
L652: Of the 70 screen failures, 37/70 (52.9%) had normal OGTT results, i.e., did not meet criteria for dysglycemia on OGTT, and 20/70 (28.6%) failed screening because of OGTT results meeting criteria for T1D. Six of 70 (8.6%) patients were lost to follow-up, 3/70 (4.3%) withdrew consent prior to randomization, 2/70 (2.9%) failed screening secondary to chronic steroid use, 1/70 (1.4%) had a T1D autoantibody count not meeting criteria, and 1/70 (1.4%) had lymphopenia.

L656: Reviewer comment: Despite 1:1 randomization, there was a notable imbalance in the number of patients randomized to each treatment group. The Applicant postulates that with stratified randomization, although near balance was reached within strata, imbalance for the total trial occurred due to chance. This imbalance did not affect the interpretation of the trial integrity or efficacy endpoint.
L657: **Table 8** summarizes treatment completion status and study completion status. Three patients discontinued study treatment during the 14-day treatment course: one (2.3%) from the teplizumab group, and two (6.3%) from the placebo group. The reason for treatment discontinuation was *protocol specified drug withholding* for all three patients. To prevent missing data, these patients were followed up in the same fashion as patients who completed treatment.
L658: As prespecified in the SAP, the clinical cutoff of the study occurred when at least 40 patients were diagnosed with T1D. At that time, patients who had experienced T1D diagnoses had “completed study.” Study completion status for the 30 patients who were still T1D-free and continued to be followed-up was categorized as “ongoing.” Six patients discontinued the study before the clinical cutoff: 3 were diagnosed with T1D, and 3 were censored at their last contact date.

L662: ## 6.1.2.2 Protocol Violations/Deviations
L663: There was a single diagnosis of T1D in the teplizumab treated group in a patient who did not complete the confirmatory OGTT following clinical alert. The patient met criteria for diagnosis of T1D by initial OGTT but upon date of confirmation OGTT, there was failure to obtain IV access. The notes state that the investigator did not want to put the participant through an additional OGTT for the purpose of confirming a new diagnosis of T1D. On review of additional records, this patient was not reported to initiate insulin therapy until approximately 20 months
L664: after the initial critical OGTT date. Because the date of T1D diagnosis was reported as the date of the initial OGTT, this protocol violation may have biased the results in favor of placebo.
L665: ## 6.1.2.3 Demographic Characteristics
L666: The mean age of TN-10 patients was 19 years, more patients were male (55%) and the majority were White (97%) and from the US (95%). Almost 75% of randomized patients were younger than 18 years of age. FDA further grouped the population by age, with results presented below. Treatment groups were balanced for sex, race, and country.

L672: Reviewer comment: There is an inverse association between age and onset of T1D, and disease incidence peaks in childhood between 10 to 14 years of age (Maahs et al. 2010). This association has been shown in several cohorts of individuals at risk of T1D (Ziegler et al. 2013; Steck et al. 2015; Gorus et al. 2017), including stage 1 and 2 T1D (Jacobsen et al. 2020). It is unclear whether age impacts the risk of progression to T1D once metabolic disturbances (such as the dysglycemia seen in stage 2 T1D) are present.
L673: *The observed baseline age imbalance between teplizumab and placebo groups may bias the results in favor of teplizumab because the expected rate of progression to T1D would be slower in adults than in children; however, the primary analysis model included age as a covariate.*
L674: *Most of the enrolled patients were from the US and for the most part, the demographic characteristics of the enrolled patients are representative of US patients at risk for T1D. A possible exception is the near 100% White racial distribution. Although T1D occurs predominately in White patients, a non-negligible percentage occur in non-White patients and in patients of Hispanic ethnicity. These demographics are not represented in the trial.*
L675: ## 6.1.2.4 Other Important Baseline Characteristics
L676: ## BMI and T1D-Related Disease Characteristics
L677: The following additional factors have been identified in the literature to have a potentially modulating effect on the risk of progression to T1D in at-risk individuals: BMI z-score, presence or absence of high-risk genes, i.e., the human leukocyte antigen (HLA) genes, and islet autoantibody number and type. There are some baseline imbalances in these factors, likely related to small study size.
L678: Notably, on average, these additional baseline characteristics appear representative of the U.S. population of at-risk T1D patients.

L684: * A single patient was enrolled with 1 autoantibody detected, but the titer for the second autoantibody was borderline between positive and negative. This subject was permitted to enroll in the study at the Investigator's discretion.
L685: ## Body Mass Index Z-Score
L686: Body mass index (BMI) z-score is used in children to control for the effects of age, height, and sex on BMI. Whereas BMI was mostly balanced across groups, for patients younger than 20 years of age, the BMI-for-age z-score (BMI-z), was lower at baseline amongst patients treated with teplizumab. There is some evidence that higher BMI-z predicts more rapid T1D onset (Barker et al. 2007; Viner et al. 2008; Ferrara et al. 2017). Therefore, the lower BMI-z at baseline among teplizumab patients may favor teplizumab. Similar to age, however, it is unclear whether BMI-z affects the risk of progression to T1D in multiple-antibody-positive individuals once metabolic disturbances are present.
L687: ## Human Leukocyte Antigen (HLA) Subtypes
L688: As discussed in Section 2.1, HLA class II genes, responsible for presentation of antigens to the immune system, account for approximately half of the genetic susceptibility of T1D risk. In the TN-10 study, the presence or absence of DR4 and DR3 were balanced between treatment groups. In addition, the combinations of these genotypes, displayed in the second part of Table 10, were balanced between groups, with some variation likely due to the small study size.
L689: ## Number of Positive Autoantibodies
L690: The relationship between autoantibody status and disease progression was discussed in Section 2.1. Because of its potential prognostic effect on the clinical outcome, the number of baseline positive autoantibodies was summarized by treatment arm. There was a baseline imbalance in treatment allocation across different numbers of positive autoantibodies. Teplizumab-treated patients had a lower number of islet autoantibodies (mean 3.3, median 3 autoantibodies) than placebo patients (mean 3.6, median 4 autoantibodies). This difference is also demonstrated by a lower proportion of patients with 4 or 5 islet autoantibodies at randomization in the
L691: teplizumab arm (20/44 [45.5%]) compared with (20/32 [62.5%]) in the placebo arm. Overall, there were more teplizumab patients who had 2 or 3 autoantibodies at baseline and fewer teplizumab patients who had 4 or more at baseline. This difference may favor teplizumab if the expected rate of conversion to clinical T1D is slower in the group with fewer positive autoantibodies at baseline.
L692: *Reviewer comment: Given the small size of the TN-10 study, baseline imbalances in important disease characteristics are not unexpected. Additional sensitivity analyses conducted by the FDA statistical reviewer to address these imbalances supported the validity of the primary analysis; these are presented in Section 6.1.2.6.*
L693: ## Baseline Glycemic Status and C-peptide Concentrations
L694: The diagnosis of T1D is a dichotomous (yes/no) outcome but is based on a progression of worsening glycemic status and decreasing C-peptide. The Applicant's assessment of baseline glycemic status and C-peptide concentrations demonstrates that the treatment groups were balanced at baseline with respect to these factors (Table 11).

L699: ## 6.1.2.5 Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use
L700: Teplizumab is administered for a 14-day course by health care professionals. No difference in compliance was observed. Rescue medication is not relevant to this product. Concomitant medications to treat symptoms of cytokine release syndrome are not expected to affect the efficacy results.
L701: ## 6.1.2.6 Efficacy Results - Primary Endpoint
L702: Please see Dr. Wang's statistical review for additional information about the efficacy results. Dr. Wang recommends approval of this BLA.
L703: Forty-three patients met the primary endpoint (T1D diagnosis) prior to the study cutoff date. The majority, 79% (34/43) of patients met criteria for T1D diagnosis by two consecutive OGTTs, representing 70% (14/20) of the T1D diagnoses in the teplizumab-treated group and 87% (20/23) of the diagnoses in the placebo group.
L704: The median time to clinical T1D diagnosis was 49.5 months in the teplizumab group (95% CI: 32.1, NE [not estimable]) compared with 24.9 months in the placebo group (95% CI: 9.5, 48.6). The upper 95% confidence interval for the median time to T1D diagnosis analysis was not estimable because of the sample size, and the amount and distribution of censored data. A Cox proportional hazards (PH) model was used to analyze the time to T1D diagnosis, stratified by age and OGTT status at randomization. The results of the primary efficacy endpoint analysis are presented in Table 12. The hazard ratio for the time to clinical T1D diagnosis was 0.41 (95% CI: 0.22 to 0.78) (p=0.0066). TN-10 demonstrated a delay in the onset of stage 3 T1D by a median of 2 years.

The two curves remain essentially parallel for the remainder of follow-up.
L715: ## Sensitivity Analyses
L716: ## *Sensitivity Analyses for Baseline Imbalances*
L717: To address the concern that the imbalance at allocation between treatment groups in autoantibody count at randomization, which has a prognostic potential, may have impacted the results in favor of teplizumab, the FDA statistical reviewer performed an adjusted analysis by adding autoantibody count at randomization into the primary analysis model as a stratification factor. The adjusted analysis estimates the effect of teplizumab after adjusting for the covariate of antibody number at baseline. The hazard ratio from this analysis, 0.44 (95% CI: 0.22 to 0.87, p=0.0175), shown in Table 13, is numerically higher than what was estimated in the primary analysis, but still statistically significant.
L718: Similarly, the FDA statistical reviewer carried out an adjusted analysis by adding BMI at baseline into the primary analysis model as a stratification factor. The hazard ratio, 0.41 (95% CI: 0.21 to 0.78, p=0.006) as shown in Table 13, was similar to what was estimated in the primary analysis, suggesting that BMI did not have a modulating effect on the efficacy outcome.
L719: The FDA statistical reviewer performed an additional analysis by adjusting for both baseline positive autoantibody count and baseline BMI. The resulting hazard ratio was 0.42 (95% CI: 0.21 to 0.84, p=0.0136).
L720: Because the primary analysis model included adjustment for age as a continuous variable as well as age strata (age greater or less than 18), the statistical reviewer did not perform an additional sensitivity analysis for baseline differences in age.

L725: ## *Sensitivity Analyses for Handling of Missing Data*
L726: In TN-10, the amount of missing data was small. At the clinical cutoff, 43 subjects were known to have had T1D onset, 30 patients were T1D-free, and 3 patients (2 in teplizumab and 1 in placebo group) had missing T1D status due to early discontinuation from the study. Three pre-planned sensitivity analyses and one FDA-performed sensitivity analysis with differing methods for imputing missing data (Table 14) produced similar results compared with the primary analysis. The statistical FDA reviewer also examined primary endpoint results by study site for the seven sites with the highest enrollment and found no concerns.

L731: ## Exploratory Analysis of Baseline T1D Progression Risk in Placebo Patients Compared to Natural History Experience
L732: The natural history of the development of T1D has a predictable progression. Therefore, to further enhance confidence in the results of TN-10 we descriptively compared the rate of progression in the placebo arm of TN-10 to what would be expected in untreated individuals at a similar disease stage based on natural history studies.
L733: The risk of progression to T1D observed in the placebo arm of the TN-10 trial was 25% at 6 months, 47% at 2 years, and 69% at 5 years, values that were comparable to data observed in natural history studies; to the extent they differed, progression tended to be more rapid in the natural history studies: 40% at 6 months, 60% at 2 years, and 75% at 4 to 5 years (Sosenko et al. 2009; Krischer 2013; Insel et al. 2015). In contrast, the risk of developing T1D in the teplizumab arm differed greatly, with a 6-month, 2-year, and 5-year risks of 0%, 23%, and 41%, respectively.
L734: ## Data Quality and Integrity
L735: Data quality and integrity were determined to be acceptable. There were several additional analyses conducted by FDA to address data quality including auditing of primary endpoint assessments, traceability assessments, and cross-validation of the submitted documents using the Study Data Tabulation Model (SDTM) and Analysis Data Model (ADaM) datasets, performed by the statistical reviewer.
L736: There were no integrity concerns or financial conflicts of interest.
L737: ## Investigations into Primary Endpoint Ascertainment
L738: TN-10 is notably a small trial. This section discusses additional information that provided confidence in the primary endpoint ascertainment.
L739: First, we verified that there was not a delay between screening (qualifying OGTT) visit and randomization visit, as a difference between treatment arms in time to randomization from screening could bias results. There was a similar mean (32 teplizumab vs. 33 placebo) and median (34 teplizumab vs. 33 placebo) number of days between last screening OGTT and randomization visit, with similar standard deviations (11.7 days).
L740: Next, we confirmed that all T1D diagnoses that were based on OGTT were traced back to OGTT results that confirmed the diagnosis, and that there were no missed OGTT tests that met the diagnostic criteria, but were not counted as T1D events. We also reviewed individual records (patient profiles) for both triggering and confirmatory events for the 10/44 (22.7%) patients for whom the primary endpoint was not determined by OGTT on both instances. Of these patients, 7 had either their triggering or confirmatory event determined by OGTT. Two diagnoses were adjudicated by a committee masked to treatment assignment. On FDA audit of the two patients requiring adjudication, the determinations were considered sufficient. In all but one patient, T1D onset was evident by initiation of insulin therapy or HbA1c &gt;7%. The one patient who was marked as meeting the primary endpoint without clear evidence of disease onset was in the teplizumab arm and was reviewed as a protocol deviation in Section 6.1.2.2.
L741: Additionally, we considered that a differential lag-time between treatment groups in scheduling and conducting the confirmatory test could bias the results. The mean number of days between the triggering event and confirmation event was similar between teplizumab-treated patients (30 days, standard deviation [SD] +/- 22, with range of 8 to 85 days) and placebo (32 days, SD +/- 39, with range of 1-169 days), although the median reflected a shorter time delay between the initial and confirmatory OGTT events for placebo group (15 days) vs. teplizumab (25 days).

L746: We further explored this issue by noting that the majority of patients had their first event meeting diagnostic criteria occur at a scheduled visit (80% teplizumab-treated, vs. 91% placebo), while confirmatory events predominantly occurred on unscheduled visits in both arms: 90% teplizumab vs. 87% placebo. This finding may explain the lack of a 'step' pattern in the K-M curve shown in Figure 6. Notably, the K-M analysis for time to the first of two consecutive positive OGTTs (Table 7a) is similar to the primary K-M (Table 7b, and Figure 6), albeit with a more defined stepwise pattern likely related to the reporting of events at regularly scheduled protocol assessments. Thus, there is no evidence of bias due to differential confirmatory test lag-time between treatment groups.
L747: **Figure 7. K-M Curve Analysis of Time to First of Two Consecutive Positive OGTTs in Months (a) and Time to Diabetes Diagnosis in Months (b)**
L748: Source: FDA Clinical Reviewer generated from ADTTE.xpt
Abbreviations: KM, Kaplan-Meier; OGTT, oral glucose tolerance test; T1D, type 1 diabetes
L749: Finally, the FDA clinical reviewer examined OGTT data to confirm that no TN-10 patients without onset of T1D by the end of the study met criteria for T1D before the study cutoff date.
L750: ## Effects in Subpopulations
L751: This section presents demographic subgroup analyses of TN-10 (age, sex, race/ethnicity) that are required by regulation. These data are then followed by effects in other subgroups of interest (based on disease characteristics).
L752: There were only two patients whose race was not White and four patients who participated at sites outside the US, hence subgroup analyses on race and geographical region were not carried out.
L753: Pediatric patients (8 to 17 years of age) made up the majority (72%) of the TN-10 population. Therefore, we further divided the pediatric patient population into three groups using tertiles
L754: of the study pediatric population age distribution (see Figure 8 for patient counts and distributions among the groups).
L755: In Figure 8, for the overall population and each of the four age subgroups, the estimated hazard ratio of T1D onset and its 95% CI are plotted. All subgroup CIs have substantial overlap with the overall point estimate and indicate a consistency of findings across subgroups. No significant interaction was found between treatment and age subgroups at the 5% level (interaction test, p=0.76).
L756: **Figure 8. TN-10- Subgroup Analysis — Age groups**
L757: Source: FDA statistical reviewer
Abbreviations: CI, confidence interval
L758: The statistical reviewer also performed a running group subgroup analysis to further investigate if the treatment effect of teplizumab (HR) was modulated by patient age. For this analysis, which broke TN-10 pooled study arms into 57 groups by age (with group 1 with the 20 youngest vs. group 57 with the oldest 20 patients), there was no clear directionality (data not shown).
L759: Figure 9 shows the subgroup analysis of treatment effect by sex. Although there was a trend for females experiencing greater treatment benefit than males, there was no significant interaction was found between treatment and sex at the 5% level (interaction test, p=0.24), indicating efficacy in both males and females.

L763: ## Effects in Other Subgroups
L764: The presence or absence of specific human leukocyte antigen (HLA) gene type, islet autoantibody number, and BMI z-score were determined to be subgroups of interest.
L765: In the FDA subgroup analysis by HLA DR4, presence of HLA-DR4 was associated with a greater treatment effect (HR=0.2, 95% [0.09, 0.46], interaction test p-value = 0.006). This was also shown in the Applicant's analysis. The FDA statistical reviewer concluded that although the by-sample analysis is indicative of no treatment benefit in HLA-DR4-absent patients (HR=1.03, 95% [0.29, 3.6]), the shrinkage analysis showed that treatment benefit is established. In FDA's additional subgroup analysis of HLA type groupings, the p-value (0.018) was numerically significant for interaction test, indicating some quantitative differences in treatment effects across the HLA subgroups. However, it is important to note that absence of evidence of treatment effect in HLA DR4 absent TN-10 patients is not evidence of absence, especially in a study this small. The treatment effects for subjects with and without HLA DR3 were similar.
L766: Reviewer comment: The HLA subgroup analyses were performed post hoc with very small subject numbers, and therefore the conclusions that can be drawn from these analyses are limited. However, this information may be useful for assessing the benefit/risk for an individual patient if HLA genotype information is available.
