L782: ## Subgroup Analysis of Anti-Drug Antibody (ADA) Effect on Efficacy
L783: Anti-drug antibodies (ADA) were assessed in TN-10 and were found to be positive in 59% of patients at 3 or 6 months post-randomization. 72% of the ADA-positive patients were younger than 18 years. Immunogenicity as it relates to safety in the teplizumab development program is reviewed in Section 8.4.10. ADA positivity did not have an apparent effect on teplizumab's efficacy (Figure 14).

L787: ## 6.1.2.7 Efficacy Results – Secondary and Other Relevant Endpoints
L788: Published information on the rate of C-peptide decline in those with stage 2 T1D prior to enrollment in TN-10 showed that the pretreatment slope of C-peptide was -0.0233 pmol/mL/month (95% CI -0.0557, 0.0175) (Sims et al. 2021). For TN-10 participants randomized to placebo, the decline in C-peptide persisted, or worsened, during the first 6 months after enrollment, with no significant difference in pre- versus post-treatment slopes, even after correction for baseline age and C-peptide (Sims et al. 2021). These data are similar to the published rate of C-peptide AUC decline among those *newly diagnosed* with T1D. In an analysis of the collective placebo arms of new-onset T1D prevention trials, C-peptide AUC declined from baseline at a rate of -0.0245 pmol/mL/month (95% CI: -0.0271 to -0.0215) for the first 12 months post-diagnosis (Greenbaum et al. 2012).
L789: The FDA clinical reviewer confirmed that prior to randomization in TN-10, the rates of Mean C-peptide AUC decline were similar between treatment arms (Figure 15).
L790: **Figure 15. Exploratory Analysis — C-peptide Mean AUC Rate of Decline Pre-Randomization by Treatment Arm**
L791: Source: FDA Clinical Reviewer
L792: Abbreviations: AUC, area under the curve; OGTT, oral glucose tolerance test
L793: In TN-10, after treatment we would expect further decline in C-peptide in the placebo arm, but an increase in C-peptide AUC in the teplizumab-treated patients if teplizumab is having the intended disease-modifying effect. Figure 16 shows the comparison of C-peptide AUC change from baseline between the treatment groups. With the caveat of the informative censoring described previously (patients did not have C-peptide measurements once T1D was diagnosed) the early C-peptide trends in TN-10 (at 3 months) are suggestive that teplizumab is exerting a salutary effect on C-peptide.
L794: **Figure 16. Change From Baseline in Mean C-peptide AUC by Visit in TN-10 up to 12 Months Post-Randomization**
L795: Error bars indicate standard errors (SEs). The numbers of patients by group at each time are given above the x-axis.
L796: Source: FDA Statistical Reviewer
L797: Abbreviations: AUC, area under the curve; BL, baseline; OGTT, oral glucose tolerance test
L798: ## 6.1.2.8 Dose/Dose Response
L799: 41/44 patients in TN-10 received the intended 14-day course, therefore no dose-response data were available for this study.
L800: ## 6.1.2.9 Durability of Response and Persistence of Effect
L801: On review of the K-M curve, the single 14-day treatment course was observed to primarily exert its effect during the first 12 to 18 months of follow-up, after which the curves for T1D development for treatment and placebo become parallel. The response to a second treatment cycle (e.g., after 12 months) was not tested in TN-10.
L802: ## 6.2. Meta-Analysis of Stage 3 Studies - Supportive Evaluation of Efficacy on C-peptide Data
L803: In pre-submission meetings it was agreed that available C-peptide change from baseline data from teplizumab studies in new-onset T1D could serve as supportive evidence. Therefore, a five-study meta-analysis of the change from baseline in C-peptide AUC was performed with the inclusion of data from AbATE, Delay, Protégé, Encore, and Study 1.
L804: Table 3 summarizes key design features of the five studies, including the dosing regimen of teplizumab and C-peptide assessment schedule. Table 16 summarizes the key inclusion criteria.
L805: **Table 16. Key Inclusion Criteria Across the ISE Studies**
L806: Protégé	Encore	Study 1	AbATE	Delay
Age at entry	8-35 years	8-35 years	7.5-30 years	8-30 years	8-30 years
Time from T1D diagnosis to treatment	≤12 weeks	≤12 weeks	≤6 weeks	≤8 weeks	4-12 months
C-peptide level	detectable	detectable	≥0.2 nmol/L	≥0.2 nmol/L	≥0.2 nmol/L
Weight	≥36 kg	≥36 kg	N/A	≥25 kg	≥27.5 kg
L807: Source: Modified from sponsor's table 3, ISE
L808: Abbreviations: ISE, integrated summary of efficacy; T1D, type 1 diabetes
L809: ## 6.2.1. Individual Trials Included in Meta-Analysis
L810: ## Protégé
L811: The Protégé study was a dose-ranging study designed to assess the safety and efficacy of three teplizumab dosing regimens (a full 14-day course, a one-third dose 14-day course, and a 6-day course) of daily IV infusions compared with placebo (IV) given as two identical courses, 26 weeks apart. The study enrolled patients with newly diagnosed T1D who had experienced disease onset within the past 12 weeks, and patients underwent a 4-hour MMTT every 6 months for 2 years. The primary endpoint for Protégé was the proportion of patients who had both a total daily insulin dose of less than 0.5 units/kg/day and an HbA1c &lt;6.5% at one year after randomization. The study was terminated early due to futility on its primary endpoint.
L812: ## Encore
L813: The Encore study was intended as a replicate study of Protégé. The primary endpoint for Encore was the proportion of patients who had both a total daily insulin dose &lt;0.25 units/kg/day and HbA1c &lt;7.0% at one year after randomization. The Encore study was terminated prematurely with 254 patients (64% of the planned 400 patients) enrolled after Protégé failed to demonstrate efficacy on the primary endpoint.
L814: ## Study 1
L815: Study 1 was a randomized, open-label study to test the safety and efficacy of a single course of teplizumab on the loss of insulin production over 2 years in patients with newly diagnosed T1D (within 6 weeks) compared to standard of care. Patients returned for a 4-hour MMTT every 6 months. The primary endpoint was change from baseline in the mean C-peptide 4-hour AUC in response to an MMTT at Month 24.
L816: ## AbATE
L817: The AbATE study was a randomized, open-label study that enrolled individuals diagnosed with T1D within 8 weeks of randomization. The study drug treatment group received two courses of
L818: teplizumab: at randomization and at 12 months. Patients underwent a 4-hour MMTT every 6 months for 2 years. The parallel group control received standard of care. The primary endpoint was change from baseline in the mean C-peptide 4-hour AUC in response to an MMTT at Month 24.
L819: ## Delay
L820: The Delay study was a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study that enrolled patients 4 to 12 months after their T1D diagnosis. Patients received one course of teplizumab and underwent a 4-hour MMTT every 6 months for 1 year. The primary endpoint was C-peptide AUC in response to an MMTT at Month 12.
L821: ## 6.2.1.1 Study Endpoints
L822: The primary endpoint was the change from baseline in the mean C-peptide AUC. Because C-peptide AUC is typically log-normally distributed, values were transformed to \log(\text{AUC}+1) for analyses. C-peptide has been discussed extensively elsewhere in this review (see Section 2.1 and 6.1.1.6).
L823: Three of the five studies assessed C-peptide for up to 2 years: Protégé, Study 1, and AbATE; the Delay study was designed for only 1 year of follow-up, and Encore was terminated early to provide C-peptide AUC data up to 1 year.
L824: In the MMTTs, C-peptide measurements were taken at baseline, 15 minutes, and then every 30 minutes thereafter up to 4 hours.
L825: *Reviewer comment: The 4-hour MMTT is an acceptable method to assess the primary endpoint. Although both the 2-hour and 4-hour MMTT tests are considered the 'gold standard,' there is evidence that the 4-hour test is associated with less variability (Greenbaum et al. 2008; Boyle et al. 2016).*
L826: Whereas the MMTT is typically used *after* a diagnosis of T1D to assess stimulated C-peptide response, OGTTs (as used in TN-10) are typically used before a diagnosis of T1D in order to monitor for clinical diagnosis, and in a research setting, to measure stimulated C-peptide responses. As discussed in Section 6.1.1.6, 2-hour OGTTs were performed in TN-10 for the assessment of C-peptide AUC as a secondary endpoint. Concordance exists between an individual patient's AUC C-peptide response to 2 hour OGTT and a AUC C-peptide response to a 2 hour MMTT, when tests are performed proximally, as demonstrated in 171 OGTT-MMTT test pairs from 127 patients with T1D (r = 0.96) (Bogun et al. 2020).
L827: Figure 17. Comparison of C-peptide AUC Means from Proximal 2-hour MMTT and 2-hour OGTT Samples After T1D Diagnosis (171 test pairs from 127 subjects)
L828: Source: (Bogun et al. 2020)
L829: Abbreviations: AUC, area under curve; MMTT, mixed meal tolerance test; OGTT, oral glucose tolerance test; T1D, type 1 diabetes
L830: ## 6.2.1.2 Statistical Methodologies
L831: ## Study Selection
L832: Participant-level data from five new- or recent-onset T1D studies were selected to be pooled for the meta-analysis. Study inclusion in the meta-analysis was based on the patient population (newly diagnosed T1D), the study design (i.e., randomized, controlled, or open-label standard of care), and assessment of C-peptide as measured by a 4-hour MMTT.
L833: ## Individual Study Analyses
L834: For each study, after multiple imputation (MI) was used to impute missing values, an analysis of covariance (ANCOVA) was used to estimate the treatment effect. The dependent variable was the change from baseline in \log(\text{AUC}+1) at the last study visit (2 years for Protégé, AbATE, Study 1 in one meta-analysis, and 1 year for all five studies in another meta-analysis). Covariates were treatment, \log(\text{baseline C-peptide AUC}+1), and age.
L835: ## Meta-Analyses
L836: To support the meta-analyses, the original study visits from the individual studies were unified to provide common windows.
L837: By pooling individual participant data from the five completed studies, a one-stage random-effects model that combined and re-analyzed all studies simultaneously was proposed in the agreed-upon SAP. On the basis of the individual study analysis ANCOVA model, the meta-analysis model included two additional terms: a fixed-effect term indicating study identities, and a term for treatment-by-study interaction.
L838: In their original plan for the one-stage meta-analysis, the Applicant proposed a random-effect approach analysis. The Applicant explained, however, that because all five completed randomized studies to date were included in the meta-analysis, the purpose of the analysis was to assess the effects of these specific studies on the outcome, such that fixed effects models would be more appropriate for the meta-analysis. The fixed-effects approach assumes that the between-study variance is zero, which is a strong assumption that rarely holds. The FDA disagreed with this approach and instead carried out a two-stage random effect meta-analysis to estimate the overall treatment effect that accounts for the between-study variances. The two-stage random-effects meta-analysis calculates each individual study weighted average treatment effect estimate separately, and then combines these in a traditional meta-analysis model. The weights were derived by the inverses of the sum of within-study and between-study variance components.
L839: ## Handling of Missing Data and Sensitivity Analysis
L840: The amount, pattern, and reasons for missing data were to be described for each study. Multiple imputation was to be used for the primary analysis. The pattern mixture multiple imputation for missing data was based on two missing data patterns. For patients with missed assessment(s) secondary to administrative reasons, missing data were imputed based on data from completers. For patients with missing data secondary to non-administrative reasons such as AEs, multiple imputation was based on data from retrieved dropouts. Retrieved dropouts are subjects in the same study who discontinued treatment but completed the studies and had C-peptide measured at the scheduled 1- or 2-year timepoints.
L841: After multiple imputation had filled in missing values based on the approach described above, the modified dataset was to be used in the analyses. Rubin's rules for combining data from imputed datasets was used.

L858: ## Demographics and Baseline Characteristics by Individual Study
L859: This section highlights some key differences among studies with respect to demographic and baseline characteristics. In Protégé and Encore patients were, on average, older with a higher proportion of 18- to 35-year-olds (Figure 18). The mean ages for AbATE, Delay and Study 1 (12 to 14 years) were more in line with the age of peak incidence of T1D (Maahs et al. 2010).
L860: Duration since diabetes diagnosis also differed among the trials, in part due to the enrollment criteria listed in Table 3. Most notably, the Delay study enrolled patients four to twelve months from diagnosis. The other studies enrolled patients 6 to 12 weeks from diagnosis. Abate and Study 1 enrolled patients with the shortest duration of T1D (6 to 8 weeks) compared to Protégé and Encore, which enrolled patients up to 12 weeks from diagnosis. Average insulin dose at baseline and baseline HbA1c also differed among the trials.
L861: **Figure 18.** Histograms Demonstrating the Age Distribution and Duration of Diabetes at Time of Randomization for the Studies in the Meta-Analysis
L862: Source: Reviewer generated
L863: The enrollment criteria for baseline C-peptide also differed by study, with Protégé and Encore allowing slightly lower baseline C-peptide levels (detectable) compared with AbATE, Delay and Study 1 (which required \ge 0.2 nmol/L stimulated C-peptide). In addition, the baseline body weight requirement for Protégé and Encore was higher than the other trials. Finally, the country of enrollment differed by study, with 100% of AbATE, Delay and Study 1 completed in the US, and only 27 to 31% of patients enrolled in Protégé and Encore from the US.
L864: Reviewer comment: Heterogeneity was noted in the studies included in the ISE. The most notable heterogeneity was related to duration of T1D, age and country of recruitment, in part related to differences in design among the studies. These baseline factors may help explain the differences in efficacy conclusions noted among the individual studies. This heterogeneity among the trials was considered
L865: *in the establishment of the SAP and the planned random-effects model that incorporates both within-study and between-study variance into the analysis.*
L866: ## 6.2.2.3 Meta-Analysis Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint
L867: Please see Dr. Wang's statistical review for further information about the meta-analysis efficacy results.
L868: Table 19 summarizes mean AUC 4-hour MMTT C-peptide data availability at months 12 and 24. Note that because of the early termination of Encore, only 27.2% of the Encore study population provided month 12 C-peptide data.

L871: Source: The Applicant. FDA statistical reviewer replicated the analysis.
L872: Data from the five studies that included 1-year follow-up data were pooled for analysis of the primary endpoint at 1-year. Additionally, the three studies that included 2-year follow-up data were pooled for the 2-year analysis. For the individual studies, an ANCOVA model included the change from baseline C-peptide \log(\text{AUC}+1) at the 1-year time point or 2-year time point as the dependent variable and baseline C-peptide \log(\text{AUC}+1), age, and treatment as covariates.
L873: Figure 19 displays both individual study analysis results and the meta-analysis results based on imputed data. The 1-year random effect meta-analysis results suggest that teplizumab improves preservation of C-peptide by 0.09 (95% CI: 0.01 to 0.16) on the scale of \log(\text{Mean C-peptide AUC}+1), as compared with control. For results based on observed data please see Dr. Wang's statistical review.
L874: There was notable heterogeneity among the individual study C-peptide results. Study 1 and AbATE observed larger treatment effects than Delay. Protégé and Encore failed to demonstrate a statistically significant difference in C-peptide preservation. To account for this between-study variability, as was previously discussed, the FDA statistical reviewer performed a two-stage random effect meta-analysis to estimate the overall treatment effect. The overall treatment effect by this methodology were similar in size with the one-stage fixed effect model estimates (data not shown); however, the estimated overall treatment effect for the 2-year analysis was
L875: not statistically significant, secondary to widened confidence intervals (0.09; 95% CI: -0.04 to 0.22).
L876: Not shown, the majority of baseline C-peptide values (80%) were in the range of 0.2 to 1.1 nmol/L with mean of 0.6 nmol/L, which is expected for a recent onset T1D population.
L877: **Figure 19. Meta-Analysis: Change From Baseline in Log (Mean C-peptide AUC + 1)**
L878: Source: FDA statistical reviewer.
L879: Abbreviations: AUC, area under the curve; CI, confidence interval; LSM, least squares mean; LCL, lower confidence limit; UCL, upper confidence limit
L880: *Reviewer comment: I agree with Dr. Wang that FDA's evidentiary standard for effectiveness has been met, with confirmatory evidence from the results of the meta-analysis that showed a significant treatment effect of teplizumab vs. comparator on C-peptide AUC change from baseline at 1-year, but not 2-year. The 2-year analysis included significantly fewer patients compared to the 1-year analysis, decreasing statistical power, especially using the two-stage, random-effects model. Importantly, the estimated treatment effect was numerically similar between the 1- and 2-year analyses.*
L881: *It is important to note that the parameter of interest in this analysis is change from baseline in C-peptide, regardless of the baseline value. Although inverse associations between higher stimulated C-peptide and lower HbA1c (Rickels et al. 2020) and rate of severe hypoglycemia (Sørensen et al. 2013a; Gubitosi-Klug et al. 2021) have been reported, there is no minimum change in C-peptide that is considered clinically meaningful.*
L882: ## 6.2.2.4 Meta-Analysis Efficacy Results – Secondary and Other Relevant Endpoints
L883: ## Average Insulin Use and HbA1c
L884: The Applicant submitted supportive data regarding average insulin use and HbA1c from the studies in the meta-analysis. Insulin use and HbA1c must be considered together because higher insulin usage will result in lower HbA1c and vice versa.
L885: In all studies included in the meta-analysis, the average insulin use at each study visit was calculated by averaging documented insulin use over approximately 3 days before the visit in units/kg/day. These analyses were confirmed and replicated by the FDA statistical reviewer, with results by study presented in Figure 20. In all studies except Protégé there is a trend towards lower insulin usage in the teplizumab group.
L886: Figure 20. Average Daily Insulin Use (units/kg/day) by Visit for Individual Meta-Analysis Studies
L887: Source: FDA Clinical Reviewer
L888: Additionally, an FDA exploratory analysis confirmed a negative linear relationship between C-peptide change from baseline and HbA1c change from baseline in each of the studies in the meta-analysis individually (Figure 21) and when pooled (Table 22). This relationship was statistically significant for each study and time period independently. A similar analysis was conducted by the Applicant and presented at the Advisory Committee (but was not submitted as a part of this Application).
L889: Both analyses below were performed with pooled study level data from the teplizumab and comparator arms, 1-year data were available for Protégé, Encore, AbATE and Study 1, and 2-year data for Protégé, AbATE, and Study 1. The Delay study was excluded from this analysis given the patient population had a longer duration of T1D (4 to 12 months).
L890: Figure 21. FDA Exploratory Analysis of Effect of Change From Baseline Mean C-peptide AUC on HbA1c Change From Baseline (%) at 12 months and 24 months by Study for Pooled Teplizumab and Comparator Treatment Arms
L891: Source: FDA Clinical Reviewer
L892: Abbreviations: AUC, area under the curve; HbA1c, hemoglobin A1c; MMTT, mixed meal tolerance test
L893: Reviewer comment: These exploratory analyses strongly support a conclusion that the meta-analysis results of C-peptide change from baseline are predictive of a clinically meaningful effect and provide robust support for the use of C-peptide AUC change from baseline as confirmatory evidence of effectiveness.
L894: ## Effect of Age on Change from Baseline in C-peptide
L895: Figure 22 shows an exploratory analysis of the treatment effect of teplizumab by age sextile for the C-peptide meta-analysis.
L896: **Figure 22. Forest Plot of 1-Year Difference in Change From Baseline C-peptide AUC (nmol/L) by Patient Age Sextile Subgroups – Meta-Analysis**
L897: Source: FDA Statistical Reviewer
L898: Abbreviations: AUC, area under the curve; CL, confidence limit; LSM, least squares mean
L899: Reviewer comment: These data are suggestive of a lack of effect of teplizumab in older age groups; however, this finding was not observed in the TN-10 trial. Therefore, the evidence is insufficient to conclude that the use of teplizumab to delay on the onset of T1D should be limited to younger patients.
L900: ## 7. Integrated Review of Effectiveness
L901: ## 7.1. Assessment of Efficacy Across Trials
L902: The efficacy evaluation for teplizumab is derived from one AWC in the population for teplizumab's intended use, TN-10, in patients at-risk of T1D, and by a meta-analysis in five phase 2/3 studies in new- or recent-onset T1D. Therefore, this section is not applicable to this review.
L903: ## 7.2. Additional Efficacy Considerations
L904: ## 7.2.1. Considerations on Benefit in the Postmarket Setting
L905: Discussions of benefit in the postmarket setting are deferred until the next review cycle because of the proposed Complete Response action for this BLA. The to-be-marketed product should be demonstrated comparable (PK or other method) to the clinical trial product in order to conclude that efficacy in the postmarket setting will be similar to the treatment effect observed in the clinical development program.
L906: ## 7.2.2. Other Relevant Benefits
L907: There are no other relevant benefits that warrant discussion in this section.
L908: ## 7.3. Integrated Assessment of Effectiveness
L909: There is substantial evidence of effectiveness on the basis of which it could fairly and responsibly be concluded that teplizumab will have the effect it purports to have under the conditions of use planned for labeling. Although the Applicant amended their proposed indication during the review cycle, at this time there is agreement on the appropriate indication as: to delay the onset of T1D in at-risk individuals who are \ge 8 years of age who have two or more T1D-related autoantibodies (anti-GAD [glutamic acid decarboxylase], micro insulin, anti-IA-2, islet cell antibody [ICA], anti-ZnT8) and abnormal glucose tolerance on a 2-hour oral glucose tolerance test. Although TN-10 was limited to relatives of patients with T1D, the pathogenesis and risk of progression for stage 2 T1D individuals who are not T1D relatives are not considered to be significantly different from the at-risk population studied in TN-10, and therefore expansion of the treatment indication to include non-relatives is recommended, and was generally supported the Advisory Committee meeting.
L910: The submitted evidence of effectiveness has met the statutory evidentiary standard, consisting of one AWC clinical trial (TN-10) demonstrating efficacy on delay of T1D, and confirmatory evidence consisting of a meta-analysis demonstrating improved C-peptide change from baseline compared to placebo or standard of care in a cohort of patients with new or recent onset T1D.
L911: The primary endpoint in TN-10, delay of T1D, is an objective clinical endpoint directly measuring how a patient feels and functions. TN-10 was an event driven trial and was to be stopped when 40 subjects met the primary endpoint, to detect an effect size measured by a hazard ratio of 0.4 for teplizumab vs. placebo, with 80% power at a one-sided alpha level of 0.025.
L912: A Cox proportional hazards (PH) model was used to analyze the time to T1D diagnosis. The hazard ratio for the time to clinical T1D diagnosis was 0.41 (95% CI: 0.22 to 0.78) with a low p
L913: value of 0.0066. Sensitivity analyses addressing baseline imbalances in important demographic and disease characteristics, as well as sensitivity analyses to address missing data, were consistent with the primary efficacy analysis. No data quality or integrity issues precluding approval were noted.
L914: Consistent with clinical practice, patients in TN-10 T1D were generally diagnosed with objective laboratory-based criteria and the study employed a second confirmatory test that improved the specificity of the primary endpoint. We also performed extensive exploratory analyses of the primary endpoint that further strengthened our confidence in the results. These included excluding bias in the time to confirmatory test from the triggering test, reviewing the individual patient level associated documentation with each clinical diagnosis of T1D, reviewing the DSMB minutes describing the T1D diagnosis for patients not diagnosed with two OGTT tests, and ensuring similar glycemic status at baseline between the two groups, as well as comparing the expected rate of T1D diagnosis in the placebo group to what would be expected based on natural history experience.
L915: Subgroup analyses of the primary endpoint showed some differences in the magnitude of treatment effect among subgroups, but were largely consistent, especially in view of the small sample size. The most notable example was the analysis by HLA type. The subgroup analysis was suggestive of no treatment effect in patients with HLA DR4 absent, but the strength of this finding is insufficient to recommend restricting the indication to patients with HLA DR4 present.
L916: The pre-specified secondary endpoint of C-peptide change from baseline could not be reliably interpreted because of informative censoring, but an exploratory analysis considering only the first 3 and 6 months post-baseline is strongly suggestive of a beneficial effect of teplizumab on C-peptide, and hence beta-cell function.
L917: Limitations to TN-10 include its small sample size (see Advisory Committee discussion), but the trial accrued the pre-specified minimum number of events in order to detect a treatment difference. That 43 events of T1D were accrued with enrollment of only 73 patients speaks to the high-risk nature of the study population. With 73 patients, however, there is a higher chance that baseline imbalances could affect the efficacy conclusions. Indeed, we found a baseline imbalance in the percentage of patients age 18 or older vs. younger patients, among other important imbalances. Also, with a treatment that often has predictable adverse events, e.g., pyrexia and rash, there is a potential for unintentional unblinding that could bias the results. The FDA analyses attempted to address these concerns using the sensitivity and exploratory analyses as noted above, but it remains possible that unknown confounders had an impact on the efficacy assessment. Without a second AWC clinical study, the concern about concluding effectiveness when in fact there is none, is increased. Along the same lines, it is well known that many phase 2 studies that have demonstrated striking treatment effects that were not able to be substantiated in phase 3. There are a number of explanations for this
L918: phenomenon, including chance, i.e., a random 'high.' With the entire treatment effect in TN-10 driven by perhaps a half-dozen subjects, we must acknowledge the concern here, as the TN-10 results could represent a false positive result despite all of the considerations described above. We note, however, that the positive results on the C-peptide meta-analysis provide significant protection from the false-positive, as the findings are fairly strong. Despite these concerns, we believe that the single study is justified because of the rarity of the population of at-risk individuals and the challenge of conducting a second study. Over seven years were needed just to enroll TN-10 (ten years overall). At this time, general population screening for risk of T1D is uncommon outside of research studies.
L919: The confirmatory evidence provided in the BLA was based on effects on the biomarker C-peptide that directly measures endogenous insulin secretion and thus, beta cell function. Prior to the onset of T1D, there is a well characterized steady rate of decline reflective of the autoimmune destruction of the insulin-producing beta cell, in both the at-risk and new-onset T1D populations, who have differing stages of disease along the same continuum. The C-peptide meta-analysis was conducted using a pool of trials in new- or recent-onset T1D, which is a population with more advanced disease than TN-10's population but with similar pathophysiology. As a disease-modifying therapy, teplizumab is expected to improve beta-cell function in patients with T1D, and in patients who do not yet have T1D, delay diagnosis.
L920: Although the meta-analysis was planned post hoc, the similarity of the patient populations, follow-up times, and C-peptide assessments render the meta-analysis valid. The meta-analysis demonstrated a significant treatment effect of teplizumab vs. comparator on C-peptide AUC change from baseline to 1-year after accounting for variations in effects among studies. The FDA analysis demonstrated that teplizumab significantly improved preservation of C-peptide as compared with control at 1-year. The estimated treatment effect at 2-years was similar to the estimate at 1-year but was not statistically significant. The smaller number of patients included in the 2-year analysis reduced statistical power. Given the significant results at 1-year, the meta-analysis was considered confirmatory evidence of efficacy by both clinical and statistical FDA review teams.
L921: Taken together the results of TN-10 and the C-peptide meta-analysis support a conclusion of effectiveness in delay of onset of TN-10 through preservation of beta-cell function, although we cannot conclusively make this connection because of the lack of C-peptide follow-up data in patients diagnosed with T1D in the TN-10 trial.
L922: TN-10 demonstrated a delay in the onset of stage 3 T1D by approximately 2 years. The median time to clinical T1D diagnosis was 49.5 months in the teplizumab group compared with 24.9 months in the placebo group. Our determination that this treatment effect is valued by patients was based on a literature review conducted by the FDA reviewer as well as the Open Public Hearing portion of the Advisory Committee meeting. A delay in the onset of T1D may help
L923: patients avoid diabetic ketoacidosis, which has been associated with impaired cognitive function in newly diagnosed pediatric T1D patients, as compared to age-matched patients with T1D without diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) (Jessup et al. 2015). In addition, a delay in the onset of T1D may reduce the significant disease burden associated with living with T1D on both the patient and caregivers, and result in an improved quality of life (Kalyva et al. 2011). Moreover, an older age at diagnosis may result in improvement in glycemic control, given literature suggesting that HbA1c levels are higher in patients with younger age at onset than in those with later onset (Rawshani et al. 2018). Younger age of onset of T1D (&lt;7 years) has also been associated with worse cognitive function as compared to older age of onset of T1DM (&gt;7 to 17 years) (Ferguson et al. 2005). Although patient preference assessments were not included in this application for review, a recent publication reported that among parents of children with T1D, when told to assume their youngest child would develop T1D within 6 months to 2 years, most (98% of those surveyed) would consider treatments to delay progression and indicated their willingness to accept side effects of a novel treatment for a delay in insulin dependence (DiSantostefano et al. 2020).
L924: If approved, teplizumab would be the first treatment available to delay T1D and the first approved 'disease-modifying' therapy directed at the underlying pathophysiology of autoimmune T1D. Despite the introduction of newer and faster-acting insulin analogues and advances in glucose monitoring and insulin-delivery technologies, many patients with T1D fail to achieve glycemic control (Foster et al. 2019), contributing to the lifetime burden of long-term end-organ complications. Although not demonstrated in the clinical development program, it is not unreasonable to expect that delay of onset of T1D may delay the adverse effects of dysglycemia. Furthermore, there is now evidence that, after controlling for duration of T1D, age of diagnosis has a significant effect on cardiovascular morbidity and mortality (Rawshani et al. 2018).
L925: While acknowledging the clinical meaningfulness of the delay of T1D, some members of the advisory committee noted that the confidence around the estimate of a 2-year delay is low, and we agree that is a reasonable concern. Importantly, however, at the Open Public Hearing we heard discussion that any delay in diagnosis of T1D would be meaningful to patients and families because of the high burden of managing T1D and decreased quality of life. It is thus, not necessary, in our view to conduct a second AWC clinical study to confirm the magnitude of delay.
L926: A given individual patient will ultimately never know whether teplizumab had an impact on the disease course, unless, perhaps, that patient never develops diabetes. This issue is more relevant for a chronic therapy for which a patient is exposed to potential harms from the drug for unclear benefit and must face the decision about whether to accept therapy, and less relevant for a single course treatment such as teplizumab.
L927: Finally, we would be remiss if we did not note that the value of repeated courses of treatment for the delay of T1D with teplizumab is unknown.
L928: ## 8. Review of Safety
L929: ## 8.1. Safety Review Approach
L930: The safety review approach was previously discussed in Section 5.1. The primary safety analysis presented in this review is based on a pooled dataset (ISS) comprised of TN-10 in the at-risk population and four additional studies in patients with new- or recent-onset T1D (AbATE, Delay, Protégé, and Encore) (see Table 3 for an overview of these studies).
L931: ## 8.2. Review of the Safety Database
L932: Total exposure is summarized in Table 20, for both TN-10 and the mISS. The total exposure was agreed upon between the Applicant and division prior to BLA submission.
L933: In TN-10, total observation length was greater for the teplizumab group vs. the placebo group. The estimates of incidences of AEs may therefore be biased against teplizumab. Nonetheless, the majority of AEs occurred early in the observation period, and bias introduced by differential observation time is expected to be minimal.
L934: In the mISS, 729 patients were exposed to at least one dose of teplizumab (not including the 18 patients from Delay exposed during the open label extension), with a total of 641 patient-years of exposure. 213 patients were enrolled in control arms. Time on study was not different between teplizumab and control, and the percentage of completers was high in each group (88.5% for teplizumab and 93% for control).

L943: ## 8.2.1. Relevant Characteristics of the Safety Population
L944: The safety population for TN-10 was identical to the ITT population. Therefore, patient demographics and baseline characteristics for the safety analyses are identical to those presented in Table 9, Table 10, and in Section 6.1.2.3.
L945: The patient demographics of the mISS are generally consistent with the TN-10 population although slightly more diverse with more Asian patients and fewer patients from the US (Table 21). The teplizumab and control groups appear balanced with respect to important demographic variables. There is a lower percentage of teplizumab subjects from the US, but this finding is not expected to have an impact on safety assessment.

L950: ## 8.2.2. Adequacy of the Safety Database
L951: The safety database is adequate with respect to size, dose, duration of treatment, patient demographics, and disease characteristics with reference to the US target population. Although subjects were predominantly white and non-Hispanic/Latino, race/ethnicity is not expected to impact the safety of teplizumab, and the safety database is sufficient to establish safety and efficacy in the intended use population in the US.
L952: ## 8.3. Adequacy of Applicant's Clinical Safety Assessments
L953: In general, the schedule of clinical evaluations that were conducted to assess the safety of teplizumab and the timing of evaluations (Table 6) appeared to be adequate for the evaluation of safety in the teplizumab development program. An important exception is that patients in TN-10, once diagnosed with T1D, were not assessed for safety post-diagnosis. As described previously, the protocol allowed for safety follow-up for a minimum of two years, but it appears
L954: patients did not attend these follow-up visits. In non-TN-10 studies, AEs and SAEs were followed up until events improved/returned to baseline.
L955: ## 8.3.1. Issues Regarding Data Integrity and Submission Quality
L956: The quality of the submission was found to be adequate. Throughout the review, there were several instances where adverse event information and laboratory information were cross-checked with original CRFs for quality and verification. The key safety findings presented in this BLA were reproducible and confirmed using the submitted datasets. In addition, the adverse event dataset verbatim terms were evaluated for adequate matching to preferred terms for both TN-10 and ISS datasets. Three clinical sites were inspected and the quality reports from OSI also support the validity of the clinical data (see Section 4.1 for additional information).
L957: ## 8.3.2. Categorization of Adverse Events
L958: Except where otherwise noted (e.g., cytokine release syndrome analyses), the Applicant used the standard medical dictionary for regulatory activities (MedDRA Version 23.0) to capture and categorize AEs. The grading scale used to assess severity was the National Cancer Institute – Common Terminology Criteria for Adverse Events (NCI-CTCAE) Version 3.0. Grading for studies were unified based on CTCAE version 3.0, where studies with CTCAE grading (TN-10, Protégé, and Encore) remained as coded and grading for other studies (AbATE and Delay) was converted (mild to grade 1, moderate to grade 2, severe to grade 3, SAE to grade 4 and death to grade 5).
L959: The Applicant's translation of verbatim terms to preferred terms and their categorization of preferred terms was acceptable.
L960: Treatment-emergent AEs were defined as adverse events that occurred after the first dose of the investigational product was administered or taken regardless of whether or not the AE was considered drug related. The censoring time was not until the next dose was to be administered (e.g., after 6 to 12 months) or study completion. Although this methodology is appropriate for detection of adverse events that may develop over time, it is not appropriate for AEs that are not likely to occur once exposure to drug is no longer present. Therefore, FDA also performed analyses defining treatment-emergent as events that occurred within 28 days of the last dose of study treatment, and in some cases, the 14-day infusion period.
L961: The Applicant's coding risked diminishing safety signals through splitting of terms; this issue was noted in particular with respect to laboratory abnormality terms (e.g., hemoglobin decreased) and corresponding clinical conditions (e.g., anemia) which were not grouped for analyses. The FDA analyses use queries to address this problem.
L962: Adverse events were assessed by frequency which is appropriate for a single treatment-course therapy.
L963: Adverse events of special interest (EOSI) were pre-specified by the Applicant based on safety concerns identified in other anti-CD3 or T-cell engaging immunotherapies, data from the literature, and preclinical data from the teplizumab development program. The FDA also considered infection, cytokine-release syndrome (CRS), lymphocytopenia, allergic/hypersensitivity reactions, rash, and liver function test abnormalities as EOSIs.
L964: ## 8.3.3. Routine Clinical Tests
L965: Routine clinical testing has been described previously (Table 6) and appeared to be adequate for the evaluation of safety in the teplizumab development program. Because of the frequent use of local laboratories during the studies in the ISS, the Applicant used the New England Journal of Medicine (NEJM) laboratory to generate the normal ranges and upper limit of normal (ULN) in the dataset for adult patients, and the laboratory normal ranges/ULN in Harriet Lane Handbook for Pediatrics 2017 for pediatric patients.
L966: ## 8.4. Safety Results
L967: ## 8.4.1. Deaths
L968: There were three deaths reported in the teplizumab development program. All three deaths were reported for patients with T1D who were randomized to teplizumab, with two deaths occurring during the follow-up period of the Protégé trial and one occurring in the follow-up period of the Protégé Extension Study. Therefore, death was reported in 0.4% of 791 patients randomized to teplizumab (including the 18 Delay subjects randomized to teplizumab in the open label extension) who were included in the safety dataset compared with 0 of 227 patients in the placebo or control group. It should be noted that the observation time was reasonably balanced, with 93% of the teplizumab-treated patients followed for up to 2 years and 39% followed for greater than 2 years, compared with 89% of placebo patients followed up to 2 years, and 41% followed for greater than 2 years.
L969: A 20-year-old female patient with T1D from the Protégé trial died from DKA approximately 9 months after her last dose of teplizumab. This event is discussed further in Section 8.5.6.
L970: A 27-year-old male died of complications related to an anterior wall myocardial infarction 15 months after receiving the last dose of study drug. A 24-year-old female died of unknown causes 26 months after the last dose of study drug. On review of this patient's narrative, an acute episode of abdominal pain, vomiting, and diarrhea treated by a local doctor preceded her death.
L971: *Reviewer comment: There were numerically more deaths reported in the teplizumab-treated patients compared with the comparator group. On review of individual events, it is unlikely these deaths are causally related to teplizumab*
L972: *because of the long latency between dosing and the events. Furthermore, the etiologies were disparate and inconsistent with the mechanism of action of the drug.*
L973: ## 8.4.2. Serious Adverse Events
L974: ## 8.4.2.1 TN-10
L975: All serious adverse events (SAEs) in TN-10 were reviewed individually with review of CRFs, patient narratives and in some cases, patient profiles with individual laboratories. To evaluate serious adverse events, FDA MedDRA queries were used in addition to preferred terms, and terms included in the query for serious adverse events are listed in Table 34 in Appendix B.
L976: In TN-10, (Table 22) seven patients in the teplizumab group (15.9%) and one placebo patient (3.1%) reported SAEs. One patient had two SAEs (dizziness and ankle fracture) in the teplizumab group. All SAEs required hospitalization and occurred after drug treatment was discontinued.
L977: Immunosuppression is a safety issue of interest for teplizumab, and the cluster of four serious infections is notable, although the latency of the onset of two of the events relative to treatment start makes a causal relationship less likely. All resolved without sequelae. One infection was particularly severe and notable. A 15-year-old male presented 40 days after his last dose of teplizumab with severe, complicated, bilateral pneumonia with hypoxemia requiring care in an intensive care unit. The pathogen was not identified. The event was considered resolved after approximately 2 months. The severity of the pneumonia in a previously healthy adolescent suggests a compromised immune response, which may have been drug related. One event of serum sickness is also notable and discussed below. The other SAEs reported in TN-10 appear to be isolated events and are unlikely to be related to teplizumab dosing.

L982: A 12-year-old male was reported to have an SAE of 'serum sickness' 5 days after completion of a 14-day course of teplizumab, based on elevated C-reactive protein, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, and reduced C4 complement. Symptoms included arthralgias and fever, with laboratory data suggesting a recent streptococcal infection. The event resolved after approximately 2.5 months with a waxing and waning course. In retrospect, the patient was determined to have had a positive antinuclear antibody (ANA) prior to study treatment, and this finding raised the possibility that there was an underlying rheumatologic condition that may have been contributory (Karmacharya et al. 2015; Bayer et al. 2019). A causal relationship with teplizumab cannot be excluded. See also Section 8.5.2.

L988: DKA is discussed in Section 8.5.6.
L989: Aside from diabetes complications (DKA, hypoglycemia), the SAEs in the full ISS are qualitatively similar to the non-serious AEs (Section 8.4.5). An exception is that SAEs of infections in the ISS were observed more frequently in teplizumab treated patients, 3.4%, compared with 2% of control patients, consistent with the finding in TN-10. Of interest, an SAE of hepatic amebiasis occurred in a 15-year-old male randomized to teplizumab. At the time of his hospitalization, his lymphocyte count was low in the context of elevated total leukocyte count and neutrophils, normal eosinophils, and elevated blood glucose. The event was considered resolved at the time of hospital discharge. It is possible that immunosuppression from teplizumab treatment contributed to the event.
L990: Other notable SAEs of infection include four infections reported during or soon after teplizumab administration that required anti-infectives and either an ER visit or hospitalization. These were staphylococcal sepsis, anal abscess, epididymitis, and bronchitis. All four events were associated with lymphopenia and reported during the time period when immunosuppression related to teplizumab would be expected.
L991: There were 4 teplizumab-treated patients who were diagnosed with SAEs of viral gastroenteritis, gastritis, or infection of unknown origin, observed within 1 week of teplizumab
L992: dosing, all with presentations that have significant overlap with CRS (fever, nausea, vomiting, +/-rash) without a specific pathogen identified.
L993: SAEs of CRS, which occurred in five patients in the teplizumab group and no patients in the comparator group, are likely drug related. CRS is discussed in detail in Section 8.5.1. Similarly, lymphopenia is known to be caused by teplizumab dosing, and SAEs of lymphopenia were reported by two patients in the teplizumab group and no patients in the comparator group. Please see Section 8.5.3 on lymphopenia. Hepatic enzyme abnormalities may occur as a part of CRS through the effect of cytokines on the liver, an issue that is discussed in more detail in Section 8.5.1. There were two SAEs each of hepatic enzymes increased in the teplizumab group vs. none in the comparator group. As shown in Table 25, transaminase elevations were a commonly reported reason for treatment discontinuation.
L994: Two patients were reported to have SAEs of drug hypersensitivity in the teplizumab group vs. none in the comparator group. One of these events was related to teplizumab, occurring in the context of CRS-like symptoms, and one event was remote to teplizumab dosing (approximately 17 months after dosing) and noted immediately after subcutaneous immunotherapy in the context of allergic disease. There were two serious reports of rash in the teplizumab group vs. none in the comparator group. Neither of these rashes appeared to be consistent with a clinically important event such as Stevens-Johnson syndrome, with one event occurring concurrently with CRS and the other severe rash occurring approximately 11 months after teplizumab dosing, too remote to be drug related. Cases of rash and hypersensitivity are further reviewed in Section 8.5.2.
L995: Not shown in the table, an SAE of 'subclavian vein thrombosis' was reported for a 25-year-old patient, who had a peripherally inserted central catheter (PICC) placed in her left subclavian vein for study drug administration. Although this SAE is not directly caused by teplizumab, it is associated with the procedure for venous access needed to administer the drug.
L996: Most SAEs resolved without sequelae, there were two events reported to have resolved with sequelae, phlegmon in a placebo patient, and biliary dyskinesia in a teplizumab-treated patient which occurred approximately 1 year after the last dose of teplizumab. Any SAEs that were listed as "not recovered/not resolved" were further investigated. One teplizumab-treated patient reported an SAE of sensorineural hearing loss with symptoms noted approximately 100 days after the last study drug and was determined upon neurosensory assessment to have permanent 50% hearing loss bilaterally. This was the only reported hearing-related adverse event reported in the ISS. There were two other SAEs marked as not resolved, uncontrolled hyperglycemia (teplizumab-treated patient with T1D with elevated glucometer readings) and microalbuminuria, reported in a placebo patient.
L997: Some other isolated SAEs warranted further scrutiny, but after review of individual narratives, we determined that most were not reasonably attributed to treatment. The PTs for the SAEs are listed in Table 34.
L998: *Reviewer comment: Infection secondary to immunosuppression is an important safety issue of interest for teplizumab. There were a few infectious events that were temporally related to teplizumab dosing and consequently, temporally related to immunosuppression, with reported low lymphocyte and/or neutrophil counts reported at the time of infection. It is important to note that some of these events could have been CRS rather than infection, as constitutional symptoms and fever have the potential to be confused for infection. All infectious SAEs resolved without sequelae and none were associated with long-term complications.*
L999: *Despite these uncertainties, these data do not justify the Applicant's proposed labeling statement that teplizumab* [REDACTED] (b) (4)
L1000: *Several other SAEs were notably in the same categories as non-serious events, e.g., CRS, lymphopenia etc. lending plausibility to a positive causality assessment.*
L1001: *For other SAEs, there are too few events to draw meaningful conclusions.*
L1002: ## 8.4.3. Dropouts and/or Discontinuations Due to Adverse Effects
L1003: This section discusses AEs that led to discontinuation of teplizumab treatment. According to the teplizumab study protocols, teplizumab dosing was to be fully withdrawn if certain clinical laboratory criteria were met (Table 24). In other words, drug infusions would not be resumed if any of the conditions in Table 24 were met, and the subject would be considered as having met protocol-specified withdrawal criteria.
L1004: *Reviewer comment: The withdrawal criteria were reasonable although perhaps somewhat conservative. There are implications for safety in the postmarket setting because similar withdrawal criteria will need to be applied in the postmarket setting to expect similar safety profiles in patients using teplizumab.*

When looking at AEs leading to withdrawal during the second cycle, two cases were reported in teplizumab-treated subjects (severe neutropenia, infection of unknown origin) compared to no cases in controls.

L1025: *conservative given the data provided. It cannot be determined, however, whether the protocol-specified withdrawal criteria may have prevented more serious or permanent harms from continued dosing. Therefore, similar withdrawal criteria should be used in the postmarket setting. With this approach, it is estimated that approximately 10% of patients will not be able to tolerate the full course of teplizumab therapy.*
L1026: ## 8.4.4. Significant Adverse Events
L1027: Significant adverse events are discussed in Section 8.5.
L1028: ## 8.4.5. Treatment Emergent Adverse Events and Adverse Reactions
L1029: To evaluate 'common' adverse events, FDA MedDRA queries were used in addition to preferred terms, and terms included in the query for 'common' adverse events are listed in Table 35 in Appendix B.
L1030: Table 26 displays the most frequent adverse events occurring in the full ISS pool during the 14-day treatment period. We also evaluated TN-10 and the mISS separately and found similar results (data not shown). Exploratory analyses of time to event for AEs that informed the basis for our review strategy (to evaluate the 14-day treatment period only) are shown in Figure 32 in Appendix B. Nevertheless, when AEs were evaluated over the entire follow-up period, the frequency of reported AEs appeared similar to the analysis shown in Table 26, confirming that most teplizumab-related AEs were limited to the time of exposure (14-days).
L1031: During the treatment period (14-day course) a majority of patients reported at least one AE (&gt;95%). Leukopenia and lymphopenia were the most frequently reported AEs. All reported AEs are expected based on teplizumab's mechanism. Overall, the pattern of AEs observed demonstrate that adverse reactions caused by teplizumab treatment generally consist of low white blood cell count, rash, transaminase elevations, and other constitutional symptoms likely related to CRS (e.g., pyrexia). Transaminase elevations, CRS, rash, and lymphopenia are discussed in more detail in Sections 8.4.6.2, 8.5.1, 8.5.2, and 8.5.3, respectively. Other AEs such as chemistry panel abnormalities are probably drug related. With respect to severity, we examined the CTCAE severity rating for certain adverse events of interest, e.g., thrombocytopenia and hypocalcemia. All were reported as grade 1 or 2. In addition, all reportedly resolved without sequelae.

L1041: ## 8.4.6. Laboratory Findings
L1042: As noted previously, routine laboratory safety assessments included complete blood count (CBC) with differential, coagulation tests, chemistry panels, liver tests, Epstein-Barr Virus (EBV) and cytomegalovirus (CMV) viral loads and serologies, and urine pregnancy tests in females, and are presented in detail in Table 6.
L1043: FDA analyses were conducted for laboratory data related to teplizumab's mechanism of action. Absolute lymphocyte count data was analyzed by trend over time along with neutrophil, platelet, and INR. Lymphocyte data are presented below. The other parameters demonstrated a similar trend with the greatest deviation from normal around Day 5 to 8 with recovery soon
L1044: after. Peak ALT, AST, alkaline phosphatase (ALP) and total bilirubin were evaluated by longitudinal trend and by outlier analysis, based on the degree of elevation above the upper limit of normal (ULN). Important findings were limited to changes in lymphocyte counts and hepatic enzymes/bilirubin which are discussed in the next two sections.
L1045: Other analyses of laboratory data (analyses of central tendency) conducted by the Applicant were unremarkable and are not presented.
L1046: ## 8.4.6.1 Lymphopenia
L1047: Lymphopenia is known to result from drugs that modulate T-cell function. In TN-10, there was a decline in lymphocyte count during the treatment course with nadir at Day 5, which recovered and returned to baseline by Week 6, even while dosing continued (Figure 23).
L1048: **Figure 23. Mean Lymphocyte Count Over Time by Treatment Group for the TN-10 Study**
L1049: Source: Reviewer generated from ad b.xpt dataset, TN-10.
Error bar indicates 1 standard deviation from the mean.
L1050: A similar pattern of lymphocyte decline and rebound was observed, with nadir at Day 5 and recovery at Week 6 in the mISS (data not shown).
L1051: Reviewer comment: An analysis of adverse events with the term lymphopenia is reviewed in Section 8.5.3.
L1052: ## 8.4.6.2 Hepatic Enzyme Elevations
L1053: In the clinical studies, hepatic enzyme and bilirubin concentrations were generally measured daily during infusion periods, frequently for the 6 months (e.g., every 2 to 6 weeks), then every 12 weeks afterwards. The FDA analyzed bilirubin, ALT, and AST by time for the first treatment cycle. The maximum value for each patient and time period was selected (i.e., if a patient had daily labs drawn during the treatment cycle, only the highest value was selected). The data were normalized by the laboratory-reported upper limit of normal, pooled, and plotted to inspect longitudinal trends in ALT (Figure 24), AST, and bilirubin (not shown).
L1054: Figure 24. Observed ALT on a Semi-Log Scale, by Time Period — Full ISS
L1055: Source: Reviewer generated from ad b.xpt dataset.
Abbreviations: ALT, alanine transaminase; ISS, integrated summary of safety; ULN, upper limit of normal
Includes full ISS (TN-10 and supporting studies).
Visits were filtered to include main study visits, and exclude visits following a second cycle of treatment
L1056: ALT was balanced between treatment groups at baseline. A difference in the median and interquartile range (IQR) between treatment arms was observed during the treatment cycle but did not persist beyond 1 to 2 weeks post-treatment. Figure 24 also suggests a difference in the occurrence of ALT &gt;10X ULN for teplizumab-treated patients starting during treatment and persisting beyond 7 to 14 weeks, but there are approximately 3 times more teplizumab-treated patients than controls. Importantly, the observed ALT abnormalities (i.e., ALT &gt;5X ULN and 10X ULN) resolved either by the end of observation, or before the start of a second treatment cycle.
L1057: There were two SAEs each of hepatic enzymes increased in the teplizumab group vs. none in the comparator group. Both patients had normal hepatic enzymes at baseline, and the SAE lead to drug discontinuation. One patient's clinical course was consistent with CRS, with fever noted at the time of AST and ALT elevations (both \ge 4 \times \text{ULN}) following the 12th infusion of teplizumab, without bilirubin elevation. ALT and AST elevations resolved within 3 months without intervention. The other patient with an SAE related to hepatic enzyme elevations was noted to have Peak ALT, AST and total bilirubin were approximately 8 \times \text{ULN}, 6.5 \times \text{ULN}, and &gt; 1.5 \times \text{ULN}. This 10-year-old male patient completed 5 days of teplizumab before asymptomatic hepatic enzyme elevations were noted, leading to hospitalization workup for viral hepatitis was negative, with resolution of hepatic enzyme elevations within 2 weeks of drug discontinuation.
L1058: In addition to the two SAEs, there were two cases, in two patients with bilirubin &gt; 2 \times \text{ULN} concurrent with ALT &gt; 3 \times \text{ULN} which are reviewed in Section 8.5.4.

L1068: ## 8.4.6.4 Chemistry Parameters
L1069: FDA analyses included the assessment of electrolytes (sodium, potassium, and calcium), and markers of renal function (BUN [blood urea nitrogen], Creatinine) during the teplizumab treatment and follow-up period for the pooled studies in the ISS. Transient changes were observed with liver function test parameters including ALT, AST, ALP, and bilirubin, presented in Section 8.4.6.2. Chemistry parameter outliers were low in frequency, and generally balanced between treatment arms. Calcium and albumin were the only chemistry parameters with notable differences between treatment arms during the dosing period for the study. There was a decrease in calcium with nadir day 5 and recovery before the end of the dosing period in teplizumab-exposed subjects (Figure 28). Mean blood albumin levels also decreased in the teplizumab arm during the dosing period, with recovery by week 1 to 2 of follow-up (Figure 29). There were no noted differences between treatment arms for serum sodium, chloride, potassium, creatinine, and bicarbonate during the treatment period (data not shown).

L1074: ## 8.4.7. Vital Signs
L1075: Changes in blood pressure, heart rate and temperature are potential effects of teplizumab treatment because of its capacity to cause CRS. It should be noted that BP measurements were not performed in triplicate or with the use of standardized equipment and the studies were not designed or conducted to rigorously assess blood pressure.
L1076: The Applicant provided a description of the changes in vital signs during treatment with teplizumab and concluded that “no clinically significant differences between treatment groups were observed” (ISS 4.1.1 Vital Signs). Table 27 shows the results of the Applicant’s ‘clinically significant event’ analysis of the ISS vital signs, which were verified by the FDA reviewer.
L1077: Reviewer comment: As shown in Table 27, there are small differences between teplizumab and control in clinically relevant cutoffs for blood pressure, heart rate, and most notably for temperature, so I disagree with the Applicant that “no clinically significant differences between treatment groups were observed”.

L1081: Abbreviations: ISS, integrated summary of safety
L1082: Further blood pressure analyses were requested of the Applicant. For these analyses, hypotension was defined3 as any systolic blood pressure &lt;90 mmHg for children and adults ≥10 years of age and a systolic blood pressure &lt;70 mmHg + (2 x age in years) for children younger than age 10. Overall, the incidence of hypotension during the course 1 treatment period was not different between treatment arms, with 180 of 773 teplizumab-treated patients (23.2%) compared with 56 of 245 control patients (22.9%) with hypotension identified by the analysis.
L1083: The Applicant also conducted an analysis of blood pressure data for the 39 patients who experienced adverse events of CRS. For these patients, the mean maximal drop in SBP was 25 mmHg (range 5 to 55) compared to the mean for the ISS population (17 mm Hg). For the patients with CRS events, hypotension events were noted to be more frequent on day 5 of the 14-day dose cycle.
L1084: Because of experience with the use of OKT3, the first FDA-approved anti-CD3 monoclonal antibody, hypertension and volume overload were of interest for the FDA review of this BLA and additional analyses were requested by the FDA during the review cycle. To identify hypertension in children, the Applicant used a simplified definition of stage 2 hypertension that takes into account the guidelines from the NHLBI Fourth Report, with hypertension in ages younger than 10 years defined as a systolic blood pressure &gt;130 mmHg and for children and adults ≥10 years, a systolic blood pressure &gt;140 mmHg (National High Blood Pressure Education Program Working Group on High Blood Pressure in Children and Adolescents 2004). Results demonstrated slightly more control patients 32/245 (13.1%) than teplizumab-treated patients 92/773 (11.9%) with hypertension events during the first course of teplizumab.
L1085: 3 Adapted definition of hypotension in children provided by the Pediatric Advanced Life Support guidelines (Kleinman et al. 2010)
L1086: *Reviewer comment: Teplizumab does not appear to cause meaningful blood pressure changes in most patients, but for teplizumab-treated patients with events of CRS, there were more dramatic maximal drops in SBP (~25 mmHg) noted, usually on day 5 of teplizumab. As changes in vital signs did not appear to persist beyond the dosing period, labeling mitigation measures such as blood pressure monitoring during infusion should be sufficient. Hypotension and hypertension/volume overload are discussed further as AESIs in Section 8.5.7.*
L1087: ## 8.4.8. Electrocardiograms
L1088: Electrocardiograms (ECGs) abnormalities are not expected for a monoclonal antibody product. Based on the Applicant's summary, there were no clinically significant ECG findings reported in the two studies where ECGs were specifically conducted after teplizumab dosing (Encore and TN-10). In TN-10 triplicate ECGs were performed at baseline and 12-month follow-up visits. In Encore, triplicate ECGs were performed at baseline and on day of dosing, before and 60 minutes after the infusion of teplizumab or placebo. The Applicant concluded that available nonclinical and clinical data demonstrate no evidence of any effect of teplizumab on cardiac repolarization.
L1089: The FDA clinical reviewer reviewed the cardiac adverse events (AESOC "Cardiac Disorders"). There was no signal for an increase in cardiac adverse events. A single death due to cardiac arrest was discussed previously.
L1090: ## 8.4.9. QT
L1091: The Applicant requested a waiver to conduct a thorough QT (TQT) study. Via an email to the Division on July 7, 2020 (documented in DARRTS on June 25, 2021, the interdisciplinary review team for QT agreed with the request to waive a TQT study. They stated, "large targeted proteins and monoclonal antibodies have a low likelihood of direct ion channel interactions and a thorough QT/QTc study is not necessary, unless the potential for proarrhythmic risk is suggested by mechanistic considerations or data from clinical or non-clinical studies. (Refer to ICH E14 Q&amp;A (R3), section 6.3)."
L1092: ## 8.4.10. Immunogenicity
L1093: Anti-drug antibodies (ADA) were identified in 50 to 92% of patients in Protégé, Encore and TN-10, during the first course of teplizumab across the various treatment regimens, and the incidence of ADA was as high as 90% when a second course was administered.
L1094: Type III hypersensitivity reactions, like serum sickness or vasculitis, are observed with monoclonal antibody therapies and are mediated by the formation of antigen-antibody complexes. Depending on the form, clinical manifestations may include fever, rash, arthralgias,
L1095: glomerulonephritis and lymphadenopathy. Timing of serum sickness is generally is delayed, occurring weeks after the start of treatment, because the development of anti-drug antibodies precedes clinical manifestations. In addition, onset may be noted upon re-exposure to drug.
L1096: The Applicant performed an analysis of risk of serum sickness and vasculitis in patients who were either ADA antibody positive or negative, in Protégé (Table 30), Encore and TN-10 (Table 31). The Applicant reported that rash occurred more frequently in patients treated with teplizumab than with placebo or control comparator, but a correlation with development of ADA was not consistent across trials: ADA positivity did appear to increase the risk of AEs related to skin and subcutaneous disorders (rash) among ADA positive patients in Protégé in the first but not the second treatment cycles. In TN-10 there was a single case of serum sickness in an ADA positive patient, and there was a slightly higher frequency of rash in ADA positive patients.

L1104: *Reviewer comment: The data support the conclusions that teplizumab is immunogenic, potentially with clinical consequences of a greater risk of rash.*
L1105: ## 8.5. Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues
L1106: ## 8.5.1. Cytokine Release Syndrome
L1107: CRS was identified as an AESI (adverse event of special interest) for teplizumab because of its mechanism of action and given that CRS was frequently observed with OKT3, teplizumab's non-humanized precursor. In addition, nonclinical data from chimpanzees dosed with teplizumab demonstrated dose-dependent increases in circulating cytokines (TNF-\alpha, IL-2, IL-6, IFN-\gamma) with peak levels 6 hours post-dose. T-cell agonists, including agonistic anti-CD3 antibodies like OKT3 and teplizumab, activate T-cells directly with subsequent secretion of cytokines that may cascade to other inflammatory cells. In addition, antibodies with an intact Fc receptor may activate monocytes and macrophages, resulting in cytokine production. Teplizumab, in contrast to OKT3, has been humanized in order to minimize Fc receptor binding, and thus there is a reduced risk of immune cell activation.
L1108: Symptoms of CRS include pyrexia, malaise, rash, headache, fatigue, arthralgias, myalgias, and nausea. Severe cases may include respiratory failure, hypotension requiring vasopressors, cardiac dysfunction, renal failure, elevated transaminases, and disseminated intravascular coagulation and multi-organ failure. Clinical signs and symptoms of CRS have been observed within minutes or hours of the first dose, and up to 14 days after infusion
L1109: Relevant cytokines include TNF-\alpha, IL-2, IL-6, and IFN-\gamma. In the teplizumab development program, patients with new-onset T1D were observed to have peak serum TNF-\alpha and IL-6 concentrations, following the second dose of teplizumab. Please see the consult from DHM1 for an additional discussion of CRS.
L1110: For purposes of the evaluation of CRS, the Applicant used the National Cancer Institute Common Terminology Criteria for Adverse Events (CTCAE) version 3.0 CRS grading system. This grading method is based on clinical symptoms, the need for intervention (yes/no), and the intensity of intervention.
L1111: An overview of the CTCAE grading of CRS is presented in Table 30. For additional details please refer to Table 36 in Appendix B.
L1112: **Table 30. CTCAE Version 3.0 CRS Grading System**
L1113: Grading System	Grade 1	Grade 2	Grade 3	Grade 4
CTCAE version 3.0	Mild reaction; infusion interruption not indicated; intervention not indicated	Therapy or infusion interruption indicated but responds promptly to symptomatic treatment (antihistamines, NSAIDs, narcotics, IV fluids); prophylactic medications indicated for \le24 hours	Prolonged (e.g., not rapidly responsive to symptomatic medication and/or brief interruption of infusion); recurrence of symptoms following initial improvement; hospitalization indicated for clinical sequelae	Life-threatening consequences; pressor or ventilatory support indicated
L1114: Source: Cancer Therapy Evaluation Program, Common Terminology Criteria for Adverse Events, Version 3.0
Abbreviations: CRS, cytokine release syndrome; CTCAE, National Cancer Institute Common Terminology Criteria for Adverse Events; NSAIDs, nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs; IV, intravenous
L1115: In the ISS, there were 41 CRS cases reported during the first treatment cycle: 39 in the teplizumab group and 2 in the control group (Table 31). The majority (82%) of CRS cases were grade 1 and 2. The remaining 7 cases were grade 3, and 5/7 of the grade 3 cases were also reported as SAEs. An excess of 4.2 cases (95% CI: 2.3 to 6.1) of any grade of CRS was observed per 100 patients treated. The excess cases were primarily grades 1 and 2, with close to 1 excess case of grade 3 CRS per 100 patients.
L1116: Among the cases in the teplizumab group, ten resulted in treatment interruption or permanent treatment discontinuation. All patients with grade 3 events discontinued treatment early with a median treatment duration of 4.5 days. The median duration of the CRS event was 3 days and started 3 days into the treatment cycle. All 7 of the CRS grade 3 events occurred within 6 days of initiating the first course of teplizumab and had a median duration of 5 days.
L1117: Characteristics of the patients experiencing CRS were broadly consistent with the overall treatment population, although slightly older patients and more males reported CRS: the majority of cases of CRS were reported in males (30/41 [73.2%]). The median age of patients with CRS was also older (19 years) than the median age of the ISS population (15 years).
L1118: Of note, the identification of two cases of CRS in the control group highlights the limitations in the specificity of the CTCAE grading method as control patients should not have true CRS.

L1123: Includes both TN-10 and studies in the mISS. Only includes events occurring before initiation of a second treatment period.
L1124: Increased liver transaminases have been associated with CRS and are likely related to cytokine effects on the liver. Concomitant liver transaminase elevations were observed in 22/39 (56%) of teplizumab-treated patients experiencing CRS. The majority (64%) of liver transaminase elevations among these patients were CTCAE grade 1 (1-2.5x ULN), 32% were grade 2 (&gt;2.5-5.0x ULN), and 4.5% were grade 3 (&gt;5-10x ULN). All events of liver transaminase elevations &gt;2.5x ULN occurred in patients with grade 2 and 3 CRS events, suggesting a correlation with the severity of CRS.
L1125: CRS requiring intervention other than antipyretics was rare. There were two hospitalizations for Grade 2 CRS and one hospitalization for Grade 3 CRS. There were no fatal outcomes or ICU admissions. No patient required a vasopressor for symptoms of hypotension or experienced significant hypoxia. There were no serious morbidities associated with CRS on review of the CRS specific report forms as well as AE reports.
L1126: ## 8.5.1.1 Additional Cytokine Release Syndrome Analyses
L1127: The understanding of drug-induced CRS has improved with the expansion of use of therapeutics that activate T-cells, and newer CRS grading systems, such as the American Society for Transplantation and Cellular Therapy (ASTCT) criteria, have been developed to address limitations of older grading systems. In newer grading methods, fever (\ge 38.0^{\circ}\text{C}) is a requisite for a diagnosis of drug-related CRS.
L1128: Compared with the CTCAE method, the analysis using the ASTCT criteria showed an overall higher absolute risk of CRS in both teplizumab-treated and placebo groups, but a lower relative risk of CRS from teplizumab vs. comparator (Table 32). The majority of cases were Grade 1, which is associated with the presence of fever alone. In control patients, however, 10.2% also were classified as having Grade 1 CRS suggesting low specificity of this method. Of the 103 patients with Grade 1 CRS in the teplizumab treatment group, 10 required dose interruption. Grade 2 CRS events were infrequent. As a reminder, no patient required a vasopressor for symptoms of hypotension or experienced significant hypoxia.
L1129: Concomitant lymphopenia (defined as below the lower limit of normal) was also observed in the majority (88%) of patients with CRS events. However, the lymphopenia in the context of CRS was not more severe than that observed in the overall population.
