L1135: Reviewer comment: Both the CTCAE and ASTCT methods of CRS grading showed an increased risk of CRS with teplizumab treatment vs. controls. This finding, in addition to the mechanism of action of teplizumab and the known risk of CRS with OKT3, is strongly suggestive of a causal relationship between teplizumab and CRS. The majority of events of CRS were considered mild, although some led to treatment discontinuation (about 1/3 using the CTCAE grading system). No patient experienced irreparable harm, however. Therefore, at this time the risk of CRS seems appropriate for labeling in the Warnings and Precautions section of the Prescribing Information. Additionally, CRS should be included as a safety issue of interest in postmarketing safety study(s) to further characterize the risk.
L1136: Mitigation steps used in the protocols to reduce the risk and/or symptoms of CRS included ramp-up dosing, pretreatment with ibuprofen, or similar non-steroidal anti-inflammatory (NSAID) and antihistamine. The same premedications should be continued in the post-marketing period and be recommended in section 2 of the Prescribing Information, if approved. Given the low incidence of severe CRS, few patients requiring hospitalization, and no patients requiring escalation of care, the DHM1 consultants did not recommend additional premedication, e.g., corticosteroids as prophylaxis in this population.
L1137: ## 8.5.2. Hypersensitivity/ Rash
L1138: Anaphylaxis and serum sickness or vasculitis are two major acute infusion reactions that can be caused by monoclonal antibody therapies. These adverse reactions are less common with humanized monoclonal antibodies, but remain a concern (Isabwe et al. 2018). Patients with underlying autoimmune diseases, e.g., T1D, appear to be at a higher risk of drug hypersensitivity reactions than the general population (Karmacharya et al. 2015; Isabwe et al. 2018; Bayer et al. 2019). The Applicant identified hypersensitivity reactions as an EOSI. FDA conducted a standard MedDRA query for hypersensitivity-related AEs occurring during or following the first treatment cycle.
L1139: Our review revealed a low frequency of hypersensitivity AEs (with preferred terms “hypersensitivity” and “drug hypersensitivity”) 7/773 (0.9%) in the teplizumab group, vs. 2/245 (0.8%) in the control group. Two of the cases of hypersensitivity were reported as SAEs. One was likely related to study drug treatment: a 17-year-old male patient developed generalized cutaneous rash, fever, chills, nausea, and vomiting within 3-4 hours of teplizumab administration. The patient was hospitalized and treated with acetaminophen, loratadine, and IV hydrocortisone and discharged from the hospital the following day. Around the time of hospitalization, this patient also experienced laboratory abnormalities including low lymphocytes, neutrophils, platelets. Therefore, it is important to note that the clinical manifestations and timing of onset of this event could also be indicative of CRS. The other reported SAE associated with hypersensitivity occurred in a 26-year-old male approximately 204 days after the last dose of teplizumab and was reported immediately after receiving immunotherapy for allergic rhinitis. This event is unlikely to be related to drug administration.
L1140: There were two cases of angioedema (swelling face, periorbital edema) among teplizumab-treated patients and none among controls. No anaphylaxis was reported, but there was an SAE which appears consistent with a serious allergic reaction: the event was reported as grade 3 CRS by the investigator. The event led to drug discontinuation after acute onset of symptoms of fever, nausea, vomiting, rash, facial swelling, urticaria, and bronchospasm following the fourth infusion of study drug.
L1141: As previously discussed, rash was commonly reported. During the 14-day treatment period, 11 (1.4%) teplizumab-treated patients and 1 (0.1%) control patient experienced urticaria. The majority of rashes observed with teplizumab treatment were not serious and resolved without intervention. One event of rash was reported to be serious and appeared to be partly allergic in nature, associated with urticaria; the event resolved after 4 days despite continued teplizumab dosing.
L1142: *Reviewer comment: Allergic reactions and drug hypersensitivity appear to be a potential risk of teplizumab therapy and should be included in the product labeling in Warnings and Precautions.*
L1143: *Rash is clearly drug-related, although seems to have multiple causal pathways including CRS, ADA positivity, allergic reactions, and other mechanisms of drug-hypersensitivity. There is no evidence, however, of serious rash with sequelae.*
L1144: ## 8.5.3. Lymphopenia
L1145: Lymphopenia is known to result from drugs that modulate T-cell function. Therefore, we examined changes in lymphocyte count using both laboratory data, presented in Section 8.4.6.1, and via AE reporting with a focus on serious AEs of lymphopenia.
L1146: Lymphopenia was the most frequently reported AE during the study, observed in 72.7% of the teplizumab treatment group and 6.3% of the placebo group with a risk difference of 65.6% (95 CI: 49.3 to 81.9). Overall, 84% were grade 2 or grade 3 lymphopenia. As shown in the laboratory analysis section, in TN-10, lymphocyte count nadir occurred at Day 5, with recovery and return to baseline by Week 6, with most returning to baseline before the end of the treatment period, even while dosing continued (Figure 23). Outlier analysis for lymphocyte data for ISS (including TN-10) revealed no cases of persistent severe lymphopenia.
L1147: Two events in the ISS were reported as SAEs, one of which appears to be based on the degree of lymphocyte nadir (0.15 \times 10^3 cells/\muL on Day 8 of teplizumab infusion). The other was associated with an infection (gastritis); both resolved without sequelae.
L1148: *Reviewer comment: Overall, events of lymphopenia were predictable and transient. Besides the isolated SAE narratives above, no lymphopenia adverse events were serious. In addition to lymphopenia SAEs, any infection-related SAEs were reviewed for association with lymphopenia and are discussed in Section 8.4.2.*
L1149: ## 8.5.4. Evaluation of Potential Drug Induced Liver Injury
L1150: Maximum treatment-emergent values for all patients (full ISS) during the entire treatment and follow-up period were calculated for ALT and bilirubin and paired with each other if both laboratories were collected within 30 days (Figure 30). There were only two cases, in two patients with bilirubin &gt;2 \times ULN concurrent with ALT &gt;3 \times ULN. Both cases were determined to not be related to drug-induced liver injury and had alternative explanations (CRS and Hepatitis A).

L1155: ## 8.5.5. Infections
L1156: ## 8.5.5.1 General Infections
L1157: As noted previously, infection secondary to immunosuppression is an important safety issue of interest for teplizumab. In the full ISS, during the period of the 14-day teplizumab infusion, 38 patients (4.9%) reported any infection in the teplizumab group compared to 12 (4.9%) in the control group. During the first treatment cycle and follow-up period, 581 infections were reported by 309 patients (40.0%) in the teplizumab group compared to 17 infections among 99 patients (40.4%) in the control group.
L1158: As discussed previously, however, we found a higher incidence of serious infections in the teplizumab group compared to the control group. Additionally, the Applicant pre-specified CTCAE grade \ge 3 infections (i.e., those requiring IV anti-infectives, invasive intervention, hospitalization, and/or resulting in death) as an EOSI. Such infections were reported by 3.0% of teplizumab patients and 2.0% of controls over all observation periods.
L1159: Reviewer comment: The reason for the similar frequency of general infections, but disproportionate frequency of serious infections, is unclear, but could be
L1160: related to misdiagnoses of CRS events as infections. Given the uncertainty it seems prudent to conservatively label the product and include a Warning and Precaution for a potential risk of serious infection.
L1161: ## 8.5.5.2 Epstein-Barr Virus Reactivation
L1162: Reactivation of Epstein-Barr virus (EBV) has been observed with other immunotherapies affecting T-cell function. Cytotoxic (CD8+) T-cell exhaustion after teplizumab exposure is correlated with EBV reactivation (Long et al. 2016), and transient dose-related EBV reactivation has been observed in clinical studies with another anti-CD3 monoclonal antibody, otelixizumab (Keymeulen et al. 2021).
L1163: The Applicant conducted an exploratory MedDRA query for EBV4 and adjudicated each case as 'new infection' or 'reactivation' based on review of baseline status of seropositivity, AE listings, and clinical laboratory values. The adjudication of events was confirmed by the FDA clinical reviewer based on blinded review of the data.
L1164: At baseline, 76.6% of teplizumab patients and 72.3% of controls were noted to have evidence of prior EBV infection (as assessed by presence of EBV IgG antibody). The adjudication demonstrated a higher rate of EBV reactivation among teplizumab patients (4.5%) than controls (2.3%). Teplizumab treatment, however, did not appear to increase the risk of primary EBV infection, with slightly higher rates of new EBV infection in the control group (3.3%) compared with the teplizumab group (2.5%). Of the cases of EBV reactivation in the teplizumab group, 58% occurred within 30 days of teplizumab dosing.
L1165: ## 8.5.5.3 Epstein-Barr Viremia and Lymphoproliferative Disorders
L1166: EBV-associated lymphoproliferative disorder (LPD) has been observed to be caused by drugs that suppress T-cell immune function. LPD is most commonly observed in the year following initiation of immunosuppressive therapies in solid organ transplant recipients (Green and Michaels 2013). EBV-associated LPD is more common in initially seronegative individuals who undergo organ transplantation and subsequently acquire a primary EBV infection (Walker et al. 1995; Nijland et al. 2016). In addition, chimpanzees dosed with teplizumab at 10 mg/kg (approximately 40x the human dose proposed) died secondary to non-neoplastic B-cell LPD resulting from T-cell immunosuppression and infection with an EBV-like virus.
L1167: 4 PTs included Epstein-Barr viraemia, Epstein-Barr virus test positive, Epstein-Barr virus antibody positive, Epstein-Barr virus antigen positive, Epstein-Barr virus infection, Epstein-Barr virus infection reactivation, mononucleosis syndrome and infectious mononucleosis.
L1168: There were no events of LPD reported in the ISS population of patients with recent-onset T1D, or at-risk for T1D. We are aware of a single case of EBV-associated nonmalignant LPD noted in an islet cell transplant recipient dosed with teplizumab along with other immunotherapies (tacrolimus, sirolimus and etanercept). The confounding of chronic immunosuppressive therapies makes it unclear if teplizumab contributed to this event.
L1169: Higher peak quantitative EBV viral DNA titer is correlated with higher rates of post-transplant LPD (Green and Michaels 2013).
L1170: In the TN-10 study, EBV viral DNA was collected on Day 20, Week 6, and Months 3, 12, 24, 36, and 48. Nine patients in the teplizumab treatment group experienced increases in EBV viral DNA &gt;3,000 copies/mL, a threshold selected by the Applicant, compared to none in the placebo group. All nine patients were noted to have increased EBV DNA titers at either the 20-day or 3-month visit after teplizumab dosing, with peak titer ranging from 3,045 to 32,325 copies/mL. A peak EBV viral DNA &gt;1,000,000 copies/mL was noted in a single patient at Day 31, with resolution to &lt;500 copies/mL 84 days later. Notably, all patients experienced a reduction in EBV viral load &lt;1,000 copies/mL within 3 months postdosing, with continued reductions over time. One patient with EBV titers &gt;3,000 copies/mL presented with pharyngitis, whereas the others were asymptomatic. The time course of EBV viral DNA increases observed in TN-10 mirrors the timeline of EBV viral reactivation observed in published T1D trials featuring the anti-CD3 monoclonal antibody, otelixizumab, where peak EBV titers were noted at Day 21 post-dose with subsequent recovery (Keymeulen et al. 2021).
L1171: *Reviewer comment: It is reassuring that no events of LPD were reported in the ISS, despite follow-up times as long as seven years. Nevertheless, LPD remains a potential risk because of evidence of EBV viremia in some teplizumab-treated patients and the possible long-latency of LPD. In addition to labeling recommendations to prevent patients with active EBV or CMV infections from being treated with teplizumab, I recommend study of LPD in the postmarketing setting (via enhanced pharmacovigilance or an exposure registry). Further discussion of this issue is deferred to the next review cycle.*
L1172: ## 8.5.5.4 Other Notable Infections
L1173: Cytomegalovirus (CMV) is another common virus for which reactivation events are observed more commonly in immunocompromised hosts. There were very few adverse events of CMV infection within the mISS population, with 9/729 (1.2%) teplizumab-treated patients and 2/213 (0.9%) controls with 'Cytomegalovirus infection' or 'Cytomegalovirus test positive' events.
L1174: Herpes infections, which included the terms 'oral herpes' and 'herpes simplex', occurred in 7 of 773 teplizumab-treated patients (0.9%) and in 1 control patient (0.4%). VZV infections, which
L1175: were reported as 'varicella', 'herpes zoster', and 'varicella zoster virus infection' occurred in 9 teplizumab-treated patients (1.2%) and 1 control patient (0.4%).
L1176: Also, of interest, cases of tuberculosis were balanced between study arms with a single case of pulmonary tuberculosis, reported as an SAE in a teplizumab-treated patient approximately 151 days after treatment completion, and a SAE of disseminated pulmonary tuberculosis in a placebo patient 75 days after dosing.
L1177: *Reviewer comment: Comparative rates of CMV, herpes virus, and VZV are all in favor of placebo (higher frequency in teplizumab) but absolute incidence is very small.*
L1178: ## 8.5.6. Diabetic Ketoacidosis
L1179: As discussed in Section 2.1, DKA is a substantial risk in children and adolescents with T1D and is caused by relative insulin insufficiency to metabolic need.
L1180: Importantly, there were no events of DKA in TN-10, in patients developing overt T1D, possibly reflecting the close follow-up of patients. All events of DKA occurred in the new- or recent-onset T1D trials. In the teplizumab development program, events of metabolic acidosis and DKA were identified by investigator-reported AEs that included PTs suggestive of possible DKA: Diabetic Ketoacidosis, Diabetic ketosis, Ketosis, and Ketoacidosis. The FDA analysis included assessment of the mISS pool during the entire follow-up period (including course 2 and follow-up period for patients in Protégé, Encore and AbATE given a second treatment course), and limited to course 1 and the course 1 follow-up period.
L1181: There was one fatal DKA event. On review of the DKA event, there are several features that lead to the determination that it was unlikely related to study drug. The DKA event occurred 9 months after the last dose of teplizumab, in a patient who had 6 DKA events preceding the event, including DKA prior to treatment with teplizumab. An insulin dose reduction from ~0.5 units/kg/day to approximately ½ the dose, 0.2 units/kg/day was observed prior to the onset of DKA episodes. Records from the first two DKA hospitalizations indicate disturbance of diet and insulin therapy were precipitating factors for DKA events, and substantial insulin dose increases were noted after the second hospitalization.
L1182: Over the entire duration of the follow-up period (course 1 and course 2), there were 32 episodes of DKA among 22 patients with a higher rate of DKA among teplizumab-treated patients 20/729 (2.7%) than among control patients 2/213 (0.9%). One DKA event in the teplizumab group was fatal as previously discussed.
L1183: The mean average time to event for the event analysis was 284.7 days after the last dose of teplizumab for the teplizumab-treated patients. The risk of DKA did not appear to be dose dependent (data not shown).
L1184: A detailed review of the narratives of DKA events among patients who were teplizumab-treated (Table 37) suggested that in many cases (30%), insulin dose was lowered prior to the DKA event; relative insulin insufficiency is a well-known cause of the development of DKA. For the two comparator patients, no insulin dose reduction was reported.
L1185: On further review of the narratives, we could identify in 80% of DKA cases a potential cause, i.e., a prior illness immediately preceding the DKA event, such as a viral upper respiratory illness, UTI, pyelonephritis, pneumonia. In one case, dengue fever was determined to be the preceding illness. Pump malfunction and investigator reported non-compliance were additional noted associated factors. Two patients also experienced DKA between diagnosis of T1D and screening for the study.
L1186: **Table 33. Factors Associated With DKA Events — mISS**

L1189: Abbreviations: DKA, diabetic ketoacidosis; ISS, integrated summary of safety; mISS, modified ISS
L1190: The total number of events (N=20) includes DKA events in the teplizumab arm
L1191: *Reviewer comment: There was a large difference in events of DKA not favoring the teplizumab group (20 vs. 2) among patients with T1D in the mISS population. This large of a difference is unlikely to be play of chance, but there is no clear mechanism how teplizumab could cause DKA. Teplizumab per se, may not be the cause, however, given that insulin dosing was disproportionately lowered for patients in the teplizumab group. The reason for this is unclear but could be related to inadvertent unblinding of treatment groups, especially because investigators had access to lymphocyte count monitoring information. In light of this uncertainty, DKA should be an adverse event of interest in postmarketing safety evaluations.*
L1192: ## 8.5.7. Hypotension and Hypertension/Volume Overload
L1193: As discussed previously, hypotension is a symptom of CRS. In addition, hypertension/volume overload was a safety concern for OKT3, teplizumab's non-humanized precursor.
L1194: We analyzed adverse events related to hypotension, including the following PTs: 'blood pressure decreased', 'diastolic blood pressure decreased', 'hypotension', 'orthostatic hypotension' in the ISS population. There were 30/773 (3.9%) teplizumab-treated vs. 8/245 (3.2%) control participants reporting hypotension-related terms during the first treatment cycle including the follow-up period. When this analysis was limited to the 28 days following any dose of study drug (the more appropriate analysis for this AE), the difference in frequency was more apparent: 20/773 (2.5%) among teplizumab-treated patients and 4/245 (1.6%) among controls. Importantly, however, there was no difference between groups in the frequency of syncope or presyncope.
L1195: An FDA MedDRA query was performed for events related to volume overload. Overall, there were more teplizumab patients 8/747 (1.1%) with MedDRA events related to volume overload (edema, non-pulmonary edema, fluid retention, overload) within 28 days of the last dose of study drug in comparison to 0 of the control.
L1196: *Reviewer comment: AEs related to hypotension and volume overload were slightly more frequent among teplizumab-treated patients, but absolute incidence was low. These findings are consistent with the blood pressure analyses in Section 8.4.7.*
L1197: ## 8.6. Safety Analyses by Demographic Subgroups
L1198: Safety analyses by demographic subgroup utilizing the placebo-controlled pool were performed to evaluate whether significant adverse events (cytokine release syndrome, infections, cytopenias, hepatic enzyme elevations) differed importantly among subgroups.
L1199: Overall, the incidence of AEs in the full ISS (TN-10 and the supporting studies) were similar by quartiles of age (Figure 31). No treatment by age group interactions were observed.
L1200: Also shown in Figure 31, there were no differences in safety findings among subgroups of sex (male and female) and race (White, Asian, Other). Note that the data were primarily from White patients, however, limiting conclusions.

L1205: ## 8.7. Specific Safety Studies/Clinical Trials
L1206: No other specific safety studies were conducted.
L1207: ## 8.8. Additional Safety Explorations
L1208: ## 8.8.1. Human Carcinogenicity or Tumor Development
L1209: It is important to note that the clinical safety data submitted in the BLA are not sufficient to evaluate the potential risk of malignancy because of the short duration of follow-up as well as the relatively young age of the safety population. The longest trial duration available in publication is the 7-year follow-up of the AbATE trial (n=43), and this trial reported no malignancy among the 43 patients treated with two courses of teplizumab (given 1 year apart). In the ISS population, one malignancy was reported: a teplizumab-treated 21-year-old male was diagnosed with melanoma of the scalp with metastases to regional lymph nodes (supraclavicular and suprascapular). The patient had a history of dysplastic nevi and a pre-existing lesion that was not noted until after study entry. The affected tissue and lymph node metastases were excised, and no recurrence has been reported. Although it is extremely unlikely that teplizumab caused the malignancy, a contribution of teplizumab to a more rapid metastasis cannot be excluded.
L1210: Immunosuppressive therapies carry a theoretical concern for malignancies, as immunosuppression is known to impact anti-oncogenic immune activity (Wu and Shapiro 2011). OKT3, the progenitor mAb to teplizumab, has a labeled risk for post-transplant lymphoproliferative disorders, sometimes fatal. For OKT3, malignant lymphomas appear to develop early after transplantation, the majority within the first four months post-treatment (Swinnen et al. 1990). Therefore, this concern may be more relevant to chronic therapies using higher doses, such as in the setting of transplant rejection, than to therapies consisting of a single course of treatment.
L1211: Reactivation (1 to 4 months post-transplant) of EBV and CMV, and subsequent lymphoproliferative disorder (LPD) is a labeled risk for OKT3 therapy. There were observed EBV reactivations in the teplizumab development program. Most EBV reactivation events were reported early after treatment with teplizumab, with a minority of cases occurring more than 30 days after the end of treatment. However, no cases of LPD were observed in the teplizumab program, but there is a theoretical risk for the occurrence of LPD in the postmarket period.
L1212: Based on published reports in transplant patients, lymphoproliferative disorders occurred frequently within a year of transplantation (Opelz and Henderson 1993). Thus, the duration of follow-up (see Section 8.2.1) for teplizumab was likely sufficient to detect a signal for LPD should it exist and be clinically important. The consultant from DHM1 estimated that the risk of LPD with teplizumab is &lt;1%, so a registry study may not be practical, but instead, recommended addressing this theoretical risk through enhanced pharmacovigilance in the postmarketing period.
L1213: Nonclinical carcinogenicity or genotoxicity studies were not required to support this indication
L1214: given the short duration of treatment, as it was not considered appropriate to assess the long-term effects of treatment.
L1215: ## 8.8.2. Human Reproduction and Pregnancy
L1216: Consultative review was provided by DPMH to assist with review of the human reproduction, pregnancy, and lactation data, as well as the Pregnancy and Lactation subsections of labeling.
L1217: There are no data on teplizumab and pregnancy outcomes in the published literature. A total of 13 pregnancies were reported in 12 teplizumab-treated women in the ISS. Two additional pregnancies were observed in Study 1 and Protégé Extension, which were not included in the ISS. Outcomes for the 15 pregnancies include 11 normal neonates, 2 elective terminations, 1 spontaneous abortion, and 1 unknown lost to follow-up. The last dose of teplizumab in relation to conception was \le 2 months for 4 pregnancies in teplizumab-treated patients, with 3 pregnancies reported without any complications, and one noted to have conception before treatment end, this pregnancy was electively terminated. The one pregnancy with adverse outcome (spontaneous abortion) was complicated by Graves' disease and T1D and occurred \sim 20 months after the last dose of teplizumab, making causality unlikely. None of the cases of abortion (elective or spontaneous) reported congenital malformations. Adherence to a contraceptive regimen was a requisite for participation in all teplizumab clinical studies.
L1218: There are no data on effects of fertility in humans. Fertility and reproductive performance were unaffected in female and male mice that received a murine analog of teplizumab administered by the subcutaneous route at doses up to 7 times the cumulative clinical dose based on body surface area. However, in offspring exposed to teplizumab in utero or via lactation in the pre- and postnatal toxicity study, a reduction in fertility was evident at 20 mg/kg (7 times the cumulative clinical dose based on BSA).
L1219: Two reproductive and developmental toxicity studies were conducted in mice using the surrogate murine antibody. In an embryofetal development study, fetal harm was observed at the highest dose (three doses administered at gestational day 6, 10, and 14) in pregnant dams (equivalent cumulative dose, based on BSA scaling, to 20 times the cumulative human dose). There was an increased incidence of post-implantation loss that included the loss of the entire litter for multiple females. The next lowest dose was deemed the NOAEL, which is 3/10th of the cumulative human dose. No gross abnormalities were noted in the viable pups. In a Prenatal and Postnatal Development study, the surrogate murine antibody was administered on gestational day 6 until lactational day 19. This is up to 12 doses assuming a 21-day gestational period. The highest dose administered (20 mg/kg, 60 mg/m2) was considered to be the NOAEL for the pregnant dam, which is 80 (60 mg/m2 * 12 = 720. 720 (nonclinical)/9 (clinical) = 80x margin) times the cumulative human dose based on BSA. The middle dose (3 mg/kg, 9 mg/m2) was considered to be the NOAEL for the offspring, as reductions in sperm count/motility and reduction in number of pregnancies was seen in the highest dose.
L1220: In summary, nonclinical data during pregnancy report increased resorptions of fetuses in mice exposed to the murine analog to teplizumab. Although there were no major congenital malformations (MCMs) or high rates of spontaneous abortion in pregnancies during the clinical trials, data are too limited to make any conclusions about reproductive safety. Teplizumab is being proposed for a relatively narrow indication, "for the delay and prevention of clinical type I diabetes in at-risk individuals." This is not expected to be a large population but would include females of reproductive potential. DPMH recommends a postmarketing Single-arm Pregnancy Safety Study (SPSS) to assess major congenital malformations, spontaneous abortions, stillbirths, small-for-gestational-age infants, and pregnancy complications such as preeclampsia and gestational diabetes in women exposed to teplizumab during pregnancy. This could be done as a stand-alone postmarketing requirement (PMR) or as part of a larger safety registry PMR.
L1221: Monoclonal antibodies are increasingly transported across the placenta as pregnancy progresses, with the largest amount transferred during the third trimester and may interfere with immune response to infections and live vaccines, such as the rotavirus vaccine. To minimize exposure to a fetus, teplizumab should be administered at least 30 days (6 half-lives) prior to planned pregnancy. Females of reproductive potential should have a pregnancy test prior to treatment with teplizumab.
L1222: Studies of male and female mice pups on postnatal days 11 to 13 indicated exposure to teplizumab; however, because levels were not obtained at birth, the nonclinical reviewer concluded that there is insufficient information to determine if the detection of teplizumab in rat pups was related to in utero or lactational exposure. The DPMH review noted that 'Human IgG is known to be present in human milk. The effects of exposure of teplizumab to the infant's gastrointestinal tract and potential exposure to the infant are unknown. If teplizumab is present in human milk, it is likely that it will only be present in small amounts given the large molecular weight of the product and will likely to be destroyed in the infant's gastrointestinal tract'.
L1223: DPMH recommends the following labeling language for section 8.2: Lactation: There are no data on the presence of teplizumab in human milk, the effect on the breastfed child, or the effects on milk production. Published data from other monoclonal antibodies generally indicate low passage of monoclonal antibodies into human milk and limited systemic exposure in the breastfed child. Although the developmental and health benefits of breastfeeding should be considered along with the mother's clinical need for [TRADENAME] and any potential adverse effects on the breastfed child from [TRADENAME] or from the underlying maternal condition, a lactating woman may consider interrupting breastfeeding and pumping and discarding breast milk during treatment and for 20 days after [TRADENAME] administration in order to minimize drug exposure to a breastfed infant.
L1224: ## 8.8.3. Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth
L1225: Nonclinical chronic toxicity studies have only been done in sexually mature (six to seven weeks PNA) mice.
L1226: Safety in the pediatric population age 8 years and older is discussed extensively in this primary review. We did not find any efficacy or safety issues specific to the pediatric population. An impact on growth was not assessed in the safety program but is not expected for a monoclonal antibody targeting CD3. The agreed Pediatric Study Plan for the relevant IND states that the Applicant will request a deferral for the pediatric 0 to 8-year age group. If approved, to address the Pediatric Research Equity Act (PREA) a post-marketing requirement will be issued for an assessment in patients younger than age 8 years: an open-label, single-arm, 12 month study to evaluate the safety and PK of teplizumab in 15 to 20 pediatric patients 0 to 7 years of age with stage 2 T1D, with two T1D-related autoantibodies and dysglycemia on oral glucose tolerance testing.
L1227: ## 8.8.4. Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal, and Rebound
L1228: Teplizumab will be administered via transfusion by health care personnel; therefore, intentional overdose is not expected. In the rare case of accidental overdose, exaggerated effects of CRS may occur, as previous studies that used doses up to 2 times higher than the proposed dose were with associated exaggerated effects of CRS.
L1229: No studies have been specifically designed to evaluate the potential for teplizumab to cause withdrawal and rebound. The safety data on teplizumab administration available for 1 to 2 courses of 6 to 14 days administered 6 to 12 months apart.
L1230: There are no relevant studies or information regarding a dependence or abuse potential of teplizumab. There was no Controlled Substance Staff (CSS) consult obtained or abuse potential assessed for this application given teplizumab is administered in a single 14-day course by a licensed medical provider in an outpatient healthcare setting, therefore was felt to have a low risk for abuse.
L1231: ## 8.9. Safety in the Postmarket Setting
L1232: ## 8.9.1. Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L1233: Not applicable. No post-marketing data is available for teplizumab as it is not approved in any country at this time.
L1234: ## 8.9.2. Expectations on Safety in the Postmarket Setting
L1235: The pooled safety population included in this Application included pediatric and adult patients
L1236: at risk of T1D age \ge 8 years to young and mid-adulthood and represent a large portion of patients who would qualify for use of teplizumab in the postmarket setting. Subpopulations that are not adequately represented in the safety dataset adults, Black or African American patients, are not expected to have a different safety experience.
L1237: The indication statement will not include patients less than 8 years of age. Although we cannot be certain, we believe that off-label use is unlikely to occur in this population because of insurance reimbursement issues. Nevertheless, our review of the safety data did not identify safety risks that were more prominent or severe in younger patients to warrant any additional regulatory action to prevent off-label use. Another potential population in whom off-label use may be attempted is the stage 1 T1D population (2 or more T1D autoantibodies without evidence of dysglycemia). Although the safety profile would not be expected to differ between stage 1 and stage 2 patients, the benefit/risk assessment may be less favorable for stage 1 patients because they have a longer lead-time before anticipated progression to T1D. Natural history studies show 40% of stage 1 patients develop dysglycemia within 2 years, compared with 60% of stage 2 patients, who develop T1D within 2 years of detection of dysglycemia (Krischer 2013). Again, no additional regulatory approach to prevent off-label use is warranted.
L1238: Additionally, the long-term safety profile of teplizumab is unknown, because patients were followed for one to two years in each of the studies used to support safety, with an average exposure of 133 patient-years for the 44 teplizumab-treated patients in TN-10. The uncertainty with regards to long-term latent safety events like malignancy may be adequately addressed through the proposed 10-year registration study which is a requirement in the postmarketing period should teplizumab be approved.
L1239: Safety issues which could potentially arise in the postmarket setting are related to the ability of the healthcare provider to appropriately identify CRS and/or serious infection. This risk could be appropriately mitigated by clearly specifying symptoms of cytokine release syndrome and recommended treatment in labeling in order ensure that health care providers and patients are informed and take necessary steps to manage the risks.
L1240: The teplizumab clinical trials had broadly similar discontinuation criteria that provided a standardized approach to pausing or discontinuing drug treatment, with provisions to stop treatment for laboratory abnormalities including bilirubin and aminotransferase elevations, or abnormal platelet, neutrophil, hemoglobin, or coagulation parameters, and the occurrence of certain adverse events. If this approach to treatment discontinuation is applied in the postmarket setting, it is estimated that approximately 10% of patients will not be able to tolerate the full course of teplizumab therapy. We do not know if the protocol-specified withdrawal criteria may have prevented more serious or permanent harms from continued dosing. Therefore, similar criteria for treatment discontinuation should be used postmarketing.
L1241: ## 8.9.3. Additional Safety Issues from Other Disciplines
L1242: There are no additional safety issues from other disciplines that warrant discussion.
L1243: ## 8.10. Integrated Assessment of Safety
L1244: The safety of teplizumab was evaluated through a pooled dataset, the integrated summary of safety (ISS) dataset consisting of the TN-10 study, which provides clinical safety for the intended population of patients at-risk of T1D (N=76, 44 treated with teplizumab) plus 4 additional studies completed in patients with new- or recent-onset T1D. Thus, the majority of the exposure was in T1D patients, which is acceptable because the continuous nature of the disease allows applicability of safety findings from the new-onset T1D population to the at-risk population. The FDA performed safety analyses using a pooled dataset comprised of the ISS studies without TN-10 to evaluate for adverse events specific to the population with T1D (modified ISS, or 'mISS'). The submitted safety database was adequate for the proposed indication in the proposed at-risk population.
L1245: The safety profile for teplizumab appears similar in character to, but milder than, that of other T-cell stimulating monoclonal antibodies. Safety issues include cytokine release, infection, immunosuppression/lymphopenia, and rash, although many of these adverse events were self-limited, mild, or transient (limited to the 14-day dosing period).
L1246: There are no serious safety issues that rise to the level of a Boxed Warning or Contraindication. Potential serious risks include cytokine release, infection, and hypersensitivity reactions and should be described in the Warnings and Precautions section of the Prescribing Information.
L1247: ## Cytokine Release Syndrome
L1248: As with other drugs that modulate, or partially activate T-cell function, cytokine production has been observed following teplizumab treatment. Circulating cytokines were increased in a dose-dependent manner following teplizumab administration in nonclinical animal studies. Humanized monoclonal antibodies like teplizumab demonstrate notably less immune cell activation than non-humanized antibodies, and incremental dose increases were incorporated into teplizumab clinical studies as a strategy to reduce immune activation. Despite these strategies, clinically relevant cytokine release was still noted following teplizumab use in the clinical development program.
L1249: CRS events were identified in up to 5% of teplizumab-treated patients using the Applicant's analysis based on CTCAE version 3.0 criteria. DHM1 noted that the methodology for CRS grading used in the development program was somewhat obsolete/different than the current understanding of CRS. When newer criteria were adopted (the ASTCT grading system), a higher number of cases (~16% of teplizumab treated patients) were detected, although 10% of patients in the comparator arm also met criteria for CRS based on the presence of fever.
L1250: In general, the rates of severe CRS appear to be very low, regardless of the grading methodology used. The majority of cases of CRS were mild and self-limited without need for intervention beyond 24 hours (82%) and were responsive to antipyretics or interruption of drug infusion. All cases of CRS resolved without sequelae. There were 7 patients (0.9% of the teplizumab treatment group) who required hospitalization, symptomatic treatment past 24 hours, or both. No events required pressors or ventilator support, and all CRS events resolved without sequelae. The risk of CRS can be mitigated with labeling approaches and no REMS is recommended (see DRM memo).
L1251: Should teplizumab be approved, the label will need to be inclusive of the risks and include risk mitigation strategies that inform providers of common symptoms and timeline for recognition of important symptoms (symptoms may include fever, nausea, fatigue, myalgia, and malaise, and typically occurs during the first 5 doses), with median time to onset of CRS and recommendations to monitor for signs and symptoms as well as treatment if symptoms are detected (anti-pyretics [e.g., NSAIDs] or acetaminophen), antihistamines and/or anti-nausea medications). Importantly, if dyspnea, hypoxia, or hypotension occurs, the labeling should advise providers to consider a temporary pause of dosing for 1 to 2 days after improvement or until resolution of symptoms.
L1252: ## Infections
L1253: In clinical trials, patients treated with teplizumab had a higher rate of serious infections than placebo- or control- treated patients (3.4% vs. 2% for ISS) over the entire observation period, and this difference was more pronounced when analyzing the TN-10 trial alone. The rate of total infections (serious and nonserious) was not different between teplizumab treated patients or patients treated with placebo/control. On analysis of patient narratives of infection SAEs, a temporal relationship with lymphopenia was observed with several infections. It is important to note that symptoms of infection overlap with symptoms of CRS making distinguishing between the two difficult. Although an excess risk of serious infection with teplizumab is not certain based on the available data, temporary immunosuppression from teplizumab lends biologic plausibility. If approved, the Applicant's proposed label should be revised to strengthen the language about serious infections.
L1254: ## Hypersensitivity and Rash
L1255: Anaphylaxis and serum sickness are two major acute infusion reactions that can be caused by monoclonal antibodies and were investigated closely for teplizumab use, as immunogenicity was observed with teplizumab. The Applicant reported that 50-90% of patients developed anti-drug antibodies (ADA) during the first course of teplizumab across the various treatment regimens, and that the ADA incidence was as high as 100% when a second course was administered. An analysis of ADA status association with adverse events related to hypersensitivity (anaphylaxis, rash and serum sickness) revealed that the frequency of rash may
L1256: be higher in patients who are positive for ADA. Although rash was also observed at a 13-fold higher rate in the teplizumab group compared with the placebo group, the majority of rashes (with the exception of two SAEs) were generally non-serious and did not require additional intervention.
L1257: Overall, FDA analysis revealed a low frequency of reported hypersensitivity AEs: seven in the teplizumab group (0.9%) vs. two in the control group (0.8%), but there were some events consistent with anaphylaxis, bronchospasm, and angioedema in teplizumab-treated patients. There was one report of serum sickness in a patient treated with teplizumab in Study TN-10, that occurred in a patient who may have underlying risk (rheumatologic disease).
L1258: ## 'Common' AEs
L1259: The most frequently reported treatment emergent adverse events (TEAEs) were lymphopenia and leukopenia, reported in \ge 75\% of teplizumab-treated patients in the ISS studies during the 14-day treatment period. Both are expected pharmacodynamic effects based on nonclinical studies of teplizumab and clinical experience with teplizumab's non-humanized progenitor, OKT3. Rash was a common AE among teplizumab-treated patients (45%) but was self-limited and all cases resolved without sequelae. Transaminase elevations were also frequently reported AEs and were observed in \sim 25\% of teplizumab-treated patients during the first treatment course and follow-up period. Similar to lymphopenia, transiently elevated transaminases were expected given teplizumab's mechanism and are attributed to the direct effects of cytokine release on the liver. On comprehensive review and outlier analysis of transaminase elevations, there was no signal for drug-induced liver injury with teplizumab use. Other cytopenias (neutropenia, thrombocytopenia, anemia) were also observed frequently, and similar to lymphopenia, recovery was noted within 2 to 4 weeks of treatment. Pruritis, pyrexia, nausea, and headache were all common and reported in \sim 15\% of teplizumab-treated patients during the 14-day treatment period but were also noted at relatively high frequency among control patients (5 to 14%). Other electrolyte abnormalities, including blood bicarbonate decreased, hypocalcemia, and hyponatremia were all reported at higher frequencies in the teplizumab-treated safety population, and no events were considered severe.
L1260: ## Treatment Discontinuations
L1261: Approximately 10% of patients were unable to complete their treatment course with the majority of events related to protocol-driven laboratory drug discontinuation criteria. Laboratory abnormalities leading to drug discontinuation included transaminase elevations, hematologic parameters (lymphopenia, neutropenia, anemia, and thrombocytopenia) and cytokine release syndrome. Transaminase elevations accounted for half of drug discontinuations, occurring in 5.6% of teplizumab patients and 0.9% of control patients. It is unknown if adhering to prespecified drug discontinuation criteria prevented more serious adverse events during the clinical trials; therefore, a conservative approach to mitigate risk
L1262: should teplizumab be approved would be to incorporate a modified version of the discontinuation criteria included in the ISS studies into teplizumab's labeling.
L1263: ## Other Safety Issues
L1264: Certain safety findings identified within the teplizumab development program, e.g., DKA, are not expected to be adverse events observed in the intended patient population. Importantly, no patients in TN-10 presented with DKA at diabetes onset.
L1265: ## Long-Latency Risks: Uncertainties
L1266: During the safety review, potential longer-term risks were identified based on nonclinical findings from teplizumab in chimpanzees and safety events observed with OKT3, teplizumab's non-humanized precursor. These potential risks include lymphoproliferative disorders (LPDs) caused by Epstein-Barr virus (EBV) reactivation and or other malignancy due to immunosuppression. Nonclinical studies in chimpanzees demonstrated mortality related to T-cell immunosuppression and malignancy resulting from infection with EBV-like virus at high doses of teplizumab. A higher frequency of EBV reactivation was observed among teplizumab treated patients (4.5%) compared to control patients (2.3%) and EBV viral load assessments in TN-10 demonstrated mostly asymptomatic, transient EBV viral reactivations among a portion of patients treated with teplizumab. Although EBV-associated LPDs have been observed in drugs that suppress T-cell immune function, LPD incidence is associated with high degrees of immunosuppression (dose and duration dependent) and therefore, have a lower likelihood in a therapy with limited duration, as is being proposed with teplizumab. There were no cases of LPD detected during the teplizumab clinical development program in the ISS population. It is important to note that EBV-associated LPD and malignancy risks are only theoretical at this time as they are more likely to be associated with chronic immunosuppression. One mitigating strategy in the currently proposed label is to avoid teplizumab treatment in patients with active EBV (or CMV) infection, as CMV is a similar virus associated with long-term LPD risk. In order to address uncertainty related to long latency events, a 10-year exposure registry PMR may be considered to evaluate over time if there is an associated risk of malignancy with teplizumab use.
L1267: ## 9. Advisory Committee Meeting and Other External Consultation
L1268: The Endocrine and Metabolic Drugs Advisory Committee (EMDAC) met on May 27, 2021 to discuss the findings from the teplizumab development program, and to specifically address the following questions. Note that only a brief summary is provided here, and the official transcript is publicly available.
L1269: ## Question 1 (Discussion)
L1270: The Applicant is seeking approval of teplizumab to delay T1D mellitus in at-risk individuals. Discuss the strength of the overall evidence presented herein to conclude that effectiveness has been established for teplizumab for the proposed indication.
L1271: ## **Committee discussion:**
L1272: *For this question, the committee members discussed in detail the merits of the single adequate and well-controlled trial, TN-10. The majority agreed that TN-10 provided adequate evidence of efficacy, although this view was not unanimous. Several committee members mentioned they were reassured that the primary efficacy outcome (the delay of T1D in at-risk individuals) held up to multiple analyses by the FDA. Several committee members acknowledged that they believed there was a benefit associated with teplizumab use, but were not confident in the magnitude of the observed effect size of the 24 month delay given the small size of the study with notable baseline imbalances in age and genetics. There were comments about the small study limiting the ability to assess differential efficacy in subgroups, particularly noting the HLA analyses. There was not strong support for the use of the C-peptide meta-analysis as confirmatory evidence of effectiveness. One member stated that her view was because C-peptide is not a validated surrogate, and another stated that C-peptide is not convincing because the Protégé study failed its primary endpoint. They would also have considered measures of glycemic control in patients with stage 3 T1D supportive in this population, as the major effector of complications in T1D is related to glycemic control.*
L1273: ## Question 2 (Discussion)
L1274: Discuss the clinical meaningfulness of the observed median 2-year delay of onset of T1D demonstrated in study TN-10.
L1275: ## **Committee discussion:**
L1276: *The majority of the committee members acknowledged the unmet need and supported the clinical meaningfulness of a 2-year delay, but again expressed concern that the confidence in the estimate of 24 months is low. One member stated that the treatment effect is probably overestimated, while another stated that even less of a delay is still meaningful. This view seemed to be largely influenced by the Open Public Hearing speakers and their testimonials about quality of life. The patient representative also stated that any amount of delay would have been meaningful to her. Some members mentioned that any time*
L1277: *period of delay (even 6 months) appears to be meaningful for at-risk patients and their families. Some committee members mentioned that it is unknown whether this 2-year median delay will make an impact on the prevention of long-term complications of T1D.*
L1278: ## Question 3 (Discussion)
L1279: Discuss your view of the safety issues identified in the clinical development program and the potential for unobserved, longer latency safety issues (e.g., malignancy) given the mechanism of action of teplizumab. Discuss whether these safety concerns can be adequately mitigated through labeling and/or required postmarketing studies.
L1280: ## *Committee discussion:*
L1281: *A broad range of opinions were given about the safety findings. Overall, these ranged from a concern about the safety of the product in light of rates of serious adverse events, and diabetic ketoacidosis, to a concern about inadequate data to assess safety, to a view that the safety database was adequate for approval with postmarketing safety follow-up. There was concern noted that patients in TN-10 were not followed for safety after T1D diagnosis. The majority thought this deficiency could be addressed with a postmarket safety study, although one stated that the lack of data would be difficult to address through labeling. Several committee members acknowledged that uncertainty over long-latency adverse events of interest like malignancy could be adequately mitigated through rigorous post-marketing studies, and were reassured that with the limited exposure of teplizumab (a single 14-day course) the malignancy risk is likely low. Others, however, noted that if the drug 'works' then the immune system is being altered in such a way that could have an impact on safety, although it was unclear how. Additionally, the committee members expressed concern about the imbalance in risk of diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) observed in teplizumab-treated patients the phase 2/3 studies in the new- or recent-onset population with T1D. Although they were reassured that DKA was not observed in TN-10 patients, several members of the committee mentioned that the Applicant and FDA should explore the risk of DKA further through continued study in the at-risk population after diagnosis of T1D, in order to ensure that this population too may not at some point be at excess risk of DKA (vs. untreated patients). In addition, the three deaths in the teplizumab development program were discussed in detail as safety issues worth investigating further, although another committee member commented that given the randomization ratio, the deaths observed in the teplizumab program in the population with T1D did not signal imbalance. The rheumatologist on the committee was overall reassured by the breadth of the safety data, since other immune-modulating immunotherapies in autoimmune*
L1282: *disorders primarily affecting pediatrics often have much smaller safety datasets, and the Applicant's inclusion of safety data for patients with stage 3 T1D, allowed for a robust amount of safety data in the teplizumab development program. Overall, the committee agreed that further postmarketing commitments or controlled studies would help to further characterize these safety issues.*
L1283: ## Question 4 (Discussion)
L1284: The Applicant's proposed indication statement is "Teplizumab is for the delay of type 1 diabetes mellitus (T1D) in at-risk individuals." TN-10 was conducted in individuals ages 8 and older and enrolled relatives of patients with T1D with two or more positive autoantibodies and dysglycemia. Based on available data, discuss how the indicated population should be described to ensure that the expected benefit(s) of teplizumab will outweigh the risks of treatment. If you have any other recommendations for the indication statement, please provide them.
L1285: ## *Committee discussion:*
L1286: *For this question, the majority of committee members agreed that the indication should not be restricted to relatives of patients with T1D, and instead should include both non-relatives and relatives meeting the criteria for stage 2 T1D as used in the TN-10 enrollment criteria (2 or more T1D-specific autoantibodies plus dysglycemia on OGTT). Some committee members also mentioned incorporating HLA DR4 present individuals in the labeling in order to advise patients and families of the most likely patient to benefit from therapy, while others cautioned the committee about restricting use to patients by subgroup analyses, which were not powered to detect treatment effect among groups.*
L1287: ## Question 5 (Voting)
L1288: Does the information provided in the background documents and presentations by the Applicant and FDA, show that the benefits of teplizumab outweigh the risks in support of approval to delay type 1 diabetes mellitus? Vote yes or no. If you voted yes, provide your opinion on the appropriate indication statement, and discuss whether you recommend any post-marketing safety studies. If you voted no, provide your rationale, and provide recommendations for additional data and/or analyses that would support a favorable benefit-risk profile and approval of teplizumab.
L1289: ## *Committee vote and discussion:*
L1290: *Ten members voted yes, and 7 voted no. The committee unanimously acknowledged significant unmet need in patients at risk of developing T1D and acknowledged and credited the moving testimonials from patients and family*
L1291: *members who discussed the clinical meaning of delay of T1D. The primary concern voiced was related to unclear efficacy (e.g. study not replicated, bias from baseline imbalances, uncertainty around the 2-year estimate, confirmatory C-peptide data not convincing). Only a small number of members cited safety concerns as their reason for voting no. Both those who voted yes and those who voted no stated these concerns as well as acknowledged the unmet need but came to different conclusions. Some committee members stated uncertainty, that more safety data, replication of efficacy, and diversity in the trial population would strengthen their certainty of the benefit of teplizumab in light of the risks.*
L1292: ## Network of Experts Panel Meeting
L1293: During the review, the review team sought out experts in the care of children and adults with T1D to better understand provider practice with regard to the identification and monitoring of patients at-risk of developing T1D. As treatment options become available for patients with earlier stages of T1D, it is important to understand how clinicians plan to identify populations who would benefit from therapeutics meant to delay T1D. Specifically, the review team was interested in further understanding the current practice of identification of the stage 2 T1D population, and whether identification of these at-risk individuals through autoantibody screening is considered common clinical practice, and what information in the labeling of products intended to delay the onset of clinical T1D in this population would be useful to providers. Four experts were identified through the FDA Network of Experts (NoE) program who have experience in the care of patients with T1D. All selected experts were screened by the NoE staff for conflict of interest. In a virtual roundtable forum, the following questions were posed to the experts (answers summarized below):
L1294: - Are you aware of the proposed 3 stage staging system for type 1 diabetes, and do you utilize this approach in your practice?All of the experts in the group were aware of the staging system, and expressed familiarity with screening through the research setting, with some citing familiarity with TrialNet as a screening program. For the second part of the question, the consensus was that they currently do not utilize this approach in practice (outside of research) primarily because there are no therapies to offer patients identified as at-risk of T1D.
- How do you identify patients with stage 2 type 1 diabetes? What antibody tests do you typically order for at-risk patients?For this question, there were differing answers among the experts. Some identify patients who present with dysglycemia (for example, steroid-induced hyperglycemia, or stress-induced hyperglycemia), and some identify patients with other autoimmune conditions at risk of T1D who are noted to be antibody positive in the process of evaluation for underlying causes. Additionally, two of the experts identified TrialNet as a
L1295: screening program through which stage 2 patients are identified in their clinical practice. The approach to antibody testing varied according to the setting, with emergency rooms often testing only islet cell antibodies (ICA) or GAD-65, whereas the provider may also test for zinc transporter 8 (ZnT8) and insulin autoantibodies (IAA). One research-based physician consistently ordered a panel of 4 autoantibodies including: insulin, GAD65, islet tyrosine phosphatase 2 (IA-2), and ZnT8.
L1296: 3. At what interval do you repeat autoantibody panels if you identify individuals at risk?

- If one autoantibody is positive, most experts would repeat the autoantibody testing after one year, either by protocol (research) or through clinical monitoring, in order to monitor for risk of T1D.
4. Do you rely on any particular laboratory or notice any difference in performance among the available islet autoantibody tests?

- For this answer, several different laboratories were mentioned. For the ZnT8 autoantibody, one expert stated they use [REDACTED] (b) (4), and another (who uses autoantibody determinations in research) uses the [REDACTED] (b) (4). Otherwise, several providers mentioned they didn't have a preference, or were not aware of any difference in performance for the islet autoantibody tests. One expert also mentioned that at the time of autoantibody determination, often patients are in the hospital, and therefore the institution chooses the laboratory to perform the autoantibody testing. One expert that utilizes IAA for research mentioned that they noted some variability in the commercially available labs [REDACTED] (b) (4) when used in the past, and currently uses the reference lab at his institution for clinical autoantibody determination. Another expert with research experience using autoantibodies, would often perform both standardized research assays and commercial assays (as they tended to return faster), and mentioned that these tests typically agreed with each other.
- For autoantibody testing in research, the [REDACTED] (b) (4) was mentioned by the expert involved in research, stating that some of the cutting-edge autoantibody assays have been developed at the [REDACTED] (b) (4) over the years, as well as the [REDACTED] (b) (4).
- The Immunology of Diabetes Society Program was also mentioned as a source for assay comparisons as they have quantitative data comparing the sensitivity and specificity across different labs, through the testing of standardized samples.
5. What is your approach to follow-up of patients identified with preclinical type 1 diabetes?

- The follow-up approaches detailed by experts all included education on the clinical symptoms of T1D and follow-up in clinic to obtain laboratory (HbA1c) and clinical assessments. Several experts mentioned they prescribed home glucometers.
L1297: - One expert discussed that she instructs patients and their families on how to use a meter and to check blood glucoses twice daily (one fasting and one 2 hours postprandial) for 2 weeks in order to assess for dysglycemia, and following that, she usually recommends checking once weekly and contacting the office if any blood glucoses are &gt;200 mg/dL, with follow-up in 3 months, then every 6 months thereafter.
- One expert mentioned that the majority of patients at his institution are identified as at-risk through the TrialNet Pathway to Prevention Trial, and that most patients with stage 1 or 2 T1D are identified either through this program or another institution-based research program. Therefore, they follow clinical protocol of assessments, monitoring single-autoantibody positive patients once yearly by checking autoantibody assays, for patients with double autoantibodies they perform more formal assessments like oral glucose tolerance testing (OGTT), sometimes fasting blood glucoses, and HbA1c to follow for the development of dysglycemia.
- The experts also mentioned their approach to identification and follow-up of these patients depends on the treatment options available. It was emphasized that providers currently have no available treatments to recommend to patients once they are identified as at-risk of T1D. Other experts mentioned that having a prevention measure would change the approach taken to these patients as it would be worthwhile to monitor more frequently and to see patients in clinic more frequently.
- It was mentioned also that clear guidelines on when to start a disease-modifying treatment would be useful to help determine the frequency of autoantibody testing (and to mitigate over-testing). Some mentioned that they would not increase the frequency of autoantibody testing beyond their current practice, but instead would increase monitoring for dysglycemia in patients identified to have multiple autoantibodies.
6. Do providers read the FDA labels, and find them useful and what are the clinical organizations or clinical practice guidelines that you typically trust and rely on for additional information in clinical practice, outside of an FDA drug label?

- The experts largely stated that they read FDA labels, especially when reviewing side effect profiles and recommendations for monitoring.
- The American Diabetes Association standards of clinical care (Pediatrics chapter, which includes a pre-diabetes for T1D section) currently recommends that family members be screened through a research program (this expert referenced TrialNet). Additionally, one expert mentioned that the ADA guidelines discuss that at this time family members/at-risk individuals aren't recommended to be screened outside of a research setting, in large part, because there are no therapeutics available to offer those identified as "at-risk" as far as an intervention.
- Also, the International Society of Pediatric and Adolescent Diabetes (ISPAD) Guidelines, issued every 3 years, provide guidance on screening for at-risk patients.
L1298: - The American Academy of Pediatrics (AAP) Bright Futures program generally specifies the standard of care for preventative care for children at every age.
- TrialNet, JDRF, Helmsley Trust, and the Milken Institute were also mentioned.
7. What information would you find useful in labeling for therapeutics intended to delay or prevent type 1 diabetes in high-risk individuals?

- Suggestions for labeling included defining the indication patient population well, specifically which antibodies need to be positive (and the titers) and the glycemic indices at the point of initiation. Most expert also mentioned clear monitoring guidance for side-effects including frequency of monitoring and mitigation actions (stop therapy, dose decrease recommendation, or specific treatment). Another expert mentioned it would be useful to identify how often to assess for transition to T1D once a patient has been treated. Additionally, one expert recommended including specific baseline characteristics (genetics, HLA type, antibody types) that would help the provider understand which type of patient would benefit most from therapy (younger, GAD-dominant, etc.).
8. What would you find clinically meaningful in a therapeutic? (direct quotes from all 4 experts)

- "In general, a 2- to 3-year delay actually has real clinical impact for these families."... "As I work with children, I imagine, what if I have a kindergartener that's a new onset? Managing that comes with all sorts of layers of problems. And if you can delay that until they're a second grader, that's a very different childhood, it's a very different family, and a very different support network, as very different support is needed for that child."
- "I think from a clinical perspective, delaying clinical diabetes let's say for a year or more, prevention of DKA at presentation, I think both of those end goals I think would be clinically meaningful and relevant."
- "I think that in the younger kids, a year or two delay I think would be significant. And of course, if we can reduce DKA, even by only 50%, that would be awesome."
- "Delaying the diabetes and living with it for a shorter period of time is always a benefit."... "it's stressful for families, and a year less of living with diabetes is a better year."
L1299: ## Other Topics of Discussion
L1300: One expert mentioned the benefits of screening the general population for risk of T1D given the risk of DKA at onset of T1D can be as high as 60 to 70% depending on the population, and the DKA event at diagnosis is associated with adverse cognitive effects. Researchers at the [REDACTED] (b) (4) have shown that in after single episode of DKA, there is a measurable cognitive deficit. Research is ongoing to identify at what age screening should occur, and many in the T1D autoimmunity research community and general pediatrics community are endorsing general
L1301: population screening efforts. Another expert described that most of the patients that do come in with new onset T1D do not have relatives and thought that screening the general population would be the best tool to prevent DKA, just as lipid screening is currently used in general pediatric screening guidelines. In addition, another expert mentioned that targeting newborn HLA haplotyping or genetic screening by another method, would narrow down the at-risk population for which future autoantibody screening would be indicated. Another expert mentioned that primary care doctors perform extensive screening for other diseases and thought that T1D autoantibody screening would be easy to incorporate into this screening paradigm. Another expert mentioned the importance of raising public awareness in our communities and considering more nationalized campaigns for improving T1D awareness as the rate of DKA has been cut by over 50% as a result of these campaigns.
L1302: ## 10. Labeling Recommendations
L1303: ## 10.1. Prescription Drug Labeling
L1304: Labeling recommendations are discussed throughout this review; final labeling is deferred until the next review cycle.
L1305: ## 10.2. Nonprescription Drug Labeling
L1306: Not applicable.
L1307: ## 11. Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS)
L1308: No REMS is recommended for this BLA.
L1309: Consideration was given to a communication plan only REMS that would provide education for patients and providers on the risks, diagnosis, and management of CRS after treatment with teplizumab, recommended by the DHM1 consult. On June 24, 2021, the review team met with DRM to determine if a REMS was necessary to ensure the benefit of teplizumab outweighed the risk of CRS. The review Division and DRM have determined that based on the data currently available, if approved, a REMS is not necessary to ensure the benefits of teplizumab outweigh its risks. The rationale for this decision was that the severity of the risk was not determined to reach a level requiring a REMS. It is also important to note that based on our safety analysis, the risk of CRS did not rise to the level of a boxed warning.
L1310: DRM and DDLO discussed potential FDA-driven communications close to the time of approval to highlight the novel treatment for patients at risk of T1D and to convey safety information.
L1311: ## 12. Postmarketing Requirements and Commitments
L1312: This section discusses potential (Food and Drug Administration Amendments Act of 2007) FDAAA-related PMRs and PMCs. The Pediatric Research Equity Act (PREA) is discussed elsewhere in this review.
L1313: During the review we identified the following safety issues that may require a PMR safety study: CRS, infection, and longer latency events like LPD or other malignancy. The Applicant was notified of these potential PMR issues during the mid-cycle communication meeting. The Applicant also proposes to assess the risk of DKA in the postmarket setting. Final determination of postmarketing requirements and commitments are deferred until the next review cycle.
L1314: DPMH is also recommending a pregnancy study be performed in order to collect data for women and their offspring who are exposed to teplizumab during pregnancy in order to assess risk of pregnancy and maternal complications, adverse effects on the developing fetus and neonate, and adverse effects on the infant.

L1410: **Table 36. CTCAE v.3.0 Grading System for Cytokine Release Syndrome**
L1411: Cytokine release syndrome/acute infusion reaction	Cytokine release syndrome	Mild reaction; infusion interruption not indicated; intervention not indicated	Requires therapy or infusion interruption but responds promptly to symptomatic treatment (e.g., antihistamines, NSAIDS, narcotics, IV fluids); prophylactic medications indicated for \le24 hrs	Prolonged (i.e., not rapidly responsive to symptomatic medication and/or brief interruption of infusion); recurrence of symptoms following initial improvement; hospitalization indicated for other clinical sequelae (e.g., renal impairment, pulmonary infiltrates)	Life-threatening; pressor or ventilatory support indicated	Death
REMARK: Cytokine release syndromes/acute infusion reactions are different from Allergic/hypersensitive reactions, although some of the manifestations are common to both AEs. An acute infusion reaction may occur with an agent that causes cytokine release (e.g., monoclonal antibodies or other biological agents). Signs and symptoms usually develop during or shortly after drug infusion and generally resolve completely within 24 hrs of completion of infusion. Signs/symptoms may include: Allergic reaction/hypersensitivity (including drug fever); Arthralgia (joint pain); Bronchospasm; Cough; Dizziness; Dyspnea (shortness of breath); Fatigue (asthenia, lethargy, malaise); Headache; Hypertension; Hypotension; Myalgia (muscle pain); Nausea; Pruritis/itching; Rash/desquamation; Rigors/chills; Sweating (diaphoresis); Tachycardia; Tumor pain (onset or exacerbation of tumor pain due to treatment); Urticaria (hives, welts, wheals); Vomiting.
ALSO CONSIDER: Allergic reaction/hypersensitivity (including drug fever); Bronchospasm, wheezing; Dyspnea (shortness of breath); Hypertension; Hypotension; Hypoxia; Prolonged QTc interval; Supraventricular and nodal arrhythmia – Select; Ventricular arrhythmia – Select.
L1412: CTCAE v3.0
Source: Cancer Therapy Evaluation Program, Common Terminology Criteria for Adverse Events, Version 3.0
Abbreviations: CTCAE, National Cancer Institute Common Terminology Criteria for Adverse Events

L1428: Figure 1 displays two line graphs comparing the median Ln(ADA titers) over time (Days) for subjects randomized to AGC (left panel) and Lilly (right panel). The Y-axis is labeled 'median Ln(ADA titers)' and the X-axis is labeled 'Time (Days)'. Both graphs show a rapid increase in titer from Day 1 to Day 12, followed by a plateau or slight decrease. The AGC group shows a higher median Ln(ADA titer) overall, peaking around Day 28 at approximately 6.0, and remaining high at Day 56. The Lilly group peaks around Day 28 at approximately 4.8 and decreases slightly by Day 56.
L1429: Figure 1 is a diagram illustrating the Type 1 Diabetes Disease Model, showing the progression over time and the loss of Beta Cell Mass.
The vertical axis represents BETA CELL MASS, and the horizontal axis represents Time. The diagram is divided into three stages:
Stage 1: Characterized by Genetic Risk and the presence of 1 Antibody. The Beta Cell Mass is high.
Stage 2: Characterized by the presence of \ge 2 Antibodies and Dysglycemia. The Beta Cell Mass is decreasing.
Stage 3: Characterized by Diagnosis of Type 1 Diabetes. The Beta Cell Mass is critically low.
The progression from Stage 1 to Stage 3 is marked by a downward arrow indicating the loss of Beta Cell Mass over time.
L1430: Figure 2 is a graph illustrating the rate of C-peptide decline before and after the diagnosis (Dx) of Type 1 Diabetes (T1D) in the At-Risk TrialNet TN-01 Cohort, followed longitudinally. The Y-axis represents C-peptide (scale: \log[\text{Cp}+1]), ranging from 0.0 to 2.5. The X-axis represents Months from Diagnosis, ranging from -24 to 12. The graph is divided into four age groups: >20 y, 17-20 y, 11-17 y, and 6-11 y. The data shows that C-peptide levels are stable or slightly declining before diagnosis (Months -24 to -6). At diagnosis (Month 0), there is a sharp decline in C-peptide levels across all age groups. After diagnosis (Months 0 to 12), C-peptide levels continue to decline, with the rate of decline being more pronounced in younger age groups (6-11 y and 11-17 y) compared to older age groups (>20 y and 17-20 y). The graph also indicates the number of subjects (28 No. Subjects) and the number of oral glucose tolerance tests (OGTTs) performed (28 No. OGTTs) at various time points.

L1432: Diagram illustrating the TN-10 Trial Design timeline:
Treatment Period (14 days): Subjects are randomized to receive either Full 14-day IV teplizumab or Placebo.
Follow-up Period (Event-driven, until T1D diagnosis in ≥40 patients): This period begins immediately after the 14-day treatment period.
Primary Endpoint: Time to T1D diagnosis.
Monitoring: Oral glucose tolerance testing is performed at 3 months, 6 months, then every 6 months (at 12, 18, 24, 30, 36, and 42 months).

Stratum 1: 8-17 years, Confirmed Abnormal (Square markers)
Stratum 2: 8-17 years, Unconfirmed Abnormal (Circle markers)
Stratum 3: 18+, Confirmed Abnormal (Triangle markers)

L1435: Figure 7 displays two Kaplan-Meier (K-M) survival curves comparing Placebo (red line) and Teplizumab (blue line) treatment groups. Both curves show the probability of remaining T1D free over time (months). Curve (a) shows Time to first of two consecutive positive OGTTs (months), and Curve (b) shows Time to diabetes diagnosis (months).
